#10940. Best Static Website Hosting Providers in 2027: Free and Paid Options Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You've finished building your website. The HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files are ready, everything works locally, and now you need somewhere to publish it.

Static website hosting is one of the simplest ways to get a website online. Many providers offer free plans, and you don't need to maintain a traditional web server.

The harder part is choosing the right provider. Some let you upload a single HTML file and share it immediately. Others connect to GitHub, automatically deploy code changes, and support entire development teams.

We've compared eight static hosting providers, including TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel, and GitHub Pages. Below, you'll find their free hosting limits, pricing, advantages, and drawbacks, along with recommendations for different types of projects.

Static website hosting is a service that stores and delivers pre-built website files, including HTML, CSS, JavaScript, images, and fonts.

Unlike traditional server-rendered websites, static sites don't need a server to generate each page when someone visits. The HTML is already prepared and can be delivered directly to the visitor's browser.

That doesn't mean static websites can't be interactive. JavaScript can still handle navigation, forms, filtering, and other features. Static sites can also communicate with external APIs or databases.

The main distinction is how pages are generated.

You can write a static website using plain HTML or generate one with frameworks and tools such as Astro, Hugo, Eleventy, or Next.js static export.

Static hosting is also commonly associated with Jamstack, an approach that separates the front end from back-end services.

Lower hosting costs: Many providers let you publish smaller websites for free.

Simpler maintenance: There's no application server or server-side runtime to maintain for the static pages themselves.

Efficient content delivery: Static files can be cached across a content delivery network (CDN), reducing the distance between visitors and website assets.

There are limitations, too. If your website requires user authentication, database operations, or server-side functionality, you'll need additional services or a hosting platform that supports them.

We compared eight providers based on their deployment methods, free plan allowances, pricing, and suitability for different projects.

Free hosting limits: Bandwidth, storage, build allowances, and restrictions.

Deployment process: Whether you can upload files directly or need a Git repository.

Custom domains and HTTPS: Support for publishing under your own domain.

Development features: Automatic deployments, preview environments, and integrations.

Pricing: What happens when a project exceeds its free allowance.

Usage restrictions: Whether free hosting is suitable for commercial projects.

The following table summarizes the main differences between all eight services.

Free plans aren't directly comparable. For example, Vercel restricts its Hobby plan to personal, non-commercial use, while GitHub Pages prohibits using the service as free hosting for an online business or commercial SaaS.

Bandwidth limits also aren't the same as visitor limits. A website serving large images or downloadable files can consume its monthly allowance with relatively few visitors.

Cloudflare Pages is a strong choice for developers who want to host static websites without worrying about a conventional monthly bandwidth cap.

You can connect a GitHub or GitLab repository, and Cloudflare automatically builds and deploys the website when you push changes. Direct uploads are also supported.

A maximum individual file size of 25 MiB.

The combination is useful for blogs, documentation websites, and other projects that primarily serve HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and images.

Cloudflare also provides preview deployments, custom domains, and HTTPS support.

No fixed bandwidth cap for static asset delivery.

Generous free plan for typical static websites.

Monthly build limits can matter for frequently updated projects.

Cloudflare's product range can make the initial setup less straightforward for beginners.

Cloudflare is increasingly emphasizing Workers Static Assets for new projects. Pages remains available, but developers starting a new application should compare both deployment approaches.

For a conventional static website, however, Pages is still a practical option, particularly when traffic could grow beyond the limits of other free plans.

See the official Cloudflare Pages limits for the current allowances.

Netlify combines static website hosting with tools designed to make development and deployment easier.

Connect a Git repository, configure your build settings, and Netlify can publish a new version whenever you push changes.

It also creates deploy previews, allowing you to inspect changes before merging them into your production branch.

This is useful when multiple people work on the same website or when you want to review design changes before publishing.

Netlify supports custom domains, HTTPS, serverless functions, and form handling.

Netlify's free plan uses a credit-based model with 300 credits per month.

Production deployments consume 15 credits each.

Web requests consume 2 credits per 10,000 requests.

Deploy previews and branch deployments consume no deployment credits.

The paid Personal plan starts at $9 per month and includes 1,000 monthly credits.

Convenient Git integration and deploy previews.

Built-in features such as forms and serverless functions.

Suitable for both simple websites and more advanced front-end projects.

Credit-based pricing requires monitoring several types of usage.

A busy site with frequent deployments can consume its free allowance faster than expected.

When comparing Netlify alternatives, Cloudflare Pages is particularly worth considering if your main concern is bandwidth.

For developers choosing between Vercel vs Netlify, the deciding factor is often the framework and workflow. Netlify offers a flexible approach to static site deployment, while Vercel has particularly close integration with Next.js.

Vercel is particularly well suited to Next.js applications, although it also supports React, Vue, Svelte, Astro, and other frameworks.

Like Netlify, Vercel connects to your Git repository and deploys updates automatically.

Every deployment receives its own URL, which helps developers review changes before publishing them to the main website.

Vercel also supports server-side rendering, functions, and other features beyond traditional static site hosting.

There is one important restriction: Vercel Hobby is intended for personal, non-commercial use only.

If you're deploying a business website or another commercial project, you'll need an appropriate paid plan.

Convenient deployment workflow for Next.js.

Supports both static and server-rendered applications.

Paid usage can involve multiple resource-based charges.

If you're building a personal Next.js application, Vercel is an obvious candidate. For a basic HTML website, however, its additional framework features may be unnecessary.

GitHub Pages is a straightforward way to publish a website directly from a GitHub repository.

It works particularly well for developer portfolios, project documentation, open-source websites, and small informational sites.

You can upload HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files to a repository, configure GitHub Pages, and publish them under a github.io subdomain.

GitHub Pages can serve existing static files or build websites using supported workflows, including Jekyll and GitHub Actions.

Published websites have a maximum size of 1 GB.

Bandwidth has a soft limit of 100 GB per month.

Free accounts can publish Pages sites from public repositories.

The bandwidth and build restrictions are soft limits, meaning they aren't necessarily enforced as strict cutoffs.

Simple integration with GitHub repositories.

Well suited to documentation and open-source projects.

Free accounts require a public repository for Pages.

GitHub Pages is not intended for running online businesses, e-commerce websites, or commercial SaaS products.

GitHub Pages is particularly convenient if your code already lives on GitHub and you only need to publish documentation or a portfolio.

For commercial websites, Cloudflare Pages or Netlify may be more appropriate.

The GitHub Pages documentation explains its hosting limits and usage restrictions.

Not every website needs a repository, deployment pipeline, or command-line interface.

If you've created a simple HTML page and want to share it, TiinyHost lets you upload an HTML file or ZIP archive and publish it under a shareable URL.

It can also host PDFs, making it useful for sharing documents and presentations alongside website projects.

The main advantage is the upload process. You don't need to configure a repository or install development tools before publishing.

Sharing an HTML file with someone who doesn't use Git.

The free plan includes one active site, a 3 MB project upload limit, and an allowance of 5,000 monthly visits.

The Tiny plan costs €5 per month when billed monthly. It supports uploads up to 25 MB and includes built-in analytics.

The Solo plan starts at €13 per month when billed monthly and adds features such as custom domains, password protection, and additional projects.

Annual billing is available at lower effective monthly prices.

No Git repository or command line required.

Suitable for previews and smaller projects.

The free plan's 3 MB limit is restrictive for websites with many images or other assets.

Custom domains require the Solo plan or higher.

Upload-based publishing is less convenient than Git automation for frequently updated websites.

For someone who simply needs free HTML hosting to share a small project, TiinyHost is worth considering.

For a larger production website that receives regular updates, Git-based hosting is likely to be more manageable.

Firebase Hosting is Google's hosting service for static websites and web application assets.

It integrates with other Firebase products, including Authentication, Cloud Firestore, and Cloud Functions.

That makes it useful when a website needs static hosting but also relies on Google's back-end services.

Deployment is handled through the Firebase command-line interface.

After configuring your project, you can deploy using:

Firebase distributes website assets through its CDN and supports custom domains with SSL certificates.

The Blaze plan uses pay-as-you-go billing while retaining applicable no-cost allowances.

One detail worth checking is the distinction between Firebase Hosting and Firebase App Hosting. They are separate products with different features and pricing models.

Integrates with Firebase Authentication and Firestore.

Useful for applications already using Google's developer services.

Initial setup is more involved than uploading an HTML file.

The daily transfer allowance may become restrictive for larger websites.

Firebase Hosting makes the most sense when Firebase is already part of your application.

For a standalone static blog or portfolio that doesn't use Firebase services, Cloudflare Pages or GitHub Pages may be simpler.

Amazon S3 and CloudFront offer a different approach to static hosting.

Instead of using a platform that handles the entire deployment process, you combine AWS services to store and deliver your website.

S3 stores the website files, while CloudFront distributes them through a CDN.

This setup provides control over caching, permissions, security, and integration with other AWS services.

It can be suitable for organizations already using AWS or developers who need specific infrastructure configurations.

AWS uses usage-based pricing for S3 storage, requests, and applicable CloudFront usage.

Free allowances may apply depending on your account and the current AWS pricing program, but you should calculate costs based on your expected traffic, storage, and requests.

Unlike simpler hosting services, there isn't one fixed monthly price for every website.

Several services can contribute to the final bill.

Incorrect permissions or origin settings can cause access errors.

One common issue when setting up S3 static website hosting is receiving a 403 Forbidden error because of bucket permissions or CloudFront origin configuration.

AWS recommends using CloudFront Origin Access Control with a regular S3 bucket origin so that the bucket can remain private. This differs from exposing the S3 website endpoint directly, which does not support HTTPS on its own.

AWS S3 and CloudFront are worth considering if you need that level of control. For a small static website, a managed hosting platform usually requires less setup.

Render is a cloud platform that supports static websites alongside web services, databases, background workers, and other application components.

Its static hosting works through a Git repository.

Connect your repository, select the build command and publish directory, and Render automatically deploys your website.

The service includes CDN delivery, custom domains, and managed TLS certificates.

Render is particularly useful when your project includes both a static front end and back-end services that you want to manage through the same platform.

Render's Hobby workspace is free and includes:

500 standard build pipeline minutes per month.

Additional bandwidth is billed at $0.15 per GB under the current pricing structure, so the Hobby workspace should not be treated as unlimited free hosting.

The Pro workspace costs $25 per month, excluding any applicable usage-based compute charges.

Static websites and back-end services on one platform.

The 5 GB included bandwidth allowance is relatively small.

Render makes sense when you're already using it for other parts of an application.

If you only need to host a static HTML website, Cloudflare Pages offers a more generous bandwidth allowance without the additional platform complexity.

Free static website hosting can be sufficient for a personal portfolio, documentation site, prototype, or small informational website.

However, the restrictions vary considerably between providers.

Some hosts measure data transfer, while others use visitor counts or a credit system. Check how each provider calculates usage rather than comparing headline numbers alone.

If your website rebuilds whenever you publish content, deployment limits matter. A blog updated a few times a month has different requirements from an application receiving dozens of deployments every day.

Free hosting doesn't always mean you can run a business website. Vercel's Hobby plan and GitHub Pages have restrictions that may rule out certain commercial projects.

Some services include custom domains on free plans. Others require an upgrade before you can connect one.

A free plan might stop serving additional usage, while another provider may bill for overages.

Before publishing a production website, check the provider's billing behavior and configure spending controls where available.

The right service depends primarily on how you build your website and what you intend to do with it.

For most developers publishing a conventional static website, Cloudflare Pages is a reasonable starting point.

If you're working with Git and want convenient previews and deployment tools, Netlify is another good choice.

For small files, temporary demos, and client previews, a simpler upload-based service may save time.

You should also consider how often the website will change.

Uploading files manually may be fine for a page that rarely changes. For a website with regular content updates, automated deployments from Git will generally be easier to maintain.

Publishing your website is only the first step. If you want search engines to discover and understand your pages, check the technical SEO basics before sharing the URL.

Every important page should have a descriptive HTML title and a relevant meta description.

You can use the Meta Tags Generator to create the necessary markup.

Open Graph tags help control how a page appears when shared on platforms that support them.

Set the title, description, canonical page URL where appropriate, and preview image.

A robots.txt file tells compliant crawlers which paths they may access. It doesn't guarantee indexing or prevent a public page from appearing in search results.

Use the Robots.txt Generator to create your crawler rules.

Include a sitemap with the canonical URLs you want search engines to discover.

Structured data helps search engines interpret eligible content.

Depending on the website, you may want Organization, Article, BreadcrumbList, or other relevant schema types.

The JSON-LD Schema Generator can help generate markup.

Confirm the correct domain loads over HTTPS.

Check canonical tags and redirect behavior.

Make sure important pages don't contain unintended noindex directives.

Test page performance and Core Web Vitals.

Verify the website in Google Search Console.

Static hosting makes serving website files relatively straightforward, but technical SEO still requires configuration and testing.

Cloudflare Pages is a good general-purpose choice because of its free static asset bandwidth and Git integration. Netlify is well suited to teams that need deploy previews, while GitHub Pages works well for portfolios and documentation. The best option depends on your deployment workflow, traffic requirements, and whether the website is commercial.

Yes. TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Render, and several other services offer free hosting options. Most free plans have restrictions involving bandwidth, deployments, storage, or usage. Check the terms before using a free plan for a business website.

A small static website can cost nothing to host if it stays within a provider's free allowances. Paid plans vary from fixed monthly subscriptions to usage-based pricing. Domain registration, paid back-end services, and additional storage or bandwidth may increase the total cost.

For a standalone HTML file, upload-based hosting is generally the simplest approach. Services such as TiinyHost let you publish a file without configuring Git or a build process. If the project already lives in a GitHub repository, GitHub Pages or Cloudflare Pages may be more convenient.

GitHub Pages is a sensible option for documentation, open-source projects, and personal portfolios. Netlify offers more deployment features, including preview environments, forms, and functions. It also supports commercial projects, making it more appropriate for some business websites that would fall outside GitHub Pages' permitted uses.

Static website hosting doesn't need to be expensive or complicated. The important part is choosing a service that matches your website and deployment process.

Cloudflare Pages is a good starting point for static websites that need generous free bandwidth. Netlify is useful for Git-based development, while GitHub Pages suits personal projects and documentation.

If you're publishing a small HTML file or sharing a prototype, upload-based hosting may be all you need.

#10938. Family YouTuber deletes nearly 600 videos after daughter grows uncomfortable with childhood footage

A Japanese family YouTuber has deleted nearly 600 videos from her channel after her eldest daughter grew uncomfortable with people she had just met being able to watch her childhood online.

The family paused its YouTube and social media activity in December 2025 after Okan confirmed that her eldest daughter had been smoking while underage. When Okan announced a return in May, she said her daughter had decided to step back from the videos, though viewers might still hear her voice in some footage.

The channel then went nearly six months without another upload.

By early August, the channel’s public video count had fallen from about 590 to 175. By early November, only the May update remained, according to Japanese YouTube news outlet Yutura.

In her November 17 update, Okan said her eldest daughter had grown embarrassed by the idea of people she had just met seeing videos from when she was little. Okan also cited stricter YouTube policies as a factor in the decision. “We deleted the videos out of respect for my daughter’s feelings,” she said, according to Yutura’s account of the video.

Okan now expects her eldest daughter to stop appearing on social media altogether. She and Yota have also decided when they’ll stop showing their younger daughter’s face, though Okan kept that date private. She plans to keep making everyday vlogs after that point.

#10935. Principal makes chess mandatory for every sixth grader in hopes it’ll improve their failing grades

A New Jersey principal has made chess lessons mandatory for every sixth grader at his school in hopes of improving students’ test scores and critical thinking skills.

Richard L. Wilson Jr., principal of Benjamin Banneker Preparatory Charter School, has introduced compulsory chess lessons twice a week across the school’s campuses in Westampton and Willingboro.

According to The Philadelphia Inquirer, students must learn how to play chess by the end of the school year, with Wilson believing the game can help improve academic performance.

“I just want them to be excited about playing chess,” Wilson said. “Anything to get them enthused.”

The decision comes as the school looks to improve its academic performance following disappointing standardized test results.

During the 2024-25 school year, just 16.4% of students demonstrated proficiency in math, while 37.5% met the required standard in language arts. Both figures were below New Jersey’s statewide averages.

Wilson believes chess can help students develop problem-solving abilities, patience, discipline, and strategic thinking that could translate into better classroom performance.

The program initially began as an optional activity during recess, but proved so popular that students were voluntarily skipping their breaks to play.

It has since expanded into a mandatory part of the sixth-grade curriculum, with the school’s official website highlighting chess as a way to develop decision-making and perseverance.

Students receive lessons covering everything from chess etiquette and the movement of pieces to more advanced strategies.

There are no grades for the lessons, although teachers monitor students’ progress. Wilson also regularly challenges students to games, with anyone who beats the principal having their name announced over the school’s PA system.

New Steam game turns chess into Dark Souls with brutal bosses

Chess rule designed to increase female participation forces all-women team to recruit a man

Parents Are Paying Fortnite Tutors To Help Their Kids Get Better and Hopefully Go Pro

The school isn’t alone in experimenting with games as teaching tools.

Earlier in 2026, a teacher went viral for bringing a PS5 to class to teach history using Assassin’s Creed, while Valorant has also been incorporated into school lessons focused on teamwork and strategy.

However, research into whether chess improves academic results remains mixed. A 2018 study involving more than 4,000 children in England found no evidence that chess instruction improved math, reading, or science test scores.

For now, Wilson is hoping the program will encourage students to enjoy learning while improving their skills.

Students can also compete in an optional chess tournament at the end of the school year, where the winner will receive a trophy and a $300 cash prize.

#10934. AI uncovers hidden Ozempic side effects across 400,000 Reddit posts

Artificial intelligence is giving researchers a new way to listen to what patients are saying about popular GLP-1 drugs. After analyzing more than 400,000 Reddit posts, a University of Pennsylvania team identified several symptoms reported by people using semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy, and Rybelsus) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro and Zepbound) that may not be fully represented in clinical trials or official regulatory information.

The study, published recently in Nature Health, examined more than five years of posts from nearly 70,000 Reddit users. Two categories stood out as particularly deserving of further investigation: reproductive symptoms, including changes in menstrual cycles, and problems involving body temperature, such as chills and hot flashes.

The findings do not establish that the medications caused these symptoms. Instead, researchers say the massive collection of spontaneous patient reports may reveal signals worth examining more closely.

"Some of the side effects we found, like nausea, are well known, and that shows that the method is picking up a real signal," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS) at Penn Engineering and the study's senior author. "The underreported symptoms are leads that came from patients themselves, unprompted, and clinicians could potentially pay attention to them."

What Patients Report Outside Clinical Trials

Clinical trials are designed to determine whether treatments work and to identify important safety problems, but they cannot necessarily capture every symptom that matters to patients once a medication is being used by a much larger population.

"Clinical trials generally identify the most dangerous side effects of drugs," adds Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author on the study. "But they can fail to find what symptoms patients are most concerned about; even though social media is not necessarily representative, a large collection of posts may reflect additional concerns."

The distinction is important. The study found associations in what people discussed online, not proof that GLP-1 drugs were responsible for those experiences.

"We can't say that GLP-1s are actually causing these symptoms," notes Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS advised by Guntuku and Ungar. "But nearly 4% of the Reddit users in our sample reported menstrual irregularities, which would be even higher in a female-only sample. We think that's a signal worth investigating."

Using Social Media as an Early Health Signal

The idea of mining online conversations for clues about drug safety predates today's AI boom. In 2011, Ungar participated in one of the earliest efforts to use material created by internet users to identify possible adverse effects from medications.

Social media can capture experiences that patients discuss with one another but may never formally report to a doctor, drug manufacturer, or regulator.

"Online patient communities work a lot like a neighborhood grapevine," says Ungar. "People who are living with these medications are swapping notes with each other in real time, sharing experiences that rarely make it into a doctor's office visit or an official report."

Since then, online patient communities have expanded enormously. That has made social media a potentially valuable source for studying how medications affect people in everyday life, although gaining access to platform data has become more difficult.

Traditional clinical research remains essential, the researchers emphasize, but online conversations can provide information far more quickly when millions of people begin using a drug.

"Clinical trials are the gold standard, but by design, they are slow," says Guntuku. "This is not a replacement for trials, but it can move much faster, and that speed matters when a drug goes from niche to mainstream almost overnight."

AI Makes Massive Social Media Analysis Possible

One of the biggest obstacles has always been scale.

Guntuku describes the approach as "computational social listening," which uses computational methods to identify patterns in large collections of online conversations about health.

Patients, however, rarely describe symptoms using standardized medical terminology. One person might describe feeling unusually cold, another might mention constant chills, while a clinician could categorize those experiences using a specific medical term.

Researchers therefore need a way to translate everyday language into standardized categories. One important reference is the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), which provides terminology widely used to classify medical conditions, symptoms, and adverse events.

Previously, connecting huge numbers of informal social media posts with standardized medical terminology required enormous amounts of work, limiting how much data researchers could realistically analyze.

Large language models such as GPT and Gemini are changing that equation by allowing researchers to process and categorize vast amounts of text more consistently and quickly.

"Large language models have made it possible to do this kind of analysis much faster with a level of standardization that could be difficult to achieve before," says Sehgal.

Unexpected Symptoms Emerge From 400,000 Posts

The researchers stress that Reddit users do not represent the overall population of people taking GLP-1 drugs. Reddit users tend to be younger, are more likely to be male, and are disproportionately located in the United States.

Even with that limitation, the analysis produced a reassuring sign that the approach was detecting genuine patterns. Many of the symptoms discussed by Reddit users closely matched already known effects of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

About 44% of users included in the study described at least one side effect. Gastrointestinal problems were the most common, consistent with the nausea and other digestive issues already associated with these medications.

More intriguing were symptoms that appeared frequently enough to attract the researchers' attention but may not be as well represented in current drug labels or conventional adverse event reports.

Nearly 4% of users who reported side effects described reproductive symptoms. These included changes in menstruation such as bleeding between periods, heavy bleeding, and irregular menstrual cycles.

Users also described changes involving body temperature, including chills, feeling unusually cold, hot flashes, and symptoms resembling a fever.

Fatigue was another notable finding. It was the second most frequently reported complaint in the Reddit data, even though relatively few clinical trials reported fatigue often enough for it to reach established reporting thresholds.

Why Menstrual and Temperature Changes Are Interesting

One possible reason these reports caught researchers' attention involves the hypothalamus, a small but extremely important region of the brain. Among its many jobs, the hypothalamus helps regulate hunger, hormones, reproduction and body temperature.

"These drugs are thought to work by engaging part of the brain called the hypothalamus, which helps regulate a wide variety of hormones," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study. "That doesn't mean the medications are necessarily causing these symptoms, but it could suggest that reports of menstrual changes and body temperature fluctuations are worth studying more systematically."

The researchers are not proposing that this biological connection proves GLP-1 drugs are responsible. Instead, it offers another reason to test the patient-reported patterns more carefully through controlled research.

Turning Online Conversations Into Research Leads

For now, the team hopes the results encourage scientists and clinicians to pay closer attention to symptoms that patients repeatedly discuss online.

"They're clearly on patients' minds, and that's worth paying attention to," says Sehgal.

The researchers also want to broaden their analysis beyond Reddit and beyond English-language communities. Doing so could help determine whether the same patterns emerge among different groups of people and on different social media platforms.

"We don't really know yet whether what we're seeing on Reddit reflects the experience of GLP-1 users globally, or whether it's particular to the kind of person who posts on Reddit in the United States," Ungar says.

In the longer term, rapid AI analysis of online patient conversations could potentially become an early detection system for emerging health concerns involving drugs, supplements and wellness products.

That could be particularly valuable for substances that become popular online faster than conventional research can keep up. Loosely regulated or unregulated products, including injectable peptides, can spread quickly through communities on Reddit, TikTok and other platforms. Discussions among users may therefore provide some of the earliest indications of unexpected effects.

"The whole point of this kind of approach is that it can move quickly, and that's exactly when it's most valuable," says Guntuku.

This study was conducted at the University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. The authors report no outside funding. Tronieri reports receiving an investigator-initiated grant, on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, from Novo Nordisk and receiving consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. The other authors report no conflicts of interest.

#10930. GTA RP streamer Ripoozi in induced coma after car crash over TwitchCon weekend

GTA roleplay streamer Ripoozi was placed in an induced coma after a car crash over TwitchCon weekend, fellow creator Deansocool said on Twitch.

Ripoozi, also known as Oozi, has about 64,000 Twitch followers and plays Larry Knox on NoPixel, a Grand Theft Auto V roleplay server. His character is part of BNE, an in-game crew that also includes streamer Cinna’s character, Kalita Dub.

The crash happened over the weekend of TwitchCon San Diego, which ran from November 13 to 15 at the San Diego Convention Center. Deansocool said members of the group were heading home in two cars when another driver struck one of the vehicles. He described the other driver as drunk.

Deansocool described the other motorist as drunk and said Ripoozi was still in the hospital when he spoke about the crash. Other people in the group were also hospitalized, he said, describing injuries that included a broken foot and a kidney laceration.

The streamer thanked xQc, Cinna, and Buddha for helping fly Ripoozi’s mother out to see him. All three have ties to NoPixel. xQc co-owns the server, while Buddha took over its day-to-day management in 2023.

The clip spread through the GTA roleplay and LivestreamFail subreddits, where fans wished Ripoozi and the others injured in the crash a quick recovery. Some addressed him by his NoPixel character’s name, with one viewer writing, “Stay strong Larry.”

#10929. The shape behind the Einstein problem just revealed strange new physics

A mathematical shape that drew global attention for solving a decades-old puzzle is now revealing an unexpected connection to physics. Researchers have found that structures based on the shape can make light form unusual chiral patterns, pointing to new ways of exploring how geometry can influence optical behavior.

In a study published in Nature Communications, researchers from the Institute of Industrial Science, The University of Tokyo, and collaborating institutions built optical structures inspired by the "Smith hat." This unusual shape is known for solving the so-called Einstein problem in mathematics. When the team illuminated the structures with laser light, they observed diffraction effects unlike those seen in conventional quasicrystals.

The Shape That Solved the Einstein Problem

The Einstein problem asks whether a single tile shape, known as a "monotile," can cover an entire surface without creating a repeating pattern.

Familiar tilings such as checkerboards and honeycombs repeat in a regular way. An aperiodic monotile, by contrast, can fill a surface without ever settling into a repeating arrangement.

In 2023, researchers discovered the first such monotile, the Smith hat. The finding attracted widespread attention because it provided a long-sought solution to the mathematical problem.

"What is especially fascinating about the hat tile is that, although the resulting pattern appears irregular at first glance, it is actually constructed from the honeycomb lattice," says lead author Yuto Moritake. "We wanted to see whether this unique shape could also produce any unexpected physical phenomena."

Turning a Mathematical Pattern Into an Optical Structure

To test that possibility, the researchers created nanoscale versions of the pattern on silicon nitride films using electron beam lithography.

When laser light was directed at the structures, the resulting diffraction patterns formed distinctive pinwheel-like shapes. These patterns directly revealed the chiral character of the aperiodic structure.

Chirality refers to a form of handedness in which a structure and its mirror image cannot be perfectly matched. In this case, the unusual arrangement of the monotile pattern caused the light itself to display a chiral response.

"We found that the diffraction patterns themselves become chiral because the structure lacks mirror symmetry," explains senior author Masaya Notomi. "This kind of optical response is fundamentally different from that observed in conventional quasicrystalline materials."

Light Responds to Direction and Polarization

The researchers also found that the diffraction pattern changed depending on both the direction and polarization of the incoming light.

When the physical structures were mirrored, their optical behavior reversed as well. This showed that the response of the light was directly tied to the symmetry of the underlying pattern, revealing a new type of symmetry-controlled optical behavior.

"These results open a new direction of research on the fusion of quasiperiodic order and chirality," remarks Moritake. "Monotile patterns provide a platform for exploring optical phenomena that emerge from the interplay of symmetry, chirality, and aperiodicity."

From Abstract Mathematics to New Optical Physics

The researchers say structures inspired by monotiles could eventually contribute to technologies designed to manipulate light, control polarization, and support advanced optical devices.

More broadly, the findings show how a discovery that began as an abstract mathematical puzzle can lead to unexpected physical effects. The Smith hat was first celebrated for solving a question about how shapes can cover a surface. Now, its unusual geometry may also help researchers uncover new ways to control and study light.

Materials provided by Institute of Industrial Science, The University of Tokyo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10927. A Practical Introduction to the Twitch Helix API

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Behind almost every Twitch tool you have ever seen, the stat trackers, the moderation bots, the stream overlays, the alert systems, sits one interface: the Twitch Helix API. Twitch is unusually generous with public data, and Helix is the door to it. If you have ever wondered how third-party sites know who is live, what a channel's viewership looks like, or details a Twitch profile page does not even display, this is the machinery.

This article is a practical overview of Helix for developers who have not used it yet: what it offers, how its authentication works, what people build with it, and where the sharp edges are.

Helix is Twitch's current REST API, served from api.twitch.tv/helix. It replaced the old "Kraken" API, which was fully retired in 2022, so any tutorial still showing Kraken endpoints is a sign to keep scrolling. Helix covers users, streams, channels, games and categories, clips, chat, moderation actions, subscriptions, and events, with JSON in and JSON out.

Two things distinguish it from many platform APIs. First, a large share of it works without any user's permission, because the data is public by Twitch's own definition. Second, it is paired with EventSub, a push system that notifies your application when things happen (a stream goes live, a user follows, a message arrives) instead of forcing you to poll. Most serious Twitch tools use both: Helix for lookups, EventSub for live events.

Authentication is where most newcomers stall, mainly because Twitch offers several OAuth flows and tutorials rarely say which one you actually need. The short version:

Every request needs a Client ID, which you get by registering an application in the Twitch developer console (two-factor authentication on your account is required first). Requests carry the Client ID and a bearer token together; forgetting the Client ID header is the classic mystery 401.

App access tokens come from the client credentials flow, a single server-to-server request with your ID and secret. They have no user attached and that is fine, because public reads, user profiles, live streams, top games, clips, need no user context. For most read-only tools, this is the only flow you ever touch.

Tokens expire, so real applications cache them with their reported lifetime and refresh on a 401 rather than assuming a token lives forever.

A tour of the endpoints that power most tools:

Users. Give the users endpoint a username and it returns the account's public record: display name, profile image, description, Affiliate or Partner status, and the account's exact creation timestamp. That last field is interesting because Twitch does not display it anywhere on a profile, yet it is one of the platform's most useful trust signals, moderators use account age to spot throwaway accounts, and streamers use it to filter giveaway entries. The creation date alone powers a small subcategory of tools: free account age checkers, such as Twitch account age checker, are essentially this one endpoint with a clean page around it, taking any public username and returning the creation date, age, avatar, and broadcaster status. It is a good illustration of how far a single well-chosen Helix field can go.

Streams. The stream's endpoint reports who is live right now, with titles, categories, thumbnail URLs, and viewer counts. This is the backbone of every "is my favorite streamer live" notifier and every directory-style site, and polling it over time is how stat trackers chart viewership history.

Games and categories. Top games, category search, and metadata. Combined with streams data, this is how analytics sites rank categories and how tools answer "how crowded is this category right now."

Clips and videos. Clip metadata, view counts, and creation tools. Highlight compilers and clip-sharing bots live here.

Helix enforces a points-based rate limit per Client ID, and every response tells you where you stand through Ratelimit-Limit, Ratelimit-Remaining, and Ratelimit-Reset headers. Well-behaved tools read those headers and back off before hitting zero; badly behaved ones retry 429s in a loop and wonder why things get worse. For lookup-style tools the practical fix is caching, data like account creation dates never changes, so there is no reason to ask twice.

A few more edges worth knowing before they bite:

Usernames change, IDs do not. Store user IDs, not logins, or your saved data will eventually point at the wrong person.

Missing users are not errors. Looking up a nonexistent username returns a successful response with an empty data array, so handle the empty case explicitly.

Batching exists. The users endpoint takes up to 100 logins in one call, which matters the moment your tool checks lists instead of individuals.

Secrets stay server-side. The client secret identifies your application; calling the token endpoint from browser code hands your app's identity to anyone who opens devtools. Public-facing tools put a small server between the browser and Twitch.

The reason Twitch's third-party ecosystem is so large is that the barrier is so low: one public API, generous free access, and a platform where small questions genuinely matter to people. That is why the space is full of narrow tools that each answer one thing well, when was this account created, is this channel live, how fast is this category growing, which clips popped this week. Most of them are a weekend of work on top of one or two endpoints, and some of them grow into real products.

If you want to start, the path is short: register an application at the developer console, request an app access token, and call the users or streams endpoint for something you actually care about. The official documentation at dev.twitch.tv is current and well maintained, which is not something every platform can claim. Pick one piece of data Twitch collects but does not display, put a clean interface on it, and you are following the same recipe as most of the Twitch tools you already use.

#10925. Meta threatens to pull WhatsApp from Canada over new surveillance law

Meta has warned that it could pull WhatsApp and other encrypted services from Canada if the government moves forward with a controversial surveillance law.

The tech giant is pushing back against Bill C-22, also known as the Lawful Access Act, which would require technology companies to provide authorities with greater access to information during criminal investigations.

When asked whether Meta could withdraw services such as WhatsApp from Canada, the company’s head of Canadian public policy, Rachel Curran, said it would have to “review all of our options.”

She confirmed that withdrawing services would be something Meta would have to consider.

Bill C-22 would make it easier for Canadian police and intelligence agencies to access people’s information, while also allowing the government to secretly order tech companies to make certain technical changes. These could include installing surveillance-related equipment, developing tools to help authorities access data, and storing metadata about users’ communications.

Although the Canadian government has amended the legislation to strengthen encryption protections, Meta argues the changes do not go far enough.

“They have protected just the core and not the outside layers that are an integral part of the encryption process,” Curran explained.

The government, meanwhile, maintains that the legislation would not force companies to create encryption backdoors or give police unrestricted access to private messages.

Canada could restrict Roblox & Minecraft for under 16s as part of social media ban

Meta could remove Instagram and WhatsApp in New Mexico amid legal battle over teen safety

This isn’t the first time Meta has considered removing its services over a legal dispute. Earlier in 2026, the company warned it could withdraw Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp from New Mexico amid a legal battle over child safety.

Canada has also been considering separate restrictions on social media access for users under 16.

For now, WhatsApp remains available in Canada, and Meta has not announced any plans or a timeline for shutting down the service.

Bill C-22 has already passed the House of Commons but remains under consideration in the Senate, where lawmakers could introduce further amendments before it becomes law.

#10923. MIT’s tiny flying robot gets 450% faster with AI

Tiny flying robots may one day help rescuers search for people trapped beneath collapsed buildings after earthquakes. Because of their small size, these robotic insects could potentially move through narrow spaces that larger drones cannot enter while avoiding walls, debris, and falling objects.

Until recently, however, aerial microrobots have been far less nimble than the insects that inspired them. They typically moved slowly and followed relatively simple flight paths.

Researchers at MIT have now demonstrated a new approach that gives an insect-scale flying robot much greater speed and agility. The team developed an AI-based controller that allows the robotic bug to perform demanding aerial maneuvers, including repeated body flips.

Using a two-part control system designed to balance performance with computational efficiency, the researchers increased the robot's speed by about 450 percent and its acceleration by about 250 percent compared with their previous best results.

The robot was agile enough to complete 10 consecutive somersaults in 11 seconds, even while wind disturbances tried to knock it away from its intended path.

"We want to be able to use these robots in scenarios that more traditional quadcopter robots would have trouble flying into, but that insects could navigate. Now, with our bioinspired control framework, the flight performance of our robot is comparable to insects in terms of speed, acceleration, and the pitching angle. This is quite an exciting step toward that future goal," says Kevin Chen, an associate professor in the Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (EECS), head of the Soft and Micro Robotics Laboratory within the Research Laboratory of Electronics (RLE), and co-senior author of a paper on the robot.

Chen is joined on the paper by co-lead authors Yi-Hsuan Hsiao, an EECS MIT graduate student; Andrea Tagliabue PhD '24; and Owen Matteson, a graduate student in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics (AeroAstro); as well as EECS graduate student Suhan Kim; Tong Zhao MEng '23; and co-senior author Jonathan P. How, the Ford Professor of Engineering in the Department of Aeronautics and Astronautics and a principal investigator in the Laboratory for Information and Decision Systems (LIDS). The research was published in Science Advances.

AI Gives the Robot a Smarter Flight Controller

Chen's group has spent more than five years developing robotic insects.

The team recently created a more durable version of their tiny robot, which is about the size of a microcassette and weighs less than a paperclip. This newer design has larger flapping wings that support more agile flight. The wings are driven by soft artificial muscles that contract rapidly enough to produce extremely fast wingbeats.

The physical design had improved, but the robot's controller remained a major limitation. This controller acts as the robot's "brain," determining where the robot is and deciding how it should move. In earlier versions, a human had to tune the controller by hand.

For the robot to fly with the speed and aggressiveness of a real insect, the researchers needed a system that could handle uncertainty while rapidly solving complex control problems.

A controller powerful enough to do that would normally demand too much computation to operate in real time, especially because the aerodynamics of such a lightweight flying machine are highly complex.

To solve this problem, Chen's group collaborated with How's team to develop a two-step AI-driven control system. The design combines the robustness needed for difficult, high-speed maneuvers with enough computational efficiency to work in real time.

"The hardware advances pushed the controller so there was more we could do on the software side, but at the same time, as the controller developed, there was more they could do with the hardware. As Kevin's team demonstrates new capabilities, we demonstrate that we can utilize them," How says.

Teaching a Tiny Robot to Plan Difficult Maneuvers

The first part of the system uses what is known as a model-predictive controller. This type of controller relies on a dynamic mathematical model to predict how the robot will behave and determine the best sequence of actions for safely following a desired flight path.

Although this method requires substantial computing power, it can plan difficult movements such as aerial flips, sharp turns, and aggressive changes in body angle. The planner also takes into account limits on the amount of force and torque the robot can produce, helping it avoid movements that could lead to a crash.

Repeated flips are particularly challenging. To perform one somersault after another, the robot must slow down in exactly the right way so that it begins each new flip under the proper conditions.

"If small errors creep in, and you try to repeat that flip 10 times with those small errors, the robot will just crash. We need to have robust flight control," How says.

The researchers then used this expert planner to train a "policy" built on a deep-learning model through a process called imitation learning. The policy serves as the robot's real-time decision-making system, determining how and where it should fly.

In effect, imitation learning allows the team to capture the behavior of the more computationally demanding controller in a faster AI model that can operate quickly enough for real-time flight.

A major part of the challenge was generating enough useful training data to teach the policy how to handle aggressive maneuvers without creating an unnecessarily large data set.

"The robust training method is the secret sauce of this technique," How explains.

During flight, the AI-based policy receives information about the robot's position and converts it into real-time commands controlling factors such as thrust force and torques.

Experiments showed that the two-step control system dramatically improved the robot's performance. The insect-scale machine flew 447 percent faster and achieved a 255 percent increase in acceleration.

It also completed 10 somersaults in only 11 seconds while remaining within about 4 or 5 centimeters of its planned flight path.

"This work demonstrates that soft and microrobots, traditionally limited in speed, can now leverage advanced control algorithms to achieve agility approaching that of natural insects and larger robots, opening up new opportunities for multimodal locomotion," says Hsiao.

The team also demonstrated a movement known as a saccade. In insects, this involves pitching the body sharply, rapidly moving to a new position, and then pitching in the opposite direction to stop. This quick acceleration and braking can help insects determine their position and maintain clear vision.

"This bio-mimicking flight behavior could help us in the future when we start putting cameras and sensors on board the robot," Chen says.

One major goal for future research is to equip the microrobots with onboard cameras and sensors. That could eventually allow them to fly outdoors without depending on a sophisticated external motion capture system.

The team also plans to investigate whether onboard sensing could help groups of the robots avoid crashing into one another and coordinate their movements while navigating.

"For the micro-robotics community, I hope this paper signals a paradigm shift by showing that we can develop a new control architecture that is high-performing and efficient at the same time," says Chen.

This research is funded, in part, by the National Science Foundation (NSF), the Office of Naval Research, Air Force Office of Scientific Research, MathWorks, and the Zakhartchenko Fellowship.

#10921. Pokemon GO leak reveals which Legendary Pokemon would live closest to you

#10919. Quantum computing’s “dark horse” just proved it can go universal

A practical quantum computer must eventually be able to handle any type of quantum algorithm, much like a conventional laptop can run many different kinds of software. Researchers have now demonstrated a new way to reach that level of flexibility using unusual quantum objects known as non-Abelian anyons.

Scientists from the University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering (UChicago PME), Harvard, Stony Brook University, and Quantinuum created and tested a full set of operations based on non-Abelian anyons. Their results provide the first experimental demonstration that this approach can support the broad range of operations required for universal quantum computing.

"We demonstrated a so-called universal gate set -- meaning that if you store information in these emergent versions of quarks, and you move them around, you can do any quantum computation you might want to do," said Ruben Verresen, assistant professor of molecular engineering at UChicago PME and a co-author of the new study published in Nature.

A Possible Shortcut Around Costly Quantum Error Correction

The strategy could do more than help create a general-purpose quantum computer. It may also offer a more efficient route toward reliable quantum machines.

Quantum computers are extremely vulnerable to errors, so researchers typically protect information by distributing it across many physical qubits. These error correction methods can preserve data, but they usually do not provide every operation needed to perform universal quantum computation on that protected information.

To fill the gap, engineers often rely on specially prepared resources called "magic states." Producing them usually requires an intensive purification process known as distillation, which can consume a large fraction of a quantum computer's available qubits. The new results suggest that non-Abelian anyons may offer a way around that expensive step.

"Non-Abelian codes are a dark horse in the race to quantum error correction," said Henrik Dreyer, managing director and scientific lead at Quantinuum's Munich office and a co-author of the study. "In this work we show the first universal gate set in a non-Abelian code, which demonstrates that fault-tolerant computations can in principle be done without resorting to magic state distillation or cultivation, which are the most expensive operations in standard quantum error correction codes."

Ordinary qubits encode information using two basic states, along with quantum combinations of those states. Non-Abelian anyons work in a fundamentally different way.

These anyons do not appear as ordinary standalone particles in nature. Instead, scientists create them inside quantum circuits by entangling many conventional qubits into a collective state that behaves as though it were a new type of particle with its own unusual rules.

"The way I think about these codes is they're creating little universes -- alternative universes, but ones that reflect some of the properties of our own," Verresen said.

Each non-Abelian anyon carries an internal state that changes when one anyon is moved around another in a process known as braiding. The sequence of those braiding operations matters (that's what non-Abelian means), allowing information to be encoded and manipulated in ways that are unavailable to ordinary particles.

Because the information is spread across many entangled qubits rather than stored in a single location, it can also be naturally protected from some of the small disturbances that frequently disrupt conventional qubits. Braiding the anyons can also perform computational operations.

In 2024, a research team that included Verresen used a Quantinuum trapped-ion computer to create anyons associated with a symmetry group called D4 -- the rotations and reflections that leave a square unchanged -- demonstrating this form of non-Abelian order on quantum hardware for the first time.

That experiment showed that the unusual particles could be created and manipulated, but braiding them alone was not sufficient to carry out every operation required for universal quantum computing.

"In that work, we didn't demonstrate that those emergent forces were enough to do quantum computation," said Verresen. "That particular universe we created was not powerful enough."

Fusion Unlocks Universal Quantum Operations

For the new study, the researchers switched to another symmetry known as S3 -- the rotations and mirror-image flips that leave an equilateral triangle unchanged -- and created the corresponding anyons on Quantinuum's H2 trapped-ion processor using 54 entangled qubits.

The S3 system had the properties needed for universal quantum computation, but only when braiding was combined with another operation called fusion. During fusion, two anyons are brought together and the resulting state is measured.

The underlying concept was proposed theoretically in 2003 by Carlos Mochon, then a student of John Preskill at Caltech. Turning that theoretical idea into an experiment that could actually run on quantum hardware, however, required extensive additional theoretical and experimental work.

The researchers used pairs of anyons to encode "topological qutrits" which store three possible levels of quantum information instead of the two levels used by ordinary qubits.

By combining different braiding and fusion operations, the team demonstrated three key tools: one entangling gate produced through braiding and two different measurements created through fusion. Together, those operations can in principle produce any quantum operation, including operations that braiding alone could not achieve.

Beyond their possible computing applications, these unusual quantum states could also help researchers investigate fundamental aspects of physics.

"It is gratifying to see ideas we have spent our PhD work thinking about realized in the lab, and it has been made possible by remarkable advances in quantum hardware over the past few years," said Anasuya Lyons and Chiu Fan Bowen Lo, graduate students at Harvard University in the group of Ashvin Vishwanath who helped lead the work.

The researchers also demonstrated that non-Abelian anyons could directly produce a magic state using topological operations -- sidestepping the costly distillation process used in most quantum systems.

The experiment did not yet include active error correction. Instead, the researchers focused on testing the individual building blocks of the method and confirming that they could create a magic state consistent with theoretical expectations.

"So far, we've ignored the question of error correction. Here, it's more like a proof of principle," Verresen said.

The next major step will be combining these operations with active error correction. If that can be achieved, non-Abelian anyons could eventually become a practical basis for large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum computers. Verresen is already working with other PME researchers on new techniques for stabilizing non-Abelian quantum memories.

Materials provided by University of Chicago. Original written by Sarah C.P. Williams. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10917. Meta’s own AI helps man sue its creators after it got him banned from Facebook

A South Carolina business owner is suing Meta for $75,000 after the company’s security systems reportedly mistook its own AI assistant for a hacker and suspended his Facebook accounts.

Rather than handling the dispute himself, the man turned to the same AI assistant that triggered the suspension, which helped him prepare a lawsuit against the company responsible for creating it.

The unusual legal dispute comes as Meta continues expanding its AI technology, including Muse, an assistant designed to handle tasks on behalf of users. The case involves a business owner who had been using the technology to manage his company’s social media presence before losing access to his accounts.

According to a November 17 report from 404 Media, Joe Thomas, who owns South Carolina-based Kong Koatings Epoxy Countertops, had been using Meta’s Muse AI agent, which he named “Baby Mia” after his granddaughter, to help run his business.

The assistant handled various responsibilities, including writing emails, creating advertisements, and producing AI-generated videos for the company’s Facebook pages.

However, after Thomas granted Baby Mia access to his business accounts, Meta’s automated security systems reportedly identified the AI’s activity as suspicious and disabled the pages.

Thomas said the suspension lasted approximately one week, preventing him from receiving new business inquiries through Facebook advertising.

“I use AI for everything I do. All my emails, she runs my business. I’ve used it so much, Mia is very intelligent,” he told 404 Media.

“Meta shut my accounts down because they detected my AI, shut my accounts down for a week. I was pretty pissed off. She jumped right up and set up emails to Congressmen, Senator Tim Scott. She filed the whole lawsuit herself. I’ve done nothing in this case, I’ve let her handle the whole case […] she sent a fax out to Trump’s office.”

Meta’s AI can now make phone calls you don’t want to make

Meta responds after Muse AI gave stranger YouTuber’s home address on Facebook Marketplace

According to the report, Baby Mia contacted numerous organizations and government officials while attempting to restore Thomas’s accounts.

Those efforts included contacting the FBI, Federal Trade Commission, Federal Communications Commission, Better Business Bureau, California Attorney General, and multiple attorneys. The assistant also contacted lawmakers, arranged a call with Senator Tim Scott’s office, and faxed the White House.

Thomas said the AI sent more than 100 emails during the dispute, while he personally sent none.

After the senator’s office became involved, a Meta employee contacted Thomas, and he regained access to his Facebook accounts.

Despite recovering them, Thomas is continuing with his lawsuit, which was filed in Oconee County Court in South Carolina on November 16.

The complaint alleges that the suspension cost his business at least $25,000 in lost work, although he is seeking $75,000 in damages.

While Thomas credits Baby Mia with preparing the lawsuit and pursuing the legal action, he personally signed the court documents.

“I wouldn’t be sitting here talking to you right now if it wasn’t for what she was doing because I have not sent out one email at all and she has sent over 100,” Thomas explained.

He also said the AI made it possible for him to pursue legal action without spending thousands of dollars on legal assistance.

“There’s just no way I could have written this up, I wouldn’t have even known what to do,” he said.

“Now people like me get the chance to defend ourselves in court without spending thousands of dollars to do it when we get stepped on by the big tech companies. Before AI, nobody in my position would have ever, ever thought about suing or even known how to do it, and I wouldn’t have done it, either.”

The lawsuit remains unresolved, and it is unclear whether Thomas will receive any compensation from Meta.
