#10890. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10888. What we've been playing - "Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Another week has climaxed and I'm so sorry you had to read that on a Saturday morning. But here we are again, with our regular feature, where we talk about what we've been playing in order that you do too. This week, Marie has been staring at monster butts; Connor has capitulated to World of Warcraft; Kelsey is discovering the joys of a beloved Zelda game for the first time; and Bertie enjoys someone being punched in the face, because I guess he's that sort of person?

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. And now onward!

My favourite thing so far about Ocarina of Time is the judgement plastered on little Link's face when he encounters, well, almost everyone. Whether he means to or not, he serves sass all over town, and in Kakariko Village, and I am very much here for it.

Also, I wasn't prepared to come face-to-face with a monster's butt cheeks. At all. Imagine the scene: I'm enjoying the tunes of the Lost Woods, processing the changes that have happened to Hyrule in the last seven years, all while making my way to the Forest Temple. Then the Moblin's turn their backs to reveal... Well, everything.

This week I've spent an ungodly amount of time playing World of Warcraft: Forever (you may have read my preview on the website) and have been essentially glued to it during every minute of free time I've had away from work. I've got both my main professions above the level 225 mark and I'm firmly in the thick of it now. Levels take longer, items are actually getting good, and I've had to make a second alt character for extra bank space.

As a result, I've not been to the gym in a while. I actually got one of those emails asking why I've fallen off, offering some tips to get back into the routine. Sorry Nuffield Health, my sleep schedule is ruined and I've substituted proper workouts for squats and basic at-home cardio. Surely, this can't go wrong...

I don't have the same level of nostalgia for Ocarina of Time that my peers do, but that has not stopped me from enjoying every minute of running around Hyrule smashing pots and trying not to cry over the touching cutscenes and, well, Saria.

The game feels like Dark Souls for children (endearing) and that is completely my jam. It's magical, satisfying, and often sad. There's creepy enemies as well as cute ones, trap floors and doors, mysterious NPCs... and my heart just feels so warm whenever I'm playing it. My fingers are now crossed that there's a Majora's Mask remake in the pipeline over at Nintendo. (As well as a Super Mario 64 remake, which I admit, is pushing it. But for what it’s worth, I'd probably sell my soul to see it get the Ocarina treatment.)

It takes me a while to settle with games; it takes me a while to settle full stop. I suppose in Fire Emblem's case I'm out of touch, seeing as the last instalment I played was on 3DS. But I struggled with the garish enthusiasm early on and the whole thing seemed overly simple. Nevertheless, I've settled a bit now, and now that the game's layering on more strategy and Intelligent System's expertise is coming to the fore, I'm beginning to enjoy myself much more.

I wasn't expecting it to be quite so boobsy, though. Was Fire Emblem always this way? Perhaps it's my fault for picking the scantily clad queen Theodora, though that sort of presentation isn't reserved only for her. She's no wallflower, though. My favourite moment so far was when she got off her elephant during a parade - yes, her elephant - and walked over to punch a teenage boy in the face for halting proceedings. Apparently she was doing him a favour.

#10887. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.

#10885. Vice City was the GTA that established Rockstar's killer formula - and 24 years on, it's still one of the best games ever made

"Get the limey out of here," Burt Reynolds tells the producers of GTA: Vice City during a recording session for the game, as Rockstar co-founder Dan Houser enters the studio. "I'm not going to work if this limey Brit is in here!" Houser refuses to leave, so the story goes - he just came down to meet one of his heroes! - and he and Reynolds end up chest-to-chest, about to come to blows, before a studio engineer breaks up the fight.

I can't verify the truth of this story. It's one of those development rumours that's been around for a long time, originally written in a book about how games conquered pop culture called "All Your Base Are Belong to Us" by Harold Goldberg. But I like to believe it's true, because in this one moment, the two crucial strands of GTA: Vice City's DNA helix together: its star power and Rockstar's arrogance.

And I'm not saying that in a pejorative way at all. I think a game of Vice City's scope - stretched to breaking with ambition, budget, and technical wizardry - couldn't exist without the ego trip of Rockstar at its highest. This is a game that (somehow!) started as a bit of DLC for GTA 3, supposedly intended to launch as a mission pack when Rockstar North figured out how to port it to PC. GTA 3 launched in October 2001, and by some miracle - and lots of crunch, no doubt - Vice City hit shelves in October 2002.

This game - a game that would come to represent everything the GTA series is, for generations to come - was put together in about a year. I'd feel pretty full of myself, too, if I was able to crank out that sort of quality in that sort of timeframe. With a development budget of approximately $5 million (massive at the time, even though it seems paltry against GTA 6's rumoured $1 billion+) and a marketing spend of $13.2 million, Vice City's budget was indicative of a series shifting toward becoming a cultural milestone - a standard bearer for video games as a whole, and as explosive and attention-grabbing as Hollywood's biggest events.

It's not like Rockstar needed to borrow credibility from the movie industry to inject some panache into its work, but even so, I think it's in its vortex of unbelievable Hollywood talent that Vice City really makes its mark. This was the first time Rockstar used a voiced protagonist, and by the developer's own admission, it was "a riddle" they had to solve. It didn't come easy; by making the protagonist a fullyfleshed-out character, players also had to like him - not an easy ask when he's a bonafide psychopath with a fetish for revenge like Tommy Vercetti. But Ray Liotta pulls it off without breaking a sweat. The actor has gone on record saying it was a rough gig ("You're creating a character that's not there before", he bizarrely complained in one interview), and Vice City director Navid Khonsari often moaned about the actor's unpredictable moods, so I imagine the mood in the booth wasn't great, but the result? The result is phenomenal.

You shouldn't like Vercetti. He's arrogant, he's brash, he's a totem for all the worst elements of misogynistic 80s excess. And yet, because he's such a cartoon caricature of a Miami gangster, he becomes a sort of quasi-romantic figure; a mob stooge who's been set up by his old 'business associates' and by the very fabric of the American Dream. Vice City is, in a sense, a redemption story dressed in the clothes of a revenge one. Vercetti dreams of being a self-made man, of taming this new frontier of unbridled capitalism and clawing back the years that were taken from him by the mob in Liberty City.

From the moment he arrives in town and a cocaine deal quickly goes south, Vercetti is on the back foot. In the opening hours of the game, he - and, of course, you by extension - work for pocket change, picking up menial jobs here and there while meeting all the freaks and strangers Vice City has to offer. There are no less than 50 story characters here, brought to life by Ray Liotta, Tom Sizemore, Luis Guzmán, Gary Busey, Danny Trejo, Debbie Harry, Danny Dyer, Philip Michael Thomas, William Fichtner, and many, many more besides.

Playing Vice City again in 2026, I was absolutely floored by this cast - and how well their personalities and mannerisms translate to the almost puppet-like visuals Rockstar deployed for the game. Reynolds - despite not having a good time in the booth, if the story up top is anything to go by - is a highlight; the bombast and bravado of his Avery Carrington feels like a prototype for some of the more extreme characters we meet later in the series. I think I can even see a bit of Trevor in him, you know? It's how the Housers would take a trope - say, ruthless Texan oil magnate - and amplify it to its furthest, most cartoon extreme: what does this person look like with all the guardrails off, with the heady intoxication of unlimited capital at their disposal? For Trevor, that trope is 'unhinged redneck' and the cartoonish extreme is 'without a single shred of a conscience'. You can apply the formula to just about anything in the series, and it makes sense.

For Tommy Vercetti, though, it's slightly more nuanced. He isn't just 'Scarface' with a touch more humanity - no, he's a tragic figure, betrayed by all his closest friends. In striving to take back some power and gain a little more control over his life, he ends up decapitating the local drug smuggling scene and taking over as its head. The first half of Vice City is Sisyphean; an endless, uphill slog to scrabble just enough money to get by. Then, all of a sudden, the world opens up: you're now head of a drug smuggling ring, operating out of a suspiciously Scarface-like mansion, with the city at your fingertips.

The way the game, previously tightly-coiled and a bit miserly with its freedom, now unfurls in front of you is a perfect reflection of this new-found power. Suddenly, your world isn't governed by the little mission icons on your minimap, you're not being told what to do over the phone by contacts wanting something from you. No, you're calling the shots. This is the 80s, so those that are making it big are making it big, and those that are failing are failing. In a breathless display of uber-capitalist Yuppie thinking, you can even buy out nearly-bankrupt businesses and run them to your liking. The strategy is two-fold: one, you gain fronts for your other illicit activities, and two, you diversify your income streams and safeguard your assets should any one element fail.

And it's here, in its glorious second half, that Vice City becomes a breezy, beautiful bit of open-world catnip that fuses gameplay and story in a way few games have done so successfully since. You can buy a strip club (where you can launder cash), the Malibu Club (which comes with an outlandish set of missions culminating in a heist), the Cherry Popper ice cream van company (a front for dealing), the boatyard (a drug smuggling base run by two excellent potheads), Sunshine Autos (a fence for stolen cars), Kaufman Cabs (headed up by an old controller played by Debbie Harry), Interglobal Studios (an adult movie production company), and the Print Works (a counterfeiting operation). The roleplaying as Vercetti takes off; you're a bigshot touring his empire, helping out here and there, meeting your crew, setting up deals. This should feel like drudgery; workaday tedium unbecoming for a legitimate businessman like yourself. But every job, every bit of flavour text and dialogue, feeds into your growing sense of power… and Vercetti's unstoppable ego.

Alongside these key story beats are Vice City's non-essential tasks - which feel like a precursor to the 'Strangers and Freaks' missions in later GTA and Red Dead games - including the Love Fist missions. These, I think, are the best missions in the game (I'd also like to note here that 'Jez Torrent' is an amazing name for a New Wave of British Heavy metal frontman). But it's the way these core and non-essential missions dovetail that feels so great, and where the open world of Vice City really comes into its own.

In many ways, Rockstar's work here created the blueprint for what would become the Grand Theft Auto 'formula'; galvanising cinematic setpieces with free-roam activities and meta-progression that manage to alloy player agency with this sense of perpetual, unstoppable momentum. And it's all fun, too. There are some abysmal tasks, sure - piloting RC aircraft to ruin building sites, navigating boats with the wonkiest physics you've ever seen around stunt courses, waiting 15 minutes a pop for rare cars to spawn - but generally speaking, Vice City has the fun factor.

I don't think that's something I can say of other GTA games: I think often, they're a bit bloated around the middle, padded out with baggy missions and feckless busywork to give the illusion of depth and breadth. GTA 4 is also one of the most dour and oppressive open worlds I've experienced (this is a compliment, mostly), but it's not particularly fun. GTA 5, especially the Trevor segments out in the rural counties, can get a bit one-note - why introduce us to this massive world, to the state of Los Santos, if you're going to quarantine us to the rural outskirts when we're just starting to familiarise ourselves with the city? Why put up rails in a world begging us to revel in its open-ended sandbox? The only time that happens in Vice City is at the start, and the game is better for it: 'if you want to live the Scarface fantasy, buddy, you've got to earn it'.

And there's so much to love about the vibes of Vice City. The guys in shorts smoking pot on the beach; the girls in bikinis rollerblading down the promenade; the neon-drenched slick of a Florida night in the rain; the still-unbelievable who's who of music, deployed with panache in certain cutscenes; the incidental chatter of coked-up presenters on the radio; the distinct realisation of the Haitian and Cuban districts on the map; how 'downtown' actually feels like downtown. You'd think all this would have aged out of relevance by now - especially with GTA 5 being as lush and premium as it is - so it's a real credit to all those Rockstar employees that no doubt spent countless nights sleeping under their desks that Vice City still feels this good now, in 2026.

GTA: Vice City is still one of the best games ever made, I think, and probably one of the most important, too. Buoyed by Rockstar's ambition, and propelled to mainstream success by the strength of its cast, it represented video games coming out of their awkward teenage years and blossoming into young adults; biting off slightly more than they could chew, but cocksure and confident all at the same time.

Given how much of Vice City is built around the visibility and power of its cast, it's disappointing we're never going to see these characters really represented in any other GTA title again. Liotta, already unhappy about the process of recording his lines, reportedly refused to come back as Vercetti for San Andreas. And that's a real shame because - by former lead writer Dan Houser's own admission - Liotta saved his "awful writing" with his talent. The actor passed away in May 2022.

Though he balked with Reynolds, Vice City director Khonsari could usually corral the rampant ego of his Hollywood hires in the GTA studio. "If you want me to break it down to you," he’d say, "these games gross over half a billion dollars, more than all of your movies put together!" I think you can see that bravado in the spirit of Vice City, and reflected in the runaway ego of Tommy Vercetti. And I think - without that almost toxic sense of self-belief - Vice City would never have happened.

#10883. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."

#10881. What's the best GTA?

As we teeter on the brink of gaming's biggest launch of 2026 - perhaps even decade - so many questions swirl: which GTA 6 cars will make the best vrrrrm? Is it possible for me to be any more obsessed with that Justice theme song? On a scale of one to ten, how many dongs? Answers will inevitably come, but for now - as we crank up the Phil Collins and roar deeper into Eurogamer's GTA Week - there's only one question we're concerned with. Yes, it's the return of our irregular series The Big Question, in which we ponder another topical talking point before wafting it lovingly over to you. And this time around we're asking, what's the best GTA?

Because I'm old, my journey with the series began all the way back in 1997, with the advent of GTA 1. I still vividly remember sitting with the included paper map sprawled across my lap, wide-eyed at the top-down sandbox as my hands reckoned with the weird controls. I remember feeling dizzy at the thrill of high-speed freedom (and possibly the antiquated frame rate) as I tore across Liberty City, San Andreas, and Vice City - particularly whenever this catchy little number came on. I was never massively into those early GTAs, though, not even when the series shifted to full 3D with GTA 3. And it wasn't until Vice City's arrival in 2002, that I began to properly appreciate the series' rhythms - even though I'll readily admit I was at my happiest tearing through the streets with the radio blaring. Vice City's soundtrack, from A Flock of Seagulls to Loverboy and everything in between, is still unmatched today.

But is it the best GTA? Vice City is certainly the GTA I've fondest memories of, even if I did mostly play it as a glorified jukebox. And as much as I've a soft spot for Chinatown Wars on Nintendo DS (a game whose propulsive style and classic top-down aesthetic did a fine job of melding GTAs old and new), Vice City is the GTA I've returned to most. But what about others on the Eurogamer team? I asked, they've answered with their own personal bests, and when they're done yammering, it's over to you - there's even a poll! And don't forget we'll be exploring the series more thoroughly as GTA Week continues.

I think, even to this day, GTA: Vice City represents what I think the best Grand Theft Auto is. The vibes, even 24 years later, are immaculate: its vision of Miami in the '80's, with all its cocaine-fueled, sun-washed Yuppie excess makes for the perfect backdrop in a game that's all about freedom and fantasy. The first half of Vice City orientates you wonderfully - casting you as Ray Liotta's arrogant, low-key psychopathic Tommy Vercetti - and letting you quietly get to know the rules of this frontier of narcotic hedonism.

Then you're let loose, wide-eyed with nostrils flared, into the second half of the game: a completely open-ended experience that's all about money, moxie, and madness. Take over businesses, build the Vercetti empire, strongarm your way into Vice City's seedy underbelly in any way you see fit. Even in a more nascent form, Rockstar managed to combine open world 'side activities' with its main narrative, building on everything GTA 3 put down without losing sight of the core theme (something I think San Andreas is guilty of; that game goes off the rails by the third island, whereas I think Vice City always keeps its focus quite neat).

With Vice City, it feels like Rockstar was at the peak of its power, the excesses of its neon-soaked in-game world reflected in the developer's ambitions in the real one: Ray Liotta, Burt Reynolds, Tom Sizemore, Luis Guzmán, Gary Busey, Danny Trejo, Debbie Harry, Danny Dyer, Philip Michael Thomas, William Fichtner… that is an absolutely ridiculous cast, and - in researching the game for something else I'm writing - I have learned there was so much on-set tension between this uncontrollable star power and Rockstar's development team that Reynolds nearly had a fistfight with studio co-founder Dan Houser.

That tension, and that slightly arrogant ambition on behalf of Rockstar, manifest in the bones of Vice City. It's a game struggling against its confines, made by a developer feeling like there is nothing in the world that can stop them. I imagine GTA 6 may feel the same, so how fitting, then, that we're going back to Vice City for the pleasure of it.

Barring the original, Vice City was the GTA I played longest, so those are the cornerstones of my GTA experiences. But San Andreas left the biggest impression on me. And I think it's because it was the first game I played that really attempted to portray Black culture, albeit through a stereotyped and potentially problematic West Coast rap lens' - I don't know how well it holds up today.

I was very into West Coast rap and hip-hop at the time, and the idea I could partially experience the lives which were referenced in that music so frequently - that I could live a kind of NWA origin story through the character CJ - really appealed to me, as it obviously did to many other people. It felt like I was coming straight outta Compton, as it were. It was rap tourism, which was probably totally fake and insincere to the actual experience of a life like that, but I drank it up all the same.

Fast-forward to GTA 5, which I think of as San Andreas mark two (in a similar way that I think of GTA 6 as Vice City mark two), and it's still startlingly apparent upon playing it - even now, many years after it was first released - that no other company attempts Black culture in the same way. No one even dares. And while I'm totally not the right person to evaluate how well Rockstar carries it off, it does make the GTA experience unique.

Back to San Andreas and I also enjoyed its ridiculous bloat: the gym sessions, the mini-games, eating burgers. It was like living a life in that sunny city, a life I couldn't live otherwise. I'm excited that some of those features are making their way back into GTA 6.

I've never visited America, but I've never been more fond of any city in the world than New York. It may not have been called that in GTA 4, but it is the closest any game has got to making me realise my dream of living in and exploring the city.

GTA 4's Liberty City has one of my favourite aesthetics in games: the dreary, autumnal look where the most sunshine you get is an overcast day. Perhaps New York's relatively compact layout (compared to sprawling Los Angeles) is what made it possible to replicate most of its boroughs in-game, and what helped it achieve a sense of place no other GTA comes close to. I knew my way around so much of that map just by landmarks. I was surprised how much of the area around Niko's house I remember when I revisited the game five-ish years ago.

I also consider GTA 4 to be the last entry in the series' classic era. Despite being the first to use Rockstar's now-standard RAGE engine, its controls were just about as loose and arcadey as its 3D predecessors. RAGE's weighty animations gave its characters and cars the look and motion of their real-life counterparts without interfering too much with how they felt to control. GTA 4 stood on the boundary of old and new while delivering the series' next evolution - a tough act for any sequel to follow.

So that's The Big Question, and the best - or at least our favourite - GTAs... now it's over to you!

N64, Nintendo Game Boy, PC, PS1, PS3, PSP

#10879. Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas is GTA 5 without the finesse - so why is the 2004 classic such a strange delight to return to?

There is a place in Whitewood Estates where a promising stretch of blacktop suddenly gives way to the desert. It feels like the road has been erased, a sentence left unfinished. It's quietly strange, a weird bit of urban wrongfooting. But I guess it makes some kind of sense. California, after all, is home to California City, a town that was planned and then never really built. It sits out there now, north of Los Angeles, streets laid out but slowly being eaten by the sand and dirt. Still, 14,000 people lived there as of 2018. Design limitation or something stranger, this digital dead end is a reminder that all of this land, so modern, so ancient - all of this is spook country.

It's fitting I guess that GTA San Andreas is such a strange delight to return to. As I've waited, like everyone else, for GTA 6, I've been back here in the GTA games of the past, via the slightly dodgy remasters from a few years ago. And what's come through for me, spending a few idle mornings messing around in San Andreas, a game I thought I knew pretty well, is how beautifully odd everything is now. The best part of 20 years have given a game that once strained against its host technology a genuine aesthetic. Surprisingly sparse in detailing, as angular and sheer as dream architecture, in love with expanses, San Andreas is perfectly placed, it seems to me, to capture just how weird California can be.

California and Nevada. This is the part I always forget. I mainly think of San Andreas these days through the lens of GTA 5, which gives you LA and its surroundings but lets the single city serve as the star. It has a bigger footprint than the earlier game, sure, but it uses that space to go deep on the detailing and the sense of a singular, fiercely specific place. San Andreas meanwhile offers three cities - and one of them isn't actually in California. Alongside spins on LA and San Francisco, you get a version of Las Vegas. It's quite neat really: the three points of some arcane spiritual triangle. The kind of thing Marvel Whiteside Parsons, aerospace engineer and Actual Wizard, might have scratched in the dust of his backyard to help him control the weather. He lived - and died - in Pasadena, so all this would have been familiar anyway.

There is a classic moment in every GTA. A moment where the map opens up in the early games, or when you explore enough in the later games, and simply get to understand the audacity of what is being attempted here. In San Andreas, and this feels apt, the best way to have this moment is to break the rules, sneaking into the open map before the game is fully ready for you. An early mission allows you to step away from the critical path, as it were, and peg it to the airport in Los Santos, where a plane is waiting to be pinched. Taxi, feel the thrust, and you're off. LA beneath you - sorry, Los Santos - and on the horizon a dinkytown version of the Golden Gate Bridge and the MGM Grand.

Dinkytown is the word. From the sky, the huge map of San Andreas, which still feels like an incredible piece of work - such ambition! - can look like toys laid out on a duvet in the remasters. Coming back from GTA 5, a masterpiece of texture, the cities of San Andreas in particular feel very weird - cramped and condensed and wonderfully stagey, arranged the way a kid might jury-rig them together from shoeboxes and stray cardboard. On your way out of Grove Street early on you pass the Los Santos equivalent of the Hollywood sign, looming so close the way it does in the movies but not in the actual city and you feel you could touch it. Downtown is reduced to a handful of the best buildings - it's always pleasing to see the one from the brilliant first act of Speed made the cut - but still manages to capture the important stuff too, which in this case is the way that modern LA looms somewhat cruelly over the old city, captured with a strange sepulchral dignity in the form of the City Hall, so easy to spot but now so small!

Elsewhere, the ersatz Las Vega Strip looks like a drawing based on memory - a few of the classic shapes are there, but it's a sketch of a place. In this way, I guess, it captures a truth about Las Vegas. Also, perhaps, it's an admission that this is a town comfortably out of the reach of satire. As for San Francisco, it's a 60s cop movie delight to fishtail around. It nails the hills and what more do you need for a game with cars in it?

Taken as a whole these cities feel mesmerising now. And it's the limitations that make them sing, I think: those sheer granite canyons of downtown LA with its towering blocks of architecture and surprisingly thin traffic, with only the very faintest of ambient soundtracks that ensures any gunfire will pop and clap and shake you out of your seat. (Remember, I am playing the remasters and sound effects may be missing.) This is the LA of Repo Man rather than Heat - an otherland where the buildings feel alien, even the air feels alien. At night, palm trees stand out against a sky that glows a deep, clean red. GTA 5 would finesse all of this, the skyboxes and whatnot, but perhaps in that finessing something is lost. (Both games, incidentally, have such a talent for capturing California's kitsch excess that they perhaps miss some of the sweeter truths, such as the astonishing diversity of cultures a city like Los Angeles finds homes for. But how could any game do that justice?)

Dinkytowns aside, what San Andreas does better than GTA 5 is space - the peculiar flat spaces of Southern California in particular, a state in which you can never quite tell where the wilderness ends and the landscaping begins, and where the ancient rocks that announce the horizon can look new, like stage settings, while the freeways feel truly ancient, concrete lizards sleeping on the dirt, bleached by the easy sun. I can lose hours out here. I have had to double- and triple-check that GTA 5 is the bigger game, in fact, because San Andreas still feels absolutely enormous, a trick that works even when you can see the not-Golden Gate Bridge seconds after taking off from not-LAX. Grab a bike and you can spend an absolute age exploring the between-spaces which video game California and real California are both so good at conjuring, stumbling on little towns you will never stumble on again, passing commercial follies, listening as the radio pumps out "All My Exes Live in Texas".

And, paradoxically, the more I play the more I realise San Andreas is very good at the little things in its own odd way. GTA 5 is an astonishing feat of seeing and transposing, but I don't think it has anything across its acres to match the sense of place offered by San Andreas' take on Grove Street, where the story begins. It's just a neighborhood, but as you cross its small space and recross it in the game's opening hours, learning how to eat food and buy clothes and get a haircut and spray paint on walls and all that jazz, the sense that this is your home is grooved and regrooved into the experience. Maybe this is why it's such a thrill to return to this earlier pass at Los Santos. Because this one is where you truly live.

#10878. People are taking an unapproved weight-loss drug. AI found something surprising

An experimental weight-loss drug that has not yet received regulatory approval is already attracting users willing to obtain unapproved versions online. Now, scientists are using artificial intelligence to investigate their experiences, uncovering unexpected symptom reports that differ from those documented in clinical trials.

The drug, retatrutide, belongs to a new generation of GLP-1 medications designed to help regulate appetite and body weight. Although it remains under investigation and is not approved for use, people are purchasing unapproved formulations that are not intended for human consumption and discussing their experiences on internet forums.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania analyzed nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments from approximately 40,000 users to investigate these accounts. Their study, published in JAMA Network Open, provides the first systematic examination of potential side effects reported by people claiming to use unapproved retatrutide.

One finding stood out. Among users whose reported symptoms could be classified, increased appetite was the most common complaint. That was particularly unexpected because retatrutide is designed to suppress hunger, and its Phase 2 clinical trials primarily identified digestive problems as the most frequent side effects.

AI Uncovers Unexpected Weight-Loss Drug Reports

"Our goal is to identify hypotheses in online conversations that we could test. AI helps us find recurring patterns in people's self-reports, including experiences that may be missed by conventional drug-safety monitoring," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS), Director of Penn's Computational Social Listening Lab and senior author of the study.

However, the researchers emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as proof of previously unknown side effects. Reddit users represent a very different population from clinical trial participants, and their accounts cannot establish whether the drug caused the symptoms they described.

There is also considerable uncertainty about what these individuals are actually taking. Products sold outside regulated medical channels may differ from the genuine experimental medication in their ingredients, purity, and dosage.

"We can't say that retatrutide is causing these symptoms, or even that Reddit users are actually taking genuine retatrutide, since the drug is not legally available, and gray market formulations vary," says Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author of the study.

Rather than confirming new risks, the research offers scientists an opportunity to identify questions that warrant more rigorous investigation.

"This is the first glimpse into the experiences of a population that is otherwise outside conventional drug safety monitoring because they are taking unapproved versions of a drug that has yet to finish clinical trials," says Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS.

How Researchers Analyzed 150,000 Reddit Posts

The project builds on previous research by the University of Pennsylvania team. In that earlier investigation, scientists used large language models (LLMs), the type of artificial intelligence that powers many modern chatbots, to examine more than 400,000 Reddit posts discussing semaglutide and tirzepatide.

That analysis revealed reports of possible side effects, including menstrual irregularities, that may not have been fully documented in clinical trials or official drug safety records.

"We wanted to apply the same methodology to gray-market retatrutide," says Sehgal.

Retatrutide is particularly interesting because it works differently from existing GLP-1 medications. Semaglutide acts on one hormone receptor involved in blood sugar control and appetite regulation, while tirzepatide targets two. Retatrutide activates three receptors, potentially producing a different combination of effects throughout the body.

To investigate what people were experiencing, the researchers used AI to examine nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments. They identified approximately 14,000 users who claimed to have personally taken an unapproved version of retatrutide.

The AI systems then extracted descriptions of symptoms that users associated with the medication and translated those descriptions into standardized medical terminology. This allowed researchers to compare reports even when different people used different words to describe similar experiences.

After excluding expected effects such as weight loss and reduced appetite, the team identified 7,823 users who reported at least one symptom that could be medically classified.

"A researcher might be able to examine a few thousand Reddit posts by hand, but looking at nearly 150,000 would be an enormous undertaking," says Sehgal. "AI makes it possible to analyze these conversations at scale, identifying patterns in what people are sharing that would otherwise be difficult to recognize."

The most striking difference involved hunger.

In retatrutide's Phase 2 clinical trials (the drug is currently in Phase 3 trials, the final step before FDA approval), participants most commonly experienced gastrointestinal complaints, including nausea. These effects are familiar concerns with medications that influence appetite and digestion.

But among Reddit users who reported taking unapproved retatrutide, the pattern looked different. Increased appetite emerged as the most frequently described classifiable symptom, despite the substantial weight-loss effects demonstrated in clinical testing.

The researchers also compared the new findings with their earlier analysis of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

Compared with those earlier Reddit reports, discussions involving unapproved retatrutide contained proportionally more mentions of appetite-related changes, sleep disturbances, increased heart rate, and muscle-related symptoms. Reports of nausea and other digestive complaints were proportionally less common.

These differences could reflect how retatrutide interacts with the body. However, they could also result from differences between the groups of people discussing the medications, their other drug use, or the contents of the unapproved products.

"Retatrutide works differently from existing GLP-1 drugs, so it's possible that its effects on the body are different, too," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study.

"But the people discussing it on Reddit also may be different," she notes. "Users willing to obtain an unapproved medication on the gray market likely differ from other clinical populations. Some may be taking multiple experimental peptides, and we can't verify exactly what they're taking. We need more research to understand what, if anything, these differences in self-reported symptoms mean."

Can AI Detect Drug Safety Concerns Earlier?

Beyond the findings about retatrutide, the study demonstrates how AI could potentially improve the early detection of drug safety concerns.

Traditional monitoring systems depend heavily on clinical trials, reports submitted to regulators, and information collected by healthcare professionals. People obtaining experimental medications through unapproved channels may not be represented in those systems.

Social media offers another source of information, allowing researchers to identify recurring experiences that might otherwise escape attention. However, these reports must be independently investigated before they can be considered reliable evidence of a drug-related problem.

The Penn team has already seen an example of how this approach can help guide further research.

In their previous investigation of semaglutide and tirzepatide, researchers identified online reports of menstrual irregularities. Those findings subsequently encouraged another scientific team to examine similar symptoms using the FDA's database of reported adverse drug events.

The follow-up study, published in Obstetrics & Gynecology, also detected disproportionate reports of certain menstrual changes. Although those findings did not establish causation, they provided additional reasons to investigate the potential connection.

The researchers hope their retatrutide findings will lead to similar independent studies, particularly as the drug advances through clinical development.

"That's what we hope will happen with this study," says Sehgal. "Other researchers can take what we find on Reddit and look for the same patterns in other datasets, using entirely different methods. Whether those signals hold up or not, the goal is to build a better understanding of what people are actually experiencing."

The research was conducted through the University of Pennsylvania's School of Engineering and Applied Science and Perelman School of Medicine. Benjamin Rader of Boston Children's Hospital also contributed as a co-author. Funding was provided in part by the University of Pennsylvania's Penn Medical Communication Research Institute. Tronieri disclosed receiving an investigator-initiated grant from Novo Nordisk on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, along with consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. No conflicts of interest were reported by the other authors.

#10876. As blockbuster game releases run scared from GTA 6, meet the brave devs tackling Rockstar's behemoth head-on

Grand Theft Auto 6. It's got cool cars, crime, a big new city to explore, and the potential to hoover up so much player time that many developers have chosen to give it the widest berth possible. It's led to an October uncommonly packed with big-hitters, and a November - ordinarily one of gaming's biggest months - that's near-barren. But not every developer is steering clear. Despite the potential risks, a select few have looked at GTA 6 and seen not a threat but an opportunity, so what's driving these brave souls?

Escape Academy 2: Back 2 School, a first-person puzzle adventure with a popular predecessor, was originally set to launch this September, but like many games this year saw its release date moved. However, its developer bucked the industry trend, shifting its sequel not away from GTA 6's orbit, but closer. "The thing that scares me more than this monocultural event - GTA 6 - is releasing in a week when there are like 15 good games rather than one big one", Coin Crew Games co-founder Wyatt Bushnell tells me when I ask about the studio's decision.

Escape Academy 2's original release date might have kept it away from Grand Theft Auto 6, but it would have instead faced a potential "bloodbath" of competition from the likes of Onimusha: Way of the Sword, Blood of the Dawnwalker, Control Resonant, and more. "That was our rationale," Bushnell says. "We can still cut into the GTA stuff, but it would have been a lot harder in September and October."

It helps, notes Bushnell, that Escape Academy 2 and Grand Theft Auto 6 - one a contemplative puzzle game, the other a blockbuster action spectacle - are serving very different crowds. That's a sentiment echoed by David Lowey, Atari's senior director of sales and marketing. Atari has similarly bucked industry trends and is releasing two games - Godzilla Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered and Barbie Rewind - in the now largely vacant November window.

When we speak, Lowey says there's a clear logic to smaller companies "sneaking" into spots that bigger-budget games can't risk filling, especially if their games differ greatly from Grand Theft Auto 6. "Look at Barbie Rewind coming out on the 12th," he elaborates. "It's a retro game, so it'll pull in people who grew up with these games [and] there's the cosy dreamhouse design at the core ... which is a vastly different game type to GTA 6 and plenty of other games.

"It's not an FPS, not a high-octane action game, there's not a lot of combat," Lowey continues. "It's designed to be the exact opposite. It's a game you're meant to curl up in a chair and play." And while he acknowledges the "reality of modern gaming" means there'll be some audience overlap between Barbie Rewind and GTA 6 players - "These sort of older ideas of what people play by gender and age have been smashed", he says - it "won't be the biggest overlap in the world". That, then, makes going toe-to-toe an easier bet.

But for Bushnell and Escape Academy, there's another consideration making GTA 6's launch window feel less of a risk: longer-tail sales. "The traditional thinking is that you have this big release and make all of your money in those first two weeks," he explains. "I don't think that holds a tonne of water anymore. The vast majority of sales for Escape Academy were made well after its release. The reality is with digital distribution, discounting, and the fact there is this shelf of games, we're in a world where games last far longer than their initial launch." That means it's less of a concern if GTA 6 sucks up everyone's attention for a few weeks.

And for Atari's David Lowey, Godzilla Destroy All Monsters and Barbie Rewind's proximity to GTA 6 has brought added benefits, enabling both games to capitalise on the hype around Rockstar's open-world epic in their own way. "One of the most rewarding things has been the fascination we've seen online around the proximity of Godzilla, Barbie, and GTA," he says.

The community has found it easy to root for games releasing in the behemoth shadow of GTA 6. Godzilla Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered - an updated version of the 2002 GameCube party fighting game, which released on 3rd November - has been dubbed, tongue in cheek, the "GTA killer" by fans, while others have joked that Rockstar is "running scared" from Barbie Rewind. "There's no question that this buzz based on the closeness has created player interest," adds Lowey. "In retrospect the timing worked well for us."

There's one game, though, that isn't just orbiting GTA 6, it's facing the triple-A juggernaut head-on. Edmund McMillen, Tyler Glaiel, and publisher Nicalis are bringing The Binding of Isaac: Repentance+ to Xbox Series X/S, PlayStation 5, and Switch 2 on 19th November, the same day GTA 6 arrives. "That was [Nicalis'] idea," McMillen laughs when we speak. "'Wouldn't it be funny if...?' I was like, whatever, that's funny ... We're done, so we've got to release this month ... It's a fun experiment."

"There's a possibility [Repentance+'s release date] wasn't that stupid an idea," McMillen adds, "and if it was a stupid idea, it's a funny stupid idea." He also hints there's a "funny stupid" thing awaiting players who do a Repentance+ daily run on any platform during GTA 6's launch...

McMillen is, though, all too aware of the impact major releases can have on other games. "My heart says it doesn't matter, but my brain has shown me it does," he explains. "When Mewgenics came out it blew up; we went past two million sales almost right off the bat. It was such a blasting, skyrocketing thing for like three weeks. Then Slay the Spire 2 came out, and it sucked everybody out. The same players who liked Mewgenics, they were Slay The Spire players. So they all went woosh and right over ... When a new thing comes out, people all move to the new thing ... You can see numbers go from astronomical to very small, and then if the game is good, it'll trickle back in."

The way McMillen sees it, though, GTA 6 isn't immune to this natural ebb of flow of player interest either, and its pull might not be as long-lasting as some fear. "I know a lot of people buy Grand Theft Auto. I don't know how many people play Grand Theft Auto," he explains. "I don't know how many people are going to be sucked into this game and stay the whole time." And for McMillen, the fact GTA 6 is putting so many players in front of consoles is a positive.

"I think the people who like Isaac are going to play the game regardless," he says. "[But] logically, there's a record number of people who are going to be on the console the game is on at launch [because of GTA 6] ... They might be like me, play [GTA 6] for a while, and say, 'Okay, I have a console with one game, what am I going to play next?' Then, you know, a rising tide will raise all ships. I think Grand Theft Auto will bring people back to consoles, because consoles are abysmal right now. Sales are bad and no one is playing anything. People own next-gen consoles and they're just sitting there..."

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Think about what's sitting in your inbox and Drive right now. Client contracts and invoices. Project specs under NDA. Staging credentials a client emailed you. That API key or password you meant to delete.

For most developers and freelancers, all of it lives in Google Workspace or Microsoft 365. Both are excellent tools, and both encrypt your data in transit and at rest. But in their standard setup, the provider holds the encryption keys, which means your provider (and the integrated apps and AI features connected to it) can access what's inside.

Proton Workspace takes a different approach: the same everyday tools, with encryption designed so that not even Proton can read your data.

Here's how the three compare, what each does best, and which makes the most sense for how you work.

Google and Microsoft are built around ecosystem integration. Proton is built around keeping your data yours, without sacrificing productivity tools.

Google Workspace is the go-to suite for startups, agencies and freelancers, and for good reason. Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Meet work together seamlessly, and several people can edit the same file in real time without emailing versions back and forth.

Google encrypts data at rest and in transit, and offers client-side encryption on eligible editions. But that extra layer requires higher-tier plans and configuration, so in the default experience Google holds the keys. A password manager and VPN aren't part of the suite.

Best for: Teams whose top priorities are real-time collaboration and third-party integrations.

If your work runs on Word, Excel and Outlook, Microsoft 365 needs little introduction. Business Standard includes full desktop apps, and Excel remains the benchmark for heavy spreadsheet work.

That breadth comes with complexity. Licensing, permissions and device management can eat into time a small team or solo developer would rather spend shipping. And like Google, Microsoft's everyday productivity experience isn't built around end-to-end encryption, while Copilot and advanced security features add cost.

Best for: Businesses built on desktop Office apps and established Microsoft workflows.

Everything is managed from one admin console, with one bill.

This is the core difference. Proton uses zero-access encryption: your data is encrypted before it reaches Proton's servers, and Proton never holds the keys. Proton can't read your data. Not won't. Can't.

The honest trade-offs: Proton Docs and Sheets cover core editing and collaboration, not every advanced Excel feature. There's no presentation app yet, and the third-party integration ecosystem is smaller than Google's or Microsoft's.

#10872. MIT scientists build a tiny robot that swims using living muscle cells

A swimming robot does not necessarily need a bulky motor or powerful mechanical parts to move through water. MIT researchers have demonstrated that a single, carefully engineered layer of living muscle cells can generate enough force to propel a tiny robot forward.

The engineers developed an extremely thin swimming robot that uses living muscle tissue to move its fins. Described in a new study published in Advanced Functional Materials, the device has a flexible gel framework roughly the length and width of a stick of gum. The gel forms two fins, each coated with a layer of muscle cells far thinner than a human hair. These cells have been genetically modified to contract when exposed to light.

By directing light onto either fin, the researchers can trigger muscle contractions that make the fin flap and push the robot through water. Adjusting which fin receives light and how frequently it is illuminated allows the team to control both its speed and direction.

In experiments, the remarkably thin robot successfully swam and turned through a simple underwater maze. At maximum speed, it covered approximately four times its body length per minute. Although that is much slower than an Olympic swimmer, who can travel up to 65 body lengths per minute, it is comparable to the swimming pace of a cow shark moving leisurely through the ocean.

"It takes a lot of force to move through water versus air," says study author Ritu Raman, associate professor of mechanical engineering at MIT. "The robot's quite strong, given its size. "

A New Generation of Robots Powered by Living Muscle

The device represents the first demonstration of a very thin, two-dimensional robot that can move from place to place using living muscle tissue.

Most existing biohybrid robots rely on relatively large, three-dimensional structures made from laboratory-grown skeletal muscle. Producing these structures typically requires millions of cells. Raman explains that thinner designs could reduce manufacturing costs while potentially improving movement efficiency.

"Currently, biohybrid robots from our group and others' are built from bulky, 3D chunks of lab-grown skeletal muscle that require millions of cells to fabricate," says Raman, who notes that thinner, less bulky designs such as the team's new bot could be cheaper to build and could move more efficiently. "We believe that biohybrid robots powered by living muscle could one day perform delicate jobs like exploring environments too fragile or unpredictable for conventional hardware, because living tissue is soft, responsive to its surroundings, and can heal itself."

The MIT research team also included first author Maheera Bawa, along with Arielle Berman, Laura Schwendeman, Ferdows Afghah, and Seanbiron Johnson.

From Artificial Muscle to a Swimming Robot

The new swimming robot builds on an earlier achievement by Raman's laboratory. Last year, the researchers created an artificial muscle disk inspired by the human iris, demonstrating how a thin layer of living cells could produce precisely controlled movements.

For that project, the engineers patterned a gel disk with grooves extending outward from its center and forming concentric circles. They then placed living muscle cells on the surface, where the cells grew into a thin layer following the patterned channels.

When illuminated, the muscle tissue contracted in different directions, causing the disk to stretch and compress. Its movements resembled the way the iris of the human eye changes shape as the pupil expands or narrows.

The experiment established that researchers could grow extremely thin muscle layers in intricate arrangements and use light to control their movements along multiple directions.

"People hadn't seen this muscle architecture engineered from scratch before," Raman says. "And the cells were moving in multiple directions. But they only moved about 100 microns. From a robotics perspective, their movements were tiny."

For the latest project, the researchers wanted to dramatically increase the amount of movement and force generated by the muscle cells. Their goal was to produce contractions powerful enough to propel a robot through water.

They determined that achieving this would require a better foundation for the living muscle tissue.

The earlier artificial iris had used fibrin, an exceptionally soft gel, as its underlying structure. However, the team found that fibrin could quickly shrink under the forces exerted by contracting muscle cells, limiting the effectiveness of the resulting movement.

To overcome this problem, the engineers investigated three characteristics of the gel foundation: its chemical composition, its stiffness, and the dimensions and geometry of the grooves used to organize muscle growth.

"For engineering any type of tissue, it's known that these are knobs you can tune," Raman says. "And we wanted to optimize all these parameters to support live muscle cells."

Engineering a Stronger Muscle-Powered Robot

Finding the right supporting material required testing gels with different formulations, stiffness levels, and surface patterns.

The researchers compared several groove shapes, including narrow channels with square profiles and longer channels with curved bottoms. They discovered that muscle cells aligned more effectively inside square-shaped grooves.

This alignment was important because muscle cells that grow in the same direction are more likely to merge into fibers. Those fibers can then form stronger muscle tissue that contracts in a coordinated way. The square grooves therefore provided a more effective structure for producing force.

The team also replaced fibrin with gelatin methacrylate (GelMA), a material commonly used to grow and engineer biological tissue.

By adjusting the formulation of GelMA, the researchers produced supporting gels with varying degrees of stiffness. They then examined how muscle cells developed on each surface and measured their ability to generate force.

Stiffer gels produced the best results. On these firmer surfaces, muscle cells grew in better alignment and generated stronger contractions.

The engineers next investigated how thick the gel should be. They determined that a film measuring approximately half a millimeter offered an effective balance. It was sturdy enough to support a single layer of muscle cells while remaining sufficiently lightweight to prevent the contracting cells from detaching from the surface.

The team further improved muscle performance through a form of exercise. Repeated pulses of light stimulated the cells to contract, strengthening the muscle tissue before it was incorporated into the swimming robot.

With the gel structure optimized and the muscle cells strengthened, the researchers assembled their swimming device.

The finished robot consisted of a thin gel framework divided into two fins. Both sides of the gel were patterned with square-bottomed grooves and covered with living muscle cells. As the cells developed, they fused into organized muscle fibers capable of contracting together.

Each fin could be activated independently using light, giving the researchers a simple way to control the robot's movements.

"You can think of the robot as having two independent muscles," Raman says. "If we shine a light on just one, only that muscle moves. If shining on both, they both flap."

To test the device, the researchers placed it inside a large petri dish filled with water. They then moved a light source by hand, illuminating the fins to guide the robot through a maze positioned inside the dish.

As the muscles contracted, the fins flapped and propelled the robot through the water. By changing the light stimulation, the engineers successfully controlled its movement and helped it navigate the obstacles.

Although the robot demonstrated the ability to swim and steer, its current shape remains relatively simple. The primary objective of the experiment was to establish that an exceptionally thin layer of engineered muscle tissue could generate enough force for swimming.

The researchers now plan to refine the robot's body to improve its speed. Beyond demonstrating a new approach to biohybrid robotics, the technology could eventually be useful for monitoring aquatic environments or operating in locations where conventional rigid machines might cause damage.

"Our next goal is to optimize the body design to enable faster swimming," Raman says. "But even at slow swim speeds, one could imagine a muscle-powered swimmer being used for purposes like environmental monitoring in aquatic environments."

The Office of Naval Research provided partial funding for this research.
