#10868. Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

#10863. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

#10862. Prompt Management in TypeScript: LangSmith vs Git vs 3 Open-Source Registries

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every prompt registry we tested can change a prompt in a running TypeScript app, roll it back, and give production and staging different versions. LangSmith's prompt hub, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore all passed those three tests. The real differences show up elsewhere: which template syntax each one accepts, how many npm packages it pulls in, how long a change takes to reach the app, and what happens when the registry is down.

We tested all of this with one small app and one prompt, which has a condition and a loop. Git files served as the baseline. Every number below comes from a recorded run.

Most teams start with prompts in code: a template string, or a .txt file next to the code that uses it. That works until a product manager wants to change one sentence, and the change has to wait for a full release.

A prompt registry moves the text out of the deploy. The app fetches the prompt by name and by a label such as production. You change which version the label points to, and the app picks up the new text without a deploy.

LangSmith's prompt hub is the best-known registry, but it is not the only one. Several of the LangSmith alternatives also manage prompts, and three of them are open source and self-hostable: Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. We covered how those tools handle tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent. This article covers prompts only.

Every tool got the same prompt, support-reply. Its template takes three variables: a customer name, a plan, and a list of help articles. It has one condition: a priority line appears only for premium customers. It also has one loop: one line per help article. In Jinja2 syntax, the template looks like this:

One help article contains the text Settings > Export. We included it on purpose, to catch any tool that HTML-escapes > into >.

Every tool then ran the same sequence from a TypeScript script:

Each tool used its own documented TypeScript SDK. The four registries passed all six steps. The rest of this article covers how each one got there, and what it cost.

Keeping prompts in the repository costs almost nothing. The template sits in prompts/support-reply.njk, and nunjucks, the JavaScript port of Jinja2, renders it:

The autoescape: false line matters. Our first version called nunjucks.renderString() directly, and the test failed. The default environment HTML-escapes its output, so Settings > Export reached the model as Settings > Export. Escaping is correct for web pages and wrong for prompts.

Git gives you full Jinja2, code review on every prompt change, and history in the same place as the code. What it can't do is change a prompt in a running app. Every edit, and every rollback, waits for a deploy.

LangSmith stores a prompt as a serialized LangChain object (ChatPromptTemplate), not as plain text. LangSmith's own documentation says you must use the LangChain npm package to pull prompts in TypeScript (Manage prompts programmatically). So a plain TypeScript app that uses the openai package still needs langchain and @langchain/core:

Those three packages installed 23 packages and 70 MB. Most of the size is @langchain/* (27 MB) and js-tiktoken (22 MB).

Templates. In TypeScript, LangChain accepts f-string and mustache templates. Mustache sections ({{#docs}}…{{/docs}}) handled our loop. Mustache has no ==, though, so the premium check became an is_premium boolean computed in code. We also tried templateFormat: "jinja2", which LangChain's Python package supports. In TypeScript it threw DEFAULT_PARSER_MAPPING[templateFormat] is not a function.

Labels and rollback. production and staging are reserved tags, and the documentation moves them in the UI. The langsmith JavaScript client has no public method that moves a tag. Our script called the same REST route the SDK uses internally (POST /api/v1/repos/-//tags). It worked, but it is not a documented API.

Caching. The SDK caches prompts for 300 seconds by default. In our test, a running app saw the new version 300 seconds after we moved the tag.

Langfuse's TypeScript client is one package, @langfuse/client. It installed 5 packages and 8 MB:

Creating a version, moving a label and reading by label are all public SDK calls: prompt.create, prompt.update and prompt.get (Get started with prompt management).

Templates. The documentation shows only {{variable}} substitution. But compile() calls the mustache library, so sections worked for our loop. The type signature accepts only string values, so passing a list needed a cast. The Langfuse UI colours the section tags red, and it lists title and body as the prompt's variables, not docs or is_premium. We would not depend on behaviour the documentation doesn't promise. When we sent the Jinja2 version instead, Langfuse returned the {% if %} and {% for %} tags unchanged, with no error. The model would have received them as text.

Caching. The cache time is 60 seconds by default (Caching). Our app saw the change after 62 seconds. Langfuse is also the only client with a fallback option: a prompt string the SDK uses when it cannot reach the server on a cold start.

Phoenix's TypeScript client fetches a prompt by name and tag, and toSDK() converts it into the message format the openai package expects. Phoenix templates only replace variables, so the condition and the loop have to run in your code before rendering:

That is 16 lines, against 6 to 8 for the other registries. All the extra lines are template logic that now lives in the app.

Templates. Phoenix offers MUSTACHE, F_STRING and NONE formats (Prompts reference). Even in MUSTACHE mode, though, rendering is a plain replacement of {{name}}. When we sent mustache sections, they came back unchanged, with no error.

Errors. getPrompt() catches every error, logs it and returns null. You can see this in the client source, src/utils/getPromptBySelector.ts. A missing prompt and an unreachable server look the same to your code, which is why the snippet checks for null.

Size and caching. The client installed 57 packages and 90 MB. Most of that is OpenTelemetry, which comes in through its @arizeai/phoenix-otel dependency. The client has no prompt cache, so every render is a network call. That is also why a label change showed up immediately: 0 seconds in our test.

AcruxCore stores Jinja2 templates and renders them on the server with nunjucks. The SDK sends the variables and gets back finished messages:

The template from the test section works as it is, condition and loop included, with no is_premium flag computed in code. A label is called an alias here, and promoteAlias() moves it (Manage prompts via the SDK). The SDK installed 3 packages and 12 MB. Most of the 12 MB is one OpenTelemetry package, @opentelemetry/semantic-conventions.

Templates. Jinja2 only. When we committed the mustache version, AcruxCore rejected it at commit time with Template parse error: unexpected token: #. That is a clear error, but it also means that moving mustache prompts from LangSmith or Langfuse requires rewriting them.

We sent the same two templates, a Jinja2 one and a mustache one, to each tool's own renderer:

The rows that matter most are the ones where the tags were left in the output, because those failures produce no error. The model receives raw template syntax, and the only symptom is a strange answer. Whatever tool you pick, test one prompt with logic in it before you move the rest.

Putting logic in the template is not automatically better. It lets someone change the condition without a deploy. It also lets them break the condition without a deploy. Keeping logic in code, as Phoenix forces you to do, puts every branch under code review and in your tests.

A registry is a new runtime dependency, so we tested an outage. The app reached each registry through a local proxy, and we switched the proxy off. A separate process then started while the registry was still unreachable.

LangSmith, Langfuse and AcruxCore keep serving the last good version while the registry is down, even after the cache time has passed. The risk is a cold start: a new container or a serverless function that boots during the outage. Langfuse's fallback option is the built-in answer to that. With the other tools, keep a copy of each prompt in your code as a fallback, which brings back part of the Git setup.

Keep prompts in Git if engineers make every prompt change and your deploys are quick. Set autoescape: false, and you get Jinja2, reviews and history for free.

Use LangSmith's prompt hub if you already use LangChain, or already trace with LangSmith. If your app doesn't use LangChain, budget for the extra dependency and for the tag moves that only the UI supports.

Use Langfuse if you want a small client, a local compile(), and a fallback for cold starts. Keep to {{variable}} substitution unless you are comfortable relying on mustache behaviour that the documentation doesn't promise.

Use Phoenix if you already use it for tracing, or if you prefer all template logic in code. Remember the null check, and that every render is a network call.

Use AcruxCore if you want real conditions and loops in the template and rendering on the server. Expect one network call per new set of variables, and a personal API key for writes.

Before you choose, run one of your own prompts with real logic in it through the tool's SDK, not through its playground. Two of the four registries we tested failed on template syntax without any error, and an SDK run is where you would see that.

#10857. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

#10854. A tiny supercurrent could unlock quantum information without destroying it

One of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing is surprisingly simple to describe: how do you read information without changing or destroying it? Unlike the bits used in ordinary computers, quantum bits, or qubits, are extraordinarily sensitive to measurement. Trying to determine their state can cause that state to collapse, potentially erasing the very information scientists want to retrieve.

Researchers have now proposed a way to overcome much of this difficulty by using an unusual electrical signal produced by the qubit itself. Their approach focuses on a special type known as an "Andreev qubit," which can generate an oscillating electric supercurrent through its own quantum behavior. By monitoring this current, scientists may be able to determine the qubit's state without directly measuring it or significantly disturbing the information it contains.

The research, supported by FAPESP, was published in Physical Review B Letters. It was led by José Carlos Egues, a full professor at the São Carlos Institute of Physics at the University of São Paulo (IFSC-USP) in Brazil, and Yugui Yao of the School of Physics at the Beijing Institute of Technology in China. If experimentally confirmed, their method could simplify quantum computing by removing the need for extra qubits used in certain measurement techniques.

Why Measuring Quantum Information Is So Difficult

Conventional computers store information in bits that have one of two values, 0 or 1. These values can be checked repeatedly without changing the information. Quantum computers work differently because their qubits can exist in superpositions, meaning they can combine the states 0 and 1 in ways that are not possible with conventional bits.

This unusual ability is central to the potential power of quantum computing. However, it also creates a major vulnerability. Quantum states can be disrupted by interactions with their surroundings, including the instruments scientists use to examine them.

"In the conventional formulation of quantum mechanics, a projective measurement causes the system to assume one of the states associated with the measured quantity. Thus, if a qubit has been prepared in a certain superposition of 0 and 1, its direct measurement tends to destroy that superposition and, consequently, the information it contained," says Egues.

To minimize this problem, physicists developed techniques known as "quantum nondemolition measurements," or QND. Many of these approaches use an additional quantum system called an ancilla. Rather than measuring the original qubit directly, researchers transfer information to the ancilla and measure that auxiliary system instead.

The term "ancilla" comes from a Latin word meaning "slave" or "servant." In modern scientific usage, it describes something that performs a supporting function. Here, the ancilla acts as an intermediary, allowing researchers to obtain information while preserving the original qubit as much as possible.

Although effective, these procedures come with practical disadvantages. Scientists must prepare the auxiliary qubits, connect them to the qubits being studied, transfer information between them, and perform repeated measurements. These additional steps require time and equipment, and the complexity becomes increasingly significant as the number of qubits grows.

"Our strategy is different. Instead of transferring the information to an auxiliary qubit, we seek to obtain it from a signal produced by the qubit under study itself," says the researcher.

A Special Quantum Bit That Produces Its Own Signal

The proposed technique relies on the unusual properties of an Andreev qubit. Its name honors Soviet physicist Alexander Andreev (1939-2023), who identified a distinctive quantum process in 1964 that occurs where normal materials meet superconductors.

Superconductors are materials that can conduct electricity without electrical resistance under appropriate conditions. At the boundaries involving these materials, special quantum states known as "Andreev states" can form. In the system studied by the researchers, these states are confined to an extremely small region between two superconducting materials.

These quantum states can represent the basic values 0 and 1, along with superpositions of those values, making them suitable for storing quantum information.

"In the system under consideration, this region is a quantum dot - that is, a nanoscale structure in which electrons are spatially confined - formed in an indium arsenide [InAs] nanowire connected to superconducting materials on both sides. It's in this environment that the states characterizing the Andreev qubit are formed," Egues explains.

The key to the new approach is an electrical phenomenon called a "supercurrent." Unlike the current flowing through ordinary electrical conductors, a supercurrent moves without resistance.

The researchers found that when the Andreev qubit exists in a superposition, the supercurrent develops a component that oscillates over time. These fluctuations arise from quantum interference between the different states making up the superposition.

Crucially, the oscillations are not random. Their behavior contains information about the qubit's underlying quantum state, potentially allowing scientists to determine what that state is without directly measuring the qubit.

"It's precisely that property that we use to read the qubit. It's that supercurrent that lies at the heart of our proposal," the researcher points out.

How a Tiny Electrical Current Reveals Quantum Information

To understand how the technique works, it helps to consider how physicists describe a qubit's state mathematically.

One common representation is the "Bloch sphere," a geometric model in which every possible pure state of a single qubit corresponds to a point on the surface of a sphere. Two angles determine the location of that point.

The first angle describes how much each of the two basic states contributes to the superposition. The second describes their relative phase, which specifies how the quantum components are aligned with one another. Together, these two measurements provide the information needed to identify the qubit's state.

According to the new research, both pieces of information can be extracted from the supercurrent. Its steady component and the size of its oscillations reveal the relative contributions of the two basic states. Meanwhile, the timing of the oscillations provides information about their phase relationship.

"Our study showed that this information is encoded in the supercurrent itself. The supercurrent has a constant component and a fluctuating component. The value of the constant component and the amplitude of the oscillation reveal the proportion of each state (0 and 1) in the superposition, and the moment when that oscillation begins reveals the phase relationship between them. Thus, by measuring how the current varies over time, it's possible to reconstruct the initial state of the qubit," Egues explains.

This means the electrical current effectively carries a signature of the quantum information stored in the system. By observing how that signature changes over time, researchers could reconstruct the qubit's original state.

A Supercurrent That Oscillates Without Voltage

Another surprising feature of the proposed system is that the electrical current can oscillate without an externally applied voltage.

Ordinarily, producing an oscillating supercurrent in this type of superconducting system requires a voltage. This familiar phenomenon is called the "AC Josephson effect."

The researchers predict something different in their Andreev qubit. Here, the current's oscillations arise naturally from the qubit's own quantum evolution. No outside voltage is required to generate them.

"Our system generates a supercurrent that varies over time like an alternating current but without voltage. It's the very evolution of the qubit's state that produces that oscillation," says Egues.

This behavior is important because it transforms quantum activity occurring on an extraordinarily small scale into a detectable electrical signal. The current provides a connection between the microscopic world of quantum states and the larger physical systems scientists can measure.

That connection is what makes the proposed reading technique possible.

Detecting Quantum States With an Extremely Sensitive Sensor

The researchers envision placing the Andreev qubit inside a closed superconducting circuit, allowing its supercurrent to circulate continuously.

Next to the circuit would sit a SQUID (superconducting quantum interference device), a highly sensitive instrument capable of detecting extremely weak magnetic fields.

Whenever an electric current flows, it produces a magnetic field. Because the supercurrent in this system oscillates, the magnetic field it generates also changes over time.

The nearby SQUID could detect these tiny magnetic variations and use them to determine the behavior of the circulating current. Since the current contains information about the qubit's quantum state, the researchers could then reconstruct that state without measuring the qubit directly.

Egues compares the arrangement to two compasses that interact through a magnetic field.

"We can think of the qubit as one compass and the SQUID as another compass. The current in the circuit produces a magnetic field, and the SQUID 'senses' that field. Since the coupling between the two is very weak, the effect of the measuring instrument on the qubit tends to be very small."

The weak interaction between the sensor and the qubit is especially important. It offers a way to gather information while minimizing the disturbance that normally accompanies quantum measurements.

Reading Quantum Information With Almost No Disturbance

In quantum physics, observing a system is not necessarily a harmless process. A measuring instrument interacts with whatever it is attempting to measure, and that interaction can change the system's behavior.

This fundamental difficulty puzzled the earliest researchers studying quantum mechanics. It remains an unavoidable feature of quantum measurements today.

The proposed technique does not eliminate this effect entirely. The SQUID still interacts with the circuit, creating a small amount of feedback that can influence the qubit. However, the team's simulations suggest that this disturbance can be reduced to a level so small that it becomes effectively negligible.

"Our method doesn't eliminate the fundamental problem of quantum measurement. But it can approach the limit of a non-destructive readout and, at the same time, avoid the use of auxiliary qubits - the ancillas - employed in other non-destructive quantum measurement protocols," Egues notes.

The approach could also remove another complication found in some existing techniques. Because researchers would not need to restore the qubit to its original state after every measurement, they could make repeated observations as the state changes.

This would allow scientists to follow the qubit's quantum evolution over time, rather than obtaining information only through separate measurement and reset cycles.

Simulations Achieve Up to 99.85% Accuracy

To investigate whether the method could work outside ideal theoretical conditions, the researchers performed computer simulations designed to account for imperfections that would arise in real experiments.

These calculations included measurement noise, as well as the small amount of feedback introduced by the detection coil. The goal was to determine whether the qubit's state could still be reconstructed accurately despite these disturbances.

The results were encouraging. Depending on the number of measurements used, the simulations produced accuracies of 99.52%, 99.81%, and 99.85%.

According to the researchers, these values fall within the standard thresholds used in quantum error correction schemes, which are designed to manage errors in quantum information processing.

The findings suggest that the method could provide highly accurate quantum state reconstruction without the additional complexity of auxiliary qubits.

However, the technique has not yet been demonstrated experimentally. The results come entirely from theoretical analysis and computer simulations, so researchers still need to establish whether a physical device can achieve comparable performance.

Could This Make Quantum Computers Simpler?

Despite the lack of experimental confirmation, the researchers emphasize that their proposal does not depend on technologies that have yet to be invented.

The calculations use realistic properties drawn from existing quantum systems, and SQUID sensors are already established tools for detecting exceptionally weak magnetic fields and electric currents.

What remains to be built is the specific arrangement of components required to implement the proposed measurement technique.

"All the numbers we used are realistic. In principle, this can be achieved. In fact, we used parameters from existing systems in our calculations. What doesn't yet exist is that geometry, that setup that we proposed," he argues.

This makes the approach different from theoretical concepts that cannot be realized until scientists develop entirely new materials or manufacturing techniques. In principle, the necessary components already exist, although assembling them into the proposed configuration and demonstrating the predicted behavior remain important challenges.

The research offers two potentially significant advances.

From the standpoint of fundamental physics, it predicts that a qubit can produce an oscillating supercurrent resembling the AC Josephson effect, but without requiring an external voltage. Instead, the oscillations emerge from the qubit's own coherent quantum evolution.

From a technological perspective, the study shows how those oscillations could serve as a measurable signature of the qubit's internal state. By analyzing the electrical current, researchers could recover quantum information while causing very little disturbance to the system.

If future experiments confirm these predictions, the method could make reading Andreev qubits substantially simpler. Eliminating auxiliary qubits and repeated resets could reduce the equipment and procedures needed to retrieve quantum information.

More fundamentally, the work points toward an intriguing possibility: information hidden in the microscopic quantum world may be accessible through an electrical current produced by the quantum system itself, offering a promising new way to observe delicate quantum states without destroying them.

#10851. Networking is awkward: Marriott and Second City want to improve your conference experience with improv

Corporate networking hours can be painfully awkward. Picture it: dozens of professionals clutching lukewarm drinks, trading similar elevator pitches, and desperately scanning badges to find someone worth talking to.

“You have a name tag, a drink in your hand, and no guidance,” Tyler Dean Kempf, creative director of Second City Works, told Fast Company. “It’s up to you to engage people in conversations that are usually focused entirely on work.”

Marriott International thinks it has a fix—and it involves pulling a move straight out of the comedy playbook.

On Tuesday, the hotel giant announced Unscripted Networking: The Second City Takeover, a new program developed exclusively with Chicago’s 65-year-old improv comedy institution, the Second City.

The program is rolling out across more than 125 properties in Marriott Bonvoy’s Convention & Resort Network throughout North America and the Caribbean. It offers a 45- to 60-minute experience that swaps traditional small talk for structured, improv-inspired group play.

A different kind of workplace networking event

Rather than putting individuals on the spot for solo stand-up bits, the program pairs two professional Second City hosts with a live music director who formats the session like an interactive variety show.

The events are designed for groups of 50 to 750 attendees; the hosts lead rapid-fire transitions through low-stakes exercises by asking participants to debate topics like coffee versus tea, or window versus aisle seats, rather than their resume highlights.

In one drill, Kempf noted, attendees act as “corporate wingmen,” learning about a partner before introducing them to another pair in the room to expand their circle.

Kempf emphasized that while pitching improv to corporate professionals might send introverts running for the exit, the structure explicitly removes the fear of performance.

“When we say the word improv, people get scared because our minds go to being vulnerable, having no script, or performing on stage,” Kempf said. “There’s no performance in this. Our hosts demonstrate vulnerability first from the stage, which signals that it’s safe for people to drop their guard down on the floor.”

Let’s get together

Amanda Cox, vice president of Sales, Convention & Resort Hotels, East Region for Marriott International, said as hybrid work environments reduce daily office interactions, live events are bearing an unprecedented burden.

“In recent years, there’s less gathering around the proverbial water cooler,” Cox said. “Live meetings and events are being looked upon to be torchbearers of an organization’s culture—and that’s a lot of pressure on event planners.”

That pressure is compounded by the challenge of bringing together a multi-generational workforce with different communication norms.

Cox noted that in customer insight sessions with event organizers, Marriott found planners struggling to get younger hires and veteran executives on the same page. 

“It is amazing the barriers that break down when you’re immediately in a place of creative collaboration,” Cox said.

Yes, and…

Marriott ran nine internal engagements with Second City over the past year to test the concept before scaling it across its resort portfolio.

Cox said that incorporating the program at the opening reception of a typical three-day conference can result in deeper engagement throughout the rest of the event.

“When you start with that genuine connection, you see the return on investment grow throughout the entire meeting,” Cox said. “You’re getting deeper much quicker because you’ve stepped away from small talk into substantial connection.”

By bringing comedy-backed behavioral science into the ballroom, Marriott and Second City are making a clear bet: The fastest way to get corporate professionals to drop their guard isn’t another open bar—it’s getting them to play along.

#10849. Are school phone bans working so far? Here’s what early studies show

The argument for school cellphone bans is simple: Phones offer endless distraction. Take them away and students can focus on classwork, engage with peers and, ultimately, learn more.

But the reality is more complicated, as emerging research shows. Schools can lock phones away in pouches or banish them to backpacks, but they can’t shut out the digital world.

In the past few years, more than 40 U.S. states have passed laws that restrict cellphones at schools, according to the Phone Free Schools State Report Card, which tracks state phone legislation. But those laws vary widely and often let schools design their own policies, resulting in a patchwork of rules and enforcement across the country.

A large-scale study published Tuesday found strict cellphone restrictions — ones that require students to put phones in locked pouches for the entire school day — result in small academic gains. But, the researchers said, the gains can add up if schools continue banning phones.

Phone bans appear to help, to a point

The study, led by Harvard’s Center for Education Policy Research, found the schools with strict phone restrictions saw “modest” improvements in academic growth, compared with students in similar schools without those restrictions. The study, which analyzed test scores of 3.3 million students at 4,600 schools nationally, estimates the academic gains were equivalent to about an additional week or two of learning over a school year.

Student achievement has been falling globally since 2013, which is roughly when social media-enabled smartphones took off. The researchers sought to determine if the decline is being reversed now that schools have started taking phones away, said Harvard professor Thomas Kane, one of the study’s authors.

“Our answer is yes, phone bans do have a positive effect on growth. But it’s not a panacea,” Kane said. Learning loss was gradual over a decade, he notes, and it’s not surprising that gains would also be gradual. The study used math and reading test data for grades four through 10 from the past five academic years.

“While schools should not expect large boosts in achievement immediately, storing students’ phones seems to be a cost-effective way to add 1-2 weeks of instruction each year, which could add up over time, just as the initial declines did,” the study says.

The boost is equal to about half the annual decline in test scores since smartphones became mainstream, the study said.

Academic gains are overall ‘disappointing,’ one researcher says

Still, the connection between phones and learning is not clear-cut.

A different study published in May concluded strict school phone bans had “close to zero” effect on test scores. It compared schools with bans with tens of thousands of other schools.

The National Bureau of Economic Research study looked at a different set of standardized test scores than Harvard’s study, as well as GPS phone activity, teacher surveys and data from Yondr, the leading provider of lockable phone pouches, at 4,600 schools that use the pouches.

The study had found “modest positive effects” in high school test scores over three years, particularly in math. But the report’s authors, researchers at Stanford, the University of Michigan, Duke and the University of Pennsylvania, were hesitant to conclude overall learning benefits from the bans.

Both studies point to a similar conclusion, said Stanford education economist Thomas Dee, an author of the NBER study. “The early results from school phone bans are largely disappointing and raise unresolved questions,” Dee said.

Noting that the Harvard study showed learning gains may accumulate over time, Dee said: “I hope they’re right. However, I’m not comfortable making that extrapolation from estimates that are small, sensitive and based on just a few years of phone bans.”

The reasons, Dee said, could be that students are still distracted on other devices at school. Or, he said, “it may be that we see these disappointing results because out-of-school phone use is harming child development.”

Phones aren’t the only tech distracting teens

Just because schools or states have phone bans does not mean they’re being enforced, said Jean Twenge, a psychologist at San Diego State University and author of “10 Rules for Raising Kids in a High-Tech World.”

Ask any teacher — or teenager — and they’ll tell you that some phone bans are largely cosmetic. Teens come up with creative workarounds, including stowing burner phones or even phone-shaped rocks in their locked pouches. At schools that rely on students to keep phones in backpacks, adherence to the ban can be spotty.

“It happens pretty often that there’s a policy of no phones during the school day, bell-to-bell, and you go into the school and phones are everywhere,” Twenge said.

Even without phones, “Do they have laptops at school? Are they just doing social media on laptops instead?” Twenge said.

Phone use outside of school affects academics too, Twenge said. “If they have that phone available when they’re studying, how much are they really taking in?”

Kane, of the Harvard study, agrees the effect of cellphone use outside of school is “the big question.” But researchers have not been able to investigate parents and educators’ suspicions that late-night binges on YouTube and other platforms are costly for students.

“I find it astonishing that social media companies know how much time kids spend on their platforms, but that data has never been linked to student outcomes,” Kane said. “We can’t provide answers until the social media companies share data.”

The Associated Press’ education coverage receives financial support from multiple private foundations. AP is solely responsible for all content. Find AP’s standards for working with philanthropies, a list of supporters and funded coverage areas at AP.org.

—Jocelyn Gecker, AP Education Writer

#10848. Inside this quantum computer, memory vibrates like a guitar string

A quantum computer might seem to have little in common with a guitar, but researchers at ETH Zurich have found a surprising connection. Physicist Yiwen Chu and her team have developed a system that uses microscopic mechanical vibrations to store and manipulate quantum information, much like the vibrating strings of a guitar produce musical notes.

Unlike the sounds produced by a musical instrument, these vibrations occur at frequencies far beyond human hearing. They take place within a tiny quantum chip, where they serve as a new form of computer memory.

By harnessing these vibrations, the researchers have created a way for quantum computers to process information efficiently while accessing a flexible working memory. "The interaction between the quantum processor and the quantum memory provides a crucial foundation with a view to establishing quantum computers as a powerful and reliable way to perform computations that are not feasible with conventional computers," says Yiwen Chu.

The breakthrough, described in the journal Science, introduces a different approach to quantum computer design. Instead of closely integrating information processing and storage, as many existing systems do, Chu's team has developed an architecture that gives these functions more distinct roles.

Quantum Memory Inspired by Conventional Computers

The researchers drew inspiration from the basic design of everyday digital computers. In a conventional machine, the central processing unit (CPU) carries out calculations while information is held separately in working memory, commonly called random access memory (RAM). The arrangement of these components plays an important role in determining how efficiently a computer operates.

Chu's quantum system follows a similar principle. A superconducting qubit acts as the processing and control unit, performing a role comparable to that of the central processing unit (CPU). Meanwhile, a separate quantum memory temporarily holds the information needed during calculations, allowing the processor to retrieve and modify it as necessary.

The major difference lies in how that information is stored.

"In our quantum working memory, however, information is not stored electromagnetically -- as is usually the case today -- but rather in the form of mechanical vibrations," explains Chu.

Quantum computers take advantage of unusual properties of quantum physics, including superposition, which allows quantum systems to exist in combinations of different states, and entanglement, which creates powerful connections between quantum states. These effects could eventually enable quantum machines to tackle certain extremely complicated calculations much faster than conventional computers, including some problems that are currently beyond their practical capabilities.

Why Vibrating Quantum Memory Could Be Better

One of the greatest challenges in quantum computing is maintaining precise control over quantum information. The states used for calculations are delicate, and even small disturbances can cause information to be lost. To perform calculations reliably, the processor and memory must interact closely enough to transfer and manipulate quantum states with high precision.

In the new system, microscopic structures called mechanical resonators hold information in particular patterns of vibration. When the superconducting qubit needs that information, it accesses the stored vibrational state, performs the required operation, and returns the modified information to memory.

Many existing quantum computers instead use electromagnetic memory alongside superconducting qubits. Researchers understand these technologies well, and they can be combined to provide extremely accurate control over quantum states, including reading, changing, and manipulating stored information.

However, electromagnetic memory systems have a significant disadvantage. They can occupy considerable space, making it difficult to expand experimental quantum devices into the larger, more capable computers that researchers and industries ultimately need.

Mechanical resonators offer several potential advantages. They are substantially smaller, allowing more compact designs. They can also support numerous different patterns of vibration, providing opportunities to store more information simultaneously than conventional electromagnetic memory.

Another important benefit is that mechanical vibrations can preserve quantum states for longer periods before the stored information begins to fade. Longer storage times could make it easier to carry out complicated calculations that require information to remain available throughout multiple processing steps.

The research published in Science provides the first experimental demonstration that mechanical resonators can work together with superconducting qubits to carry out quantum computations. The results establish that vibration-based memory is a feasible alternative to electromagnetic storage.

The achievement goes beyond simply storing information. Chu's team demonstrated that its architecture can handle both relatively straightforward calculations and more demanding quantum operations.

To evaluate the system, the researchers tested two important computational techniques: the quantum Fourier transform and period finding. Both are fundamental tools in quantum computing and require a computer to coordinate multiple quantum states with considerable precision.

"The Quantum Fourier Transform is a fundamental computational procedure required for many quantum algorithms. The period-finding algorithm we implemented served as a demonstration of how this procedure can be used," explains Igor Kladaric, doctoral student in Chu's team and co-author of the publication.

Successfully completing these tasks requires the system to store, control, and maintain coherent relationships among multiple quantum states at the same time. The experiments showed that the new architecture can meet these requirements, providing strong evidence that it can support genuine quantum computation rather than simply function as a memory device.

A Promising Step Toward Powerful Quantum Computers

One of the most significant findings is that the new system can, in principle, carry out all the fundamental operations needed for arbitrary quantum computations. This means the architecture has the essential capabilities required for a programmable, general-purpose quantum computer.

However, demonstrating those capabilities in an experimental chip is only the beginning. The next major challenge is scaling the technology to larger systems with greater processing power while preserving the precision and reliability demonstrated in the laboratory.

Chu and her colleagues are continuing to investigate how their approach can be developed further. Its long-term success will depend on whether mechanical quantum memory can remain effective as more components and increasingly complex computations are added.

Practical quantum computers powerful enough for widespread scientific and industrial applications are still some distance away. Nevertheless, by showing that tiny mechanical vibrations can serve as compact, capable quantum memory, the ETH Zurich team has demonstrated a promising new route toward making those machines a reality.

Materials provided by ETH Zurich. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10847. Here’s how high achievers self-sabotage their goals

We’re getting close to the end of the year, and if you’re an ambitious individual, you might have your new year’s goals on your mind. You might be thinking about what goals you want to commit to for the year ahead, and assessing your progress toward the goals that you set for yourself at the beginning of the year.

And if you find yourself not making as much progress as you would have liked, it might be tempting to give up on the goal altogether. After all, there isn’t that long to go until January 1. What’s the point of continuing to work on it if you’re not sure whether you can complete it 100% by the end of the year? 

If you find yourself with these kinds of thoughts in your head, you might just be suffering from a mindset that is preventing you from making major progress on your goals. 

Where “all-or-nothing” thinking comes from 

That mindset is an “all-or-nothing” approach—where you view situations and circumstances in absolute terms, with no nuance or middle ground. An all-or-nothing mindset can manifest in different ways. In goal-setting, it can look like reviewing your week, month, or year, and looking back at the times you faltered or struggled and telling yourself some versions of “I did terrible this week” or “I blew it,” says Allison Niebes-Davis, a licensed psychologist who runs a practice in Downers Grove, Illinois.  

Niebes-Davis explains that our brains are constantly making meaning of the things around us. It can do so quickly and efficiently as a survival tool. “The problem is that when the brain is doing that, it doesn’t always do that accurately. And so sometimes when it is trying to make meaning and draw conclusions, it doesn’t always draw the most accurate conclusions or the most nuanced conclusions,” she says. 

Many high-achievers might struggle with this mindset, Niebes-Davis explains, because they can have a dichotomous way of seeing themselves. “Sometimes, high-achievers can be very self-critical, and so they use a lot of words like failure, success, win, and lose.” Because of this, they “think that they will motivate themselves by seeing things in that all-or-nothing mindset,” she continues. 

How the mindset sabotages your goals

For high-achievers, this mindset might have worked in the past, or even in other areas of their lives. This is true for Erica Dhawan, author, keynote speaker, and advisor to CEOs and leaders. Dhawan has ticked off many goals she set for herself this year—from family to keynote speeches. But she found herself struggling with her weight goals. She noticed an all-or-nothing approach creeping in when she had growth and setbacks. “I do get into a wave of good health, but then when I lose it, I don’t fall back on progress. I’m known as an ambitious, high-achieving person who gets a lot done. I also wonder if all-or-nothing is perfectionism, where I do achieve the perfect goal in certain areas, and I don’t in others,” Dhawan reflects. 

Niebes-Davis stresses that while the all-or-nothing mindset can push some people toward success, it’s unlikely to be an approach that is healthy and sustainable in the long run. “Our thoughts and emotions are so directly connected,” she says. The path to achieving a goal is “rarely linear,” says Niebes-Davis, and “nobody’s getting 100%, 100% of the time.” If the only way you can describe something is success and failure, she says, you’ll end up seeing struggles and setbacks as failures. 

“That doesn’t motivate anyone to keep working or to keep trying,” she continues.  

The importance of language 

Both Niebes-Davis and Dhawan say that culture can also encourage this type of thinking. Social media, says Dhawan, gives us “picture perfect” images that can condition us into thinking anything less isn’t good enough. This has also created a culture where “we struggle so much in our current day with nuance. Things are either/or. They’re in extremes. We don’t teach kids from a young age about how to have more nuanced language.”

Niebes-Davis says that rather than the overly rigid language we often use when we fall short, it’s more accurate to say things like “I struggled around these three things. This month was hard. I made some choices that were not in line with my goals.”

A better approach  

Adopting this kind of language in your goal-setting approach, says Niebes-Davis, shifts your mindset toward prioritizing progress rather than an arbitrary outcome. She gives the example of someone who, like Dhawan, has a goal to be at a specific weight. Say that person’s goal is to lose ten pounds. “If you’re only going to give yourself credit or positive reinforcement when you lose ten pounds,” she says, “what happens at one pound? Two pounds? Three pounds?”

Being able to celebrate smaller progress, says Niebes-Davis, gives you the option to “look at things from a data-driven mindset.” That means tracking smaller things and paying attention to what moves the needle. That way, if you find yourself having a week where you ended up gaining a pound, you’re more likely to assess the situation accurately and, in turn, make the necessary changes. “Maybe you made some choices that weren’t super in line with your goals,” she says. You can then course-correct and get back on track rather than giving up on your goals altogether. 

Focusing on process, habits and progress 

Niebes-Davis also recommends that individuals focus on process rather than outcomes. Rather than just tracking outcomes, she recommends people ask themselves, “What are the processes that would contribute to movement around that goal?” She acknowledges that this is harder to do, and is something that we don’t see often. “When we look at people and their progress toward their goals, we tend to really see and celebrate that outcome-based approach.” 

This is a practice that Dhawan is committing to. Rather than giving up on her all-or-nothing weight goal, she’s committed to practicing healthy habits until the end of the year. That might mean eliminating alcohol and sugar, writing for 30 minutes, or meditating every morning. 

Moving away from the all-or-nothing mindset, says Niebes-Davis, is ultimately about training ourselves as humans. “We tend to give greater attention to the things that we struggle with, or the times we didn’t align with our goals. So a lot of being able to move the needle is to be very intentional about recognizing the successes.” 

She continues, “That takes work, and that takes intention, but that is going to put us in a much healthier place for goals, relationships, health and all of that.”

#10845. Technical Aspects of AI-Generated Video Assets for Web Design

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated videos are becoming an important part of modern web design. Designers can now create animated backgrounds, product demonstrations, hero section videos, and visual storytelling elements without relying entirely on traditional video production. However, generating a visually appealing video is only one part of the process. To use AI-generated videos effectively on a website, developers and designers must also understand video formats, visual quality, file optimization, and website performance.

These technical factors directly influence how videos appear across devices, how quickly pages load, and how smoothly visitors interact with a website.

Video format selection is one of the first technical decisions when preparing AI-generated assets for the web. Different formats offer different levels of compression, compatibility, and visual quality.

For most websites, MP4 with H.264 remains a dependable default. WebM or AV1 can be offered as additional formats where appropriate.

Developers should also consider browser compatibility before selecting a format. Providing multiple sources allows browsers to choose a supported option.

AI-generated videos can sometimes contain visual inconsistencies, unnatural movements, distorted objects, or changes in lighting between frames. These issues become more noticeable when videos are displayed in large website sections.

Resolution is an important factor in maintaining visual quality. Common resolutions include:

However, higher resolution does not automatically mean better website performance or user experience. A 4K video may look impressive, but loading it unnecessarily can increase bandwidth consumption.

Frame rate also affects the viewing experience. Many website videos work well at 24 or 30 frames per second. Higher frame rates may be useful for fast-moving scenes, but they also increase the amount of visual data that needs to be processed.

Designers should review AI-generated footage before publishing it. Checking transitions, motion consistency, sharpness, and color accuracy helps prevent distracting visual problems.

Large video files can significantly increase website loading times. This is especially important for websites with video backgrounds, animated landing pages, and multiple visual sections.

Video optimization involves reducing file size without making the footage noticeably worse.

Some practical optimization techniques include:

Compression: Video compression reduces unnecessary data while preserving important visual details. Developers can use encoding tools such as FFmpeg to adjust bitrate, resolution, and codec settings.

Bitrate control: Bitrate determines how much data is used to represent each second of video. A higher bitrate generally preserves more detail but increases file size. Selecting an appropriate bitrate depends on the video's resolution, movement, and intended display size.

Trimming unnecessary footage: Website videos rarely need to be several minutes long. Removing unused sections helps reduce file size and keeps visual content focused.

Removing unnecessary audio: Background videos that do not require sound should be exported without an audio track. This reduces unnecessary data and supports a cleaner user experience.

Responsive video delivery: Websites should serve appropriately sized assets for different screen sizes. A smaller video may be sufficient for mobile users, while desktop visitors can receive a higher-resolution version.

These techniques help developers balance visual quality with efficient content delivery.

Even a well-optimized video can affect website performance if it is implemented incorrectly. Developers should consider how videos are loaded, displayed, and processed by browsers.

For example, automatically loading several large videos when a page opens can consume significant bandwidth. Instead, developers can use lazy loading strategies for videos that appear further down the page.

For background videos, the HTML video element offers several useful attributes:

Developers should also avoid autoplaying large videos unnecessarily. A static image can often provide a better experience on mobile devices or slower connections.

Performance testing tools such as Google Lighthouse and Chrome DevTools can help identify whether video assets are affecting loading speed, bandwidth usage, or rendering performance.

Technical optimization should not come at the expense of accessibility. Background videos should not distract users or make website content difficult to understand.

Developers should respect the prefers-reduced-motion media query, which allows users who prefer reduced animation to receive a less motion-intensive experience.

A suitable static background image can be displayed as an alternative.

Mobile compatibility is equally important. Smaller screens, limited data plans, and varying network conditions can make heavy video assets less practical. Testing videos on different devices and connection speeds helps identify potential problems before publication.

AI-generated video assets offer exciting possibilities for modern web design, but their successful implementation requires more than creative output. Choosing suitable video formats, maintaining consistent visual quality, compressing files, and considering website performance are all essential parts of the development process.

By combining thoughtful video production with responsive delivery, accessibility considerations, and performance testing, designers and developers can create engaging websites without compromising loading speed or usability. As AI video technology continues to evolve, understanding these technical fundamentals will remain valuable for building efficient and visually appealing web experiences.
