#10843. Shopping for a tween? These gifts won’t get an eye roll

Buying a gift for a tween is a risky endeavor. Nail it, and you’re a legend. Miss, and you’ll get an eyeroll.

Tweens live in a tricky in-between zone. They’ve outgrown the kids’ section, but they’re not ready for the adult aisle. For the last decade, brands weren’t much help, as magazines and clothing labels targeted to this age group went out of business.

But this holiday season, tweens are back in the spotlight. A wave of startups is making clothes and skincare for kids ages 7 to 14. Claire’s is reinventing itself. Print magazines are making a comeback. Even the phone is being reimagined for them.

For this year’s guide, I went looking for gifts that hit the sweet spot. These products are mature enough to feel like a step forward without pushing kids to grow up before they’re ready. Many of them also lure kids off their screens and back into the real world.

Each of these gifts had to win over both the tween who unwraps it and the parent who pays for it.

Print is back

Anyway, Annual Magazine Subscription, $40

If you grew up taking quizzes in YM to find out which Spice Girl you were, you already understand the magic of the tween magazine. Anyway is a new print magazine for kids ages 9 to 15. It covers the awkward issues that can feel mortifying to ask about (crushes, body odor), along with style and culture. Every issue comes with a sticker sheet, a comic, and other screen-free activities.

A suitcase for jet setters in training

Away, The Carry-On in Misty Purple, $278; Featherlight Daily Backpack in Misty Purple, $98

Away built its suitcases for millennial travelers. Now those millennials have tween children, and those tweens want luggage of their own. This summer, Away launched In Between, a collection designed for this age group. The Carry-On is the brand’s classic hard-shell suitcase but sized slightly smaller than its adult version, and slightly bigger than its kids’ product. It even has room for a monogram of up to three letters. You can also get a matching backpack that slides right over the suitcase handle; it has a laptop sleeve, a water bottle pocket, and a D-ring for bag charms.

Her first fragrance

Evereden, Hair and Body Fragrance Mist Trio, $72

The first signature scent is a rite of passage. (I admit, I doused myself in Anna Sui’s Sui Dreams before the school dance.) Evereden’s trio of hair and body mists is formulated for kids and teens ages 6 and up, without phthalates, parabens, or common fragrance allergens. The scents have great names—Main Character, Darling, and Supernova—or whoever your tween wants to be that day.

GRWM: Holiday edition

Bubble, Main Attraction Hydrating Super Set, $70

After hordes of tweens took over Sephora, parents became wary of their kids’ slathering on harsh anti-aging serums meant for women decades older. So it’s comforting that Bubble designs skincare products specifically for the needs of young skin, namely moisture. The Main Attraction set packs six of the brand’s favorites into a festive travel bag, including Soft Launch cream cleanser, Cosmic Silk milky toner, Water Slide serum, Power Wave moisturizer, and Talk Back lip serum. The formulas use gentle, hydrating ingredients like hyaluronic acid and shea butter.

The hallway status symbol, upgraded

Lululemon, Everywhere Belt Bag with Long Strap 1.3L, $44

If middle school has an official uniform, the Lululemon belt bag is part of it. This year, the brand gave the original a makeover. The strap now detaches with hidden snaps, and the main zipper clips into a little paracord loop as an extra safety feature. It’s big enough for a phone, lip balm, and emergency snacks, and comes in an array of colors—like lavender and fuchsia—that tweens will love.

Screen-free relaxation

Lifelines, FlowArt Dot-By-Letter, Landscapes, $12.99

Between homework, after-school activities, and friend drama, tween life can be overwhelming. With FlowArt, kids can decompress without a screen. It works by allowing them to create landscapes using tiny dots. Each pad has 20 scenes and comes with four double-sided markers in eight colors, along with a tray to keep them in, so they don’t get lost in a bag. At under $15, it’s a perfect stocking stuffer.

A first phone parents will approve of

Freckle, $199, plus $25 per month for service

If you’re looking to give your kid independence but are wary of giving them a smartphone, you’re in luck. Freckle, which I wrote about this fall, is a phone for kids 7 to 13 that skips the browser, the app store, social media, and YouTube. Instead, it has a camera that identifies bugs, birds, and plants and saves them to a digital sticker book. Kids can call and text approved contacts, and two Freckles tapped together turn into walkie-talkies. There’s even a tap-to-pay wallet for pocket money. The first batch ships in December, so preorder soon if you want one under the tree.

A Monthly Build Box

CrunchLabs, Build Box Subscription, $329.40 per year

There’s a good chance your tween already worships Mark Rober, the former NASA engineer whose YouTube experiments include glitter bombs for package thieves and obstacle courses for squirrels. He’s launched a subscription box called CrunchLabs with a new build-it-yourself toy that arrives every month. Past builds include a coin-snatching machine, a disc launcher, and a drawing robot, and each is linked to a 15- to 30-minute video of Rober explaining the engineering behind it. The kits are designed so kids 8 to 13 can finish them on their own in an afternoon, and then spend hours playing with what they made. It’s also available month to month for $29.95 a box. Kids 14 and up can graduate to the Hack Pack, which adds coding and robotics.

Make Your Legos Move

LEGO Education, Computer Science & AI Kit, $429.95 (grades 3–5) or $529.95 (grades 6–8)

LEGO’s new kit allows kids to build machines, and in doing so, teach themselves the basics of robotics and AI. The kits come with the LEGO bricks your child has played with their whole life, but thanks to motors and sensors, they can create machines and robots that can move. The system is designed to teach coding basics in a fun way. LEGO is careful about keeping data private. No data is transferred to the company, kids don’t have log-ins of their own, and all projects are saved locally. The grades 3–5 kit suits younger tweens, and the grades 6–8 kit adds an extra motor and a controller for kids 11 and up.

The Claire’s comeback collection

Claire’s, Disney Villains Earring Stack, $29.99, and Faux Gel Nails, $19.99

Claire’s, the last survivor of the ’90s mall, is staging a comeback, with new owners and redesigned stores. Its exclusive Disney Villains collection of accessories and cosmetics features Ursula, Maleficent, the Evil Queen, or Cruella De Vil in matching earring stacks, fragrances, and faux gel nails. Tweens gravitate towards villains because they are still part of the Disney world, but they are too cool to feel babyish. If you want to make it an experience, you can pair the earrings with a trip to Claire’s for a new piercing.

Beautiful books

Juniper Books, Harry Potter Mashup Book Set, $424.99

The bookshelf is the new status symbol, and this set is a showstopper. Juniper Books wraps all seven Harry Potter hardcover editions in custom jackets that serve as decor in a tween’s room. On a shelf, the spines form a single design that blends the colors and symbols of each of the four Hogwarts houses. It’s definitely a splurge, but it is likely to be on the shelf long after they’ve outgrown many of the items on this list.

A party dress

Pleat, Ruby Dress, $72

Sara Fowler, founder of Pleat, started the brand because she couldn’t find anything for tween girls between the smocked dress and the crop top. The Ruby Dress is one of Pleat’s most popular styles. Soft pleats gather at the neckline above a bloused top, and a fitted waist gives just a hint of shape without hugging the body. It comes in a silky fabric in teal, dark green, red, and a Forget Me Nots print, in sizes 7 to 16. It’s festive enough for a holiday concert, family dinner, and New Year’s Eve.

The sleepover flex

Buttah London, The Button Me Up Lounge Set, £99 (about $130)

It’s sleepover season, and tweens are often stressed about what to pack when they’re invited to a friend’s house. London-based Buttah has created a just-right lounge set for the occasion. It features a long-sleeve top with nice details like ruching and heart embroidery. It’s cropped, but it lands right at the waistband, so there’s no midriff showing, which parents will appreciate. It pairs with wide-leg pants that have a contrasting bow.

#10841. This is the wrong reason to claim Social Security benefits early

YouTube creator Erin Moriarity recently appeared on Morningstar’s The Long View podcast. She spoke with podcast hosts Christine Benz and Amy Arnott about why she feels positive about the future of Social Security benefits; her advice for young people planning for retirement; and Social Security mistakes to watch out for.

You like to push back on concerns about the future of Social Security. Can you explain why you think that people who are, say, over 50 should feel some comfort that their promised benefits will be there for them?

MORIARITY: People over 50 vote, and those in Congress know it. If they upset that population, it’s not going to go well for them. They’re going to lose their job. I think Congress has a huge motivation to act. I do think Social Security will experience reform and will see changes. I don’t necessarily know what those changes would be, whether it’s reducing cost-of-living adjustments going forward, increasing the wage cap. I mean, there might be the potential that they try to borrow in order to fund the trust fund, which we’ve never done before. They would have to rewrite the laws, but there’s going to be a way in which they keep Social Security going forward. And I think people over 50 don’t have to worry about changes to their benefits. I think people in my generation, 30s, I think benefits will change, but benefits will be there.

The narrative is often that Social Security is going bankrupt, and that’s not true. There are current workers today who are paying into the system, and that pays out to the current retirees today. It would still be able to pay out what’s coming in, which currently is about 77% of what the payouts would be, but I don’t think benefits would be cut for near-term or current retirees.

As a younger person trying to plan for retirement, would you assume that future Social Security benefits might be reduced, or how would you handle those potential changes?

MORIARITY: If I’m talking to somebody who’s in their 20s, 30s, or even their early 40s, I don’t even want them considering Social Security at that point. Not that I don’t think it’s going to be there; I think it will be, but I want you saving and investing in a way where you’re not relying on it because I’d rather you get to retirement and say, oh, I have more than enough, or I can potentially retire early, or I can take extra vacations that I didn’t think I was going to. It’s a better position to get to retirement and have more money rather than less.

I think once you get to your late 40s and 50s, and especially in your 60s, I mean, you’re already at Social Security age—I would look at what those benefits are. I’d build my budget, figure out what my gap is between what Social Security is and how much I want to spend. But prior to 45 or something, save and invest as if you’re kind of blind to what those Social Security benefits would be.

What are the big Social Security pitfalls or mistakes that people should be aware of?

MORIARITY: I think one of the biggest is one we already touched on: Thinking that the trust fund is going to be totally depleted, meaning that there wouldn’t be benefits for you or that your benefits would be cut. I hear so many people right now saying, “Claim as early as you can so you can get something while it’s there.” And I just think that that’s a fearful mentality. The more we operate out of fear, the worse decisions we tend to make. I think if I look back over the course of my life, I have not made great decisions if I made them out of fear.

I would bring it back to what my budget looks like, what my portfolio looks like, and really, how much security do I need? There’s great value in having a reliable income stream that is inflation-adjusted for the remainder of your life.

Build around what you truly need in your plan, in your budget, and look at what you might need for the next couple of decades, and truly look at what size portfolio you have and what kind of gap you can fill, and make your decision based on that.

If you’re married, factor in that one of you is going to pass away first. It makes a lot of sense in many cases to have the higher-earning spouse delay because whoever (survives), they get to keep the higher earner’s benefit, and that can provide a lot of security to the surviving spouse.

This edited Q&A was provided to The Associated Press by Morningstar. For more retirement content, go to https://www.morningstar.com/retirement.

Christine Benz is director of personal finance and retirement planning for Morningstar and co-host of The Long View podcast. Subscribe to her free newsletter, Improving Your Finances.

Amy C. Arnott, CFA, is a portfolio strategist for Morningstar and co-host of The Long View podcast.

#10839. Nvidia earnings preview: How Jensen Huang updates could impact the stock, whether the results are strong or not

All eyes are always on Nvidia’s (Nasdaq: NVDA) earnings. The company is the backbone of the AI revolution, after all.

But today’s results—and its upcoming financial call—will be the first without cofounder and former CEO Jensen Huang at the helm, which could make this the company’s most consequential report in Nvidia’s history.

Investors will not only want to see favorable growth, but they will want an update on the company’s direction after Huang resigned amid multiple scandals last week.

And if that update doesn’t assuage fears—or if it leads to more uncertainty—the stock could continue its downward spiral. Here’s what you need to know.

Nvidia reports Q3 2027 results today

After markets close today, Nvidia will report results for its third quarter of fiscal 2027, which ended on October 25. The results are expected around 4:20 p.m. ET, with the earnings conference call scheduled to begin at 5 p.m. ET.

As far as numbers go, investors are expecting big results from Nvidia. As noted by CNBC, Wall Street analysts expect the company to report revenue of $109.1 billion and earnings per share (EPS) of $2.47. 

If Nvidia hits that revenue target, it will represent staggering growth of 91% versus the same quarter last year, when the company posted $59 billion in revenue.

However, even if Nvidia achieves 91% growth, it will be lower than the 106% YOY growth the company saw in its second quarter, when it reported $96.2 billion in revenue.

That suggests growth is slowing—something investors never like to hear. Indeed, in August, Nvidia’s then chief financial officer and current interim CEO, Colette Kress, said the company expects just 70% growth in fiscal 2028, which she attributed to a “supply constrained outlook.”

But numbers are one thing. What investors will really want to hear about is what the company plans to do in a post-Huang world.

Jensen Huang may be out, but he’s still at the top of investors’ minds

Last week, the AI world was rocked when Nvidia’s cofounder and CEO resigned amid a set of scandals.

The first scandal had to do with his affair with a woman who U.S. intelligence believed may have been spying for China. Beijing officials have strongly denied the allegations.

However, the allegations and their resulting national security concerns were enough to compromise Huang’s position at the company. Then, shortly after reports of a second alleged affair, this time with an Nvidia employee, emerged, Huang resigned as CEO.

Shortly after Huang resigned, Nvidia announced that its CFO, Colette Kress, would step into the interim chief executive role while it sought a new CEO. The company is reportedly considering both internal and external candidates.

In recent days, there have also been rumblings that Huang may be planning to make a comeback and return to the CEO role, much like OpenAI CEO Sam Altman did in 2023 after he was ousted.

Investors will want updates on both issues: how the search for Huang’s replacement is progressing and whether the board would even be open to Huang’s return.

They’ll also likely want an update on Nvidia’s internal investigation into whether the company’s secrets, or national security secrets, were compromised by Huang’s alleged affairs.

Which way will NVDA stock go?

Currently, NVDA stock is trading down about 1.2% ahead of the company’s results later today. Unfortunately for investors, that red on NVDA’s charts is par for the course as of late.

In the past five trading days alone, NVDA shares have lost more than 7.5% in value. Over the past month, shares have cratered more than 27%. 

The scandals and Huang’s departure have had an abysmal effect on Nvidia’s stock price. That fall was first exacerbated by fears that AI spending could be declining as companies start to rein in costs. The Huang scandals then acted as a pile-on.

Nvidia’s all-time closing high was $239.24 on October 6, just less than six weeks ago. At the time, the company had a valuation of more than $5.7 trillion.

But today, post-Huang scandal, the company’s share price is below $154, a level it hasn’t been at since mid-2025. The company currently has a valuation of $3.7 trillion—a fall of roughly $2 trillion.

If Nvidia’s Q3 results are stellar and the company’s new interim leadership reassures investors about its direction and future, investors could reward the stock after hours, sending it up.

But if Nvidia’s numbers don’t excite—and, more importantly, if Nvidia’s comments on Huang and the company’s future leadership don’t inspire confidence—NVDA could see more red ahead.

#10837. Slack and Teams have a neurotypical bias. Here’s how to fix it

Text-based workplace communication platforms like Slack, Teams and email can create challenges for neurodiverse workers. But most have easy fixes—and the solutions can benefit all workers.

According to an analysis by BRYGE AI, an online tool that helps make messages more neurodiverse friendly, most workers fail to recognize the ways in which their communication style poses challenges for recipients with autism, ADHD, dyslexia, or other neurological or learning differences.  

For example, research suggests that it takes most people an average of over 23 minutes to return to a task after being interrupted, and that workers are interrupted every two minutes by an email, meeting or notification. Getting back on track, however, can be especially difficult for those with ADHD, who often struggle with task paralysis, executive dysfunction and a more fragile working memory.

“Slack and Teams have the green dot, there are typing bubbles, read receipts, received and sent signals, all built into it—so now, there’s an implicit pressure to respond right away,” explains BRYGE AI founder and AI engineering lead for Uptrend Motion, Rebecca Koniahgari. “We took these flexible tools and turned them into a stopwatch, where everybody is being judged by visibility.”

Working across communication channels can also make it hard for anyone—but especially neurodiverse workers—to find a needle in a haystack of information. When answers are not easy to find, or when requests are not made explicit, those with autism or ADHD can quickly become overwhelmed.

“Messages get spread across different channels—email, DMs, Slack, Teams, iMessage, WhatsApp—and nobody knows where the decision was made,” Koniahgari says. “Sometimes you don’t respond because you don’t know what it is the other person is asking. And then they read it as ‘this person’s a no-show’ or is disengaged.”

Workplace communication tools can also create what Koniahgari calls “urgency ambiguity,” which occurs when a message inadvertently creates panic due to its lack of detail, such as a “Can we talk?” message from a manager. “One person reads it as, ‘Great, we’re going to have a good conversation later.’ And another person thinks they’re getting fired,” she says.

Text-based communication platforms also often lack the richness of context that comes with real-world interactions, leaving recipients to fill in the blanks on their own.  

“For neurodivergent people, there are a lot of gaps that need to be filled in to understand what a message means. All this ambiguity adds to the cognitive load,” Koniahgari says. “Now they have to manage the extra invisible cognitive load on top of the real work that they have to do.”

Why bridging the gap often falls on neurodiverse workers

The switch from mostly real-world to more online and asynchronous workplace communication hasn’t been all bad for neurodiverse workers.

For example, ADHD workers, who often struggle with short-term memory, can search through past conversations for information they can’t recall, while autistic workers who struggle with eye contact may find it more comfortable to communicate by text.  

At the same time, Koniahgari says online platforms also inherited the broader workplace expectation that communication practices default to neurotypical norms, putting the onus on the neurodiverse workers to adjust their approach. That is what autistic sociologist and social psychologist Dr. Damian Milton refers to as the “double-empathy problem.”

The theory, Dr. Milton says, was developed in response to accusations that those with autism lack social skills, which he believes is only true when considered through a neurotypical lens. Those perceived communication shortfalls, he says, often disintegrate when the same individual is interacting with autistic peers.

“Breakdowns can happen between any two people. However, in the autistic/non-autistic setting, the autistic person is constructed as being impaired,” he explains. “This can often lead to autistic voices being devalued.”

Communication breakdowns, he suggests, are more common between groups or individuals whose brains are wired differently. Yet only the autistic group is believed to lack communication skills.

Dr. Milton adds that the same is true in the workplace, as the onus typically falls on the neurodiverse worker to adapt to a more neurotypical communication style. According to a study by neurodiversity non-profit Understood.org, 69% of neurodivergent men and 75% of neurodivergent women feel pressure to conform to neurotypical behaviors at work.

While the double empathy problem has been primarily studied in the context of autism, Dr. Milton says he believes it would also be applicable to other forms of neurodiversity.

Finding fixes that work for everyone

Small changes to how we use workplace communication tools can make a significant difference for neurodiverse employees by reducing their cognitive load, removing unnecessary distractions and making instructions and tasks easier to follow.

“There’s such an easy fix: just add a few words,” says Sarah Greenberg, a licensed psychotherapist for neurodivergence nonprofit Understood.org. “It doesn’t take very long, and it can make such a difference in someone’s day. Like instead of ‘Can we talk?’ say, ‘Do you have time to talk? It’s not urgent. A question just came up that I think you’re the best person to answer. Let me know if you have time before tomorrow.’”

Providing a clear subject for the conversation, a clear timeline and some added context can turn a message that might derail someone’s day into the innocuous request it was intended to be.

“I would rather have my team focus on the important work that we’ve hired them to do than have them spend their time twisting and turning to try to translate what it is I mean,” Greenberg adds.

A little extra effort at the outset can also help reduce the overwhelmed feeling that can result from having information scattered across platforms and conversations.

“Accessibility is strongest when it is informed by lived experience. By bringing neurodivergent perspectives into product development, Microsoft can better understand where experiences may unintentionally exclude users, learn from diverse ways of thinking and working, and create solutions that ultimately benefit a much broader audience,” says Neil Barnett, Microsoft’s Chief Accessibility Officer. (Fast Company also reached out to Slack for comment, but didn’t hear back by time of publication.)

“When we create space for neurodivergent voices to shape our products, we don’t just improve accessibility, we build better technology for everyone,” Barnett’s statement continues.

Simple strategies, like starting a long message with the ultimate request or assignment rather than burying it within, making key details explicit, numbering points or questions to be answered or addressed in sequence, including supporting materials or clear instructions on where to find them can all go a long way.

Furthermore, giving workers permission to disappear from the Slack or Teams channel while engaged in their work, and designating a clear protocol for managing actual emergencies, can also help reduce distractions for all workers.

“One of the best things you could do as a leader—and certainly as a leader of neurodivergent individuals—is being really clear on what the goal is and when it’s needed,” Greenberg says. “If you can do that consistently, it will make a huge difference.”

Nov. 17, 12:45 p.m. ET: This story was updated to include a comment from Microsoft.

#10835. People’s AI secrets raise questions about the use of chatbots in personal life

A purple box set up on a folding table in San Francisco’s Alamo Square could have been mistaken for a tissue box. Instead, it held dozens of secrets.

Olivia Tai was nearby, watching as passersby puzzled over a sign that read, “Confession box: Everyone has an AI secret.”

Among confessions about farming out too much work or using artificial intelligence to suggest a first-date spot were several admissions about using it to write deeply personal messages: “I had AI write my breakup text.” “I had ChatGPT date my boyfriend. Every text.” “I used AI to write my girlfriend’s grandmother’s 80th birthday letter.”

“I used AI to win an argument. Lol, not proud, but I won.”

Tai, an artist who has repeated the project more than a dozen times in the Bay Area, New York and Denver, said calling it a “confession” suggested people were admitting that using AI made them feel at least a little guilty. But they used it anyway.

As more people turn to a chatbot to write emails or handle mundane tasks, it raises the question: Does using it for personal messages cross a line? Is it ever OK to use AI to write a holiday card or birthday message? What about a love letter or wedding vows?

Some use AI to write personal messages, even if they feel guilty about it

As part of research for a Ph.D. in consumer behavior, Danielle Hass conducted vignette-based experiments to determine how people felt about using AI to write personal messages. She found that people who had used AI before felt just as guilty as those who hadn’t.

“Everyone felt guilty,” said Hass, a professor at Kennesaw State University in Georgia. “The driver of this relationship was dishonesty, that people felt like they were manipulating.”

Even if people are honest about their AI use, others still view them negatively, said Scott Claessens, a senior researcher at the University of Bristol in the UK.

Claessens says his research suggests that people who use AI are perceived as lazier, less competent and less trustworthy.

He noted that people often already supplement social tasks, such as using pre-written birthday cards or poems in love notes. But something about the effortless way that AI works turns people off, he said.

“What we really want in our social relationships is for people to put effort in, because it shows that as a signal that you’re invested in the relationship,” he said.

How to know when it’s not OK to use AI

Sarah Newman, who collaborated with Tai as director of art and education at Harvard University’s metaLAB, said AI adoption is happening so quickly that norms will likely emerge about when it’s appropriate and when it’s not.

She suggested using conflicted feelings as a guide. If you imagine using AI to write your wedding vows, would you be comfortable telling your partner you used it? If not, then don’t.

“Personal messages don’t need to be perfect,” Newman said. “Part of what makes these messages authentic and endearing is that they are imperfect.”

Tai and Claessens said some situations could warrant using AI in personal situations, such as people in relationships who use it to communicate better with a partner who doesn’t share a native language. People with learning disabilities, such as dyslexia, could also use it for help.

Hass believes people are quickly accepting that AI can make them more efficient in some work contexts, but she said she’s glad people feel guilty using it for personal messages.

“Guilt’s purpose in this context is to show us our interpersonal wrongdoings,” Hass said. “It’s showing us, ‘hey, this is where you’re supposed to be human.'”

Albert Stumm writes about travel, food and wellness. Find his work at https://www.albertstumm.com

—Albert Stumm, Associated Press

#10833. Inside Hank Green’s plan to protect kids (and their parents) from YouTube

Pity the parent trying to pull the plug on YouTube, Netflix, or Disney Plus mid-video. The tears! The world-ending wails! 

Hank Green, host of popular YouTube channels Crash Course and SciShow, has been that parent. A longtime science educator and the co-creator of VidCon, Green has fought his fair share of “turn it off” battles with his son. 

“These systems are designed to keep kids watching,” he says of advertising-based platforms like YouTube and TikTok. “They don’t have any incentives to even consider designing a ‘stop’ experience.”

But parents, on the other hand, are highly incentivized to seek out an alternative to the endless scroll of sometimes questionable content that streams to family devices. Green hopes they will give his new venture, ClockwiseTV, a chance. 

Launching publicly today, ClockwiseTV is a subscription-based app featuring curated creator videos for kids ages 8-12. Parents establish a session length when opening the app; once kids start watching, ClockwiseTV helps them pick a video sequence that concludes when their session concludes. 

The app’s constrained choice philosophy extends to the video selection experience. Instead of infinite options, ClockwiseTV presents kids with a menu of just a dozen videos at a time, based on their interests (animals, space, history, etc). Each video has been vetted by a human and scrubbed of any inappropriate language. Creators in the initial library include Animal Wonders Montana, NerdForge, Crash Course Kids, and MinuteEarth. 

At launch, a subscription costs $6.99 a month or $69.99 a year. There are no ads, and the app is compliant with the Children’s Online Privacy Protection Act, or COPPA. 

No autoplay, no rabbit holes

ClockwiseTV joins a growing array of products designed to keep kids entertained without algorithms or ads. For screen-free audio content, there are Yoto players and Tonie boxes. For kids who want to gab with their besties, there is the Tin Can phone, which mimics the experience of a landline for an approved set of friends and family. There are even sticker printers that use AI to turn kids’ ideas into black-and-white stickers they can color and decorate. 

Green says he’s been working on ClockwiseTV for about a year. The decision to focus on tweens stems from his own experience as a parent. “My son is this age, so that’s part of it,” he says. “There’s a kind of no-man’s-land of content where it’s like, you’re done with Mr. Rogers, here’s all of YouTube. I think that’s probably not the right way to do it.” 

Over time, he adds, ClockwiseTV may pursue “other slices of the pie” and expand its target audience. 

More than half of children own a smartphone by age 11, according to Common Sense Media’s 2026 census. The average tween spends five hours a day watching entertainment on a screen, the census found. 

Leslie Morgan, who has worked in digital media with creators for more than a decade, joined ClockwiseTV as CEO in January. Like Green, she hopes to help parents avoid the internet’s darker corners. “There are no rabbit holes, there’s no autoplay,” Morgan says of the app. 

Incentivizing kid-friendly creators

ClockwiseTV is paying creators to license their content. “A lot of these creators would love to get in front of a younger audience, and it’s not financially viable,” says Morgan, explaining that labeling content as “made for kids” on YouTube typically lowers creators’ AdSense revenue. “For them, it doesn’t make economic sense.” With ClockwiseTV, she argues, creators can access new audiences and supplement their income from other platforms. 

When I watch YouTube with my kids, it’s often a stressful experience. Typically they’ve requested to revisit a favorite video that, in their view, is very specific (“the tap dancing man in the hat!”). It’s up to me to weed through a confounding list of thumbnails and keyword-optimized titles, searching for an elusive winner, while they squirm impatiently. 

ClockwiseTV, in contrast, is built to reward parental trust. I told the app that my son was interested in history videos, and had just 15 minutes to watch. The app suggested a short video about fossils and a slightly longer video about hunting for 50 million-year-old shark teeth. Both were educational and engaging. 

“That’s it for now,” the app announced when the allotted time was up. “Go do something else that’s awesome!” 

I found myself wishing that Instagram would tell me the same thing.

#10831. Thanksgiving airfare has jumped 17%, but AAA says there’s a way to avoid some of the holiday travel pain

Millions of Americans will take to the skies for the Thanksgiving holiday, even as travel costs are surging. But while soaring airfare prices seem inescapable, there may be some key ways to protect your bank account this year.

According to a new AAA report, slightly fewer people will travel for the holiday this year. Last year 81.6 million people hit the skies or the highway for Thanksgiving between Tuesday, Nov. 24, and Monday, Nov. 30. This year, the agency projects around 81.5 million Americans will do the same for a decline of 0.1% or 121,000 fewer travelers.  

AAA’s Vice President Stacey Barber says that the drop-off comes after a multi-year surge in the number of Thanksgiving travelers. “After three years of record highs, the number of travelers is slightly lower partly because of higher travel costs this year. But travelers won’t notice much of a difference on the roads and at the airports, since projected volumes remain near record levels,” Barber said.

In terms of air travel, the numbers drop off further. Around 5.96 million travelers are expected to travel domestically over the holiday for a 0.2% decrease over last year. The lower-than-average projection makes sense, given the cost of domestic flights is up around 17% this holiday season for an average of roughly $800 per ticket.

As the cost of plane tickets continues to climb, Americans are more invested than ever in finding deals. According to a new CheapOair survey, nearly half (49%) of travelers said they have delayed bookings in order to shop holiday travel deals. Another 60% say they are likely to consider doing so in the future.

You can’t exactly combat the cost of filling up your tank, but AAA says that driving is still the most cost-savvy way to get to your destination. That remains true even as the cost of gas has jumped from last year at this time. According to AAA’s fuel prices tracker, the national average is $3.78 per gallon. A year ago, it cost $3.07. In light of the cost of gas, around 200,000 fewer people will travel by car over Thanksgiving. While last year 73.1 million travelers hit the road, this year around 72.9 million will. 

While travelers are undoubtedly strained by travel costs, some travel categories are actually booming. AAA projects 2.6 million Americans will take buses, trains, and cruises this Thanksgiving. That’s an increase of 4.1% from last year alone. But even in 2025, train travel was ticking up. Amtrak saw record ridership that year and projected that it would have 6% more riders in 2026.

U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean P. Duffy attributed the boom to improvements to train travel as well as competitive pricing. “Amtrak’s growth is a preview of what’s possible when everyone is working together to help get the American people where they need to go,” Duffy said in a 2025 statement.

He added, “Faster trains, more affordable service, and extended routes are opening up a new era of American rail.”

#10829. Best AI Humanizer Tools Tested and Compared: 7 Options for Web Content

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best AI humanizer for web content is the one that changes how a draft reads without changing what it says. If you need a specific tone, Humanize Max gives you four styles and three lengths in one run. If you already work in QuillBot or Grammarly, their humanizers fit the account you have. GPTinf, Humanize AI and Undetectable AI put an AI check next to the rewrite, and Ahrefs gives you quick variants of a short passage.

To compare them on equal terms, we ran the same 103-word AI-written release note through each tool in November 2026. It contains a product name, two image formats, a settings path and the word "must", so it shows whether a tool keeps the details a reader acts on. We also checked every feature, limit and price on the vendor's own pages on the same day.

A humanizer earns its place only if its output needs fewer fixes than the draft you started with, and these six checks show whether a tool will do that for your content.

Humanize Max is a single-purpose humanizer: you paste an AI draft, upload a file or load the built-in sample, choose a style and a length, and get a rewritten version in a few seconds. The four styles are Academic, Casual, Creative and Formal, and the three lengths are Shorten, Standard and Expand, so you can turn a stiff feature description into casual blog copy and trim it in the same run.

The result highlights every phrase it changed, which makes it quick to check whether a product name or a settings path came through. If the rewrite doesn't land, Run it again produces a different version of the whole text. At the time of writing, it takes up to 2,000 words per run and works without an account.

Pricing: Plans and limits are still being finalized, so check the site for current terms.

Best for: Rewriting AI drafts of blog posts, product pages and emails into a specific tone.

QuillBot's AI Humanizer sits next to its paraphraser, grammar checker and AI detector, which makes it a natural pick if you already use QuillBot for rewording. Basic mode is free for up to 125 words per run, while Advanced mode, which rewrites more deeply, comes with Premium (the pricing page also lists three Advanced uses on the free plan). In our test, it asked us to create a free account before it showed the first result.

Premium removes the word limit and adds Freeze words, which keeps the terms you choose unchanged, along with tone insights, a history of past runs and a Chrome extension that humanizes text inside ChatGPT and Gemini.

Pricing: Basic mode is free. Premium is listed at $8.33 a month, billed annually.

Best for: Quick fixes to a paragraph or two for writers who already use QuillBot.

GPTinf is a writing toolkit built around its humanizer, with an AI detector, plagiarism checker, grammar checker, paraphraser and readability checker on the same site. The humanizer has two modes, Standard and Academic, and takes pasted text or a PDF, Word or plain-text file. File-to-File mode returns the humanized document in its original format, so a long brief keeps its layout.

Custom Style lets you paste 150 to 1,200 words of your own writing, and later runs are shaped to match it, with no account needed. After each run, an AI-likelihood estimate appears under the output, so you can see whether a draft needs another pass. Treat that number as a probability, not proof of who wrote the text.

Pricing: A free plan is available. Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $24.99 and Unlimited at $59.99.

Best for: Freelancers and content teams that want to rewrite and check drafts without switching tools.

Grammarly's AI Humanizer comes in two layers. The tool on its web page is free, works without an account and offers basic humanization of pasted text or an uploaded document. A free Grammarly account adds the Humanizer agent, with four preset styles, a custom voice built from a writing sample, and support for English, Spanish, French, German, Portuguese and Italian.

Grammarly says the humanizer focuses on clarity and readability rather than on AI detectors, which matches how most web teams use these tools. If you need to show how a piece was written, Grammarly Authorship shows which parts came from AI, which came from online sources and which you wrote yourself. The basic tool's word counter allows up to 1,500 words per run.

Pricing: The basic humanizer is free. Grammarly Pro is listed at $12 on the plans page, which doesn't state the billing period.

Best for: Teams that already write in Grammarly and want one voice across documents.

Humanize AI is built for people who want to tune the output. Without an account you get the Free tone, a Rewrite level slider with three stops (Quality, Balanced and Max) and Custom style, which learns your voice from 150 to 1,200 words of samples. A free account opens eight more modes, including Formal, Informal, Academic, Expand and Shorten, and on desktop a Writing level menu sets how plain or dense the result reads.

View changes highlights every edit, and File-to-File accepts PDF, DOCX, TXT and MD documents and returns them in the same format. Paid plans also include a public API that supports the same styles and rewrite levels as the web app, so a developer can add a humanizing step to a CMS or publishing pipeline.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (10,000 words), with Standard at $19.99 and Unlimited at $59.99. Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Teams that need precise tone control or want to humanize text through an API.

Undetectable AI keeps its humanizer simple: one text box, one button and no style or length settings. Its free allowance works differently from the others, with 1,000 words in total before you need an account and another 1,000 once you create a free one.

Under the output, a Check for AI button runs a detection check on the result, and the same site has a separate AI detector and plagiarism checker. Its FAQ says pasted text isn't published or shared but doesn't say whether it's stored, so keep confidential details out of the drafts you run through it.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $25 (25,000 words) and Unlimited at $59 (1,000,000 words). Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Trying a humanizer on a full-length draft before you commit to an account.

Ahrefs, best known for its SEO platform, offers a free AI Text Humanizer among its writing tools. You paste up to 2,048 characters, choose one, three or five variants, and pick the version that reads best. It ran without an account in our test.

Variants are the useful part. For a hero headline, a meta description or a product blurb, comparing three rewrites side by side is faster than prompting a chatbot three times, and you can export all results as a text file. The character limit, which fits a few short paragraphs, rules it out for full articles.

Best for: Short marketing copy where you want several options to choose from.

The best AI humanizer tool for a documentation team isn't the one a growth marketer needs, so match the tool to the content in front of you.

There isn't one winner, because these tools solve different problems. The quickest way to decide is to run a paragraph from your own work, ideally one with a product name, a number and an instruction, through two or three of the tools above, and keep the one whose output you'd publish with the fewest fixes.

Google's guidance on using generative AI content is about value rather than the tool: mass-producing pages that add nothing for readers can count as scaled content abuse. A humanizer changes phrasing, not usefulness, so the same rules apply. Google also recommends fact-checking AI-generated titles, meta descriptions and image alt text before you publish.

The best humanizer for your team is the one whose controls match your content: tone presets for blog posts, variants for short copy, change highlighting for docs and a custom voice for marketing. Run one of your own paragraphs through two or three tools from this list, then read every highlighted change, especially names, numbers and words like "must", before anything goes live.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10828. Amazon CEO Andy Jassy feared being ‘a lousy dad’—so he did this

When Amazon CEO Andy Jassy became a parent for the first time 26 years ago, he didn’t want to choose between being a good worker and being a good father.

That left him with only one option.

“I realized that I had to change the way I worked,” Jassy recently said during an appearance at Amazon’s Bring Your Parents to Work Day. “[Otherwise,] I was either going to have to leave Amazon, or I was going to be a lousy dad.”

Jassy was used to working grueling hours—often staying at the office late into the night—and he wasn’t sure if he could do his job effectively on a tighter schedule.

“I didn’t know how I would do my job without [working all the time],” he said. “But like everything else, if you think about it and you rework it and you find ways to be more efficient and you make trade-offs, you can do it.”

One step was to ensure that, whenever he was at the office, he was using his time efficiently.

“I did spend a lot of time in the hallway talking to people, and I wasn’t always prioritizing the things that mattered most,” Jassy said. “It forced me to get more efficient in what I did.”

He also established rules to help him prioritize family: being home for dinner every day, having weekly breakfasts with each of his children, and maintaining a standing Tuesday date night with his wife.

The challenge Jassy faced will likely be familiar to any parent struggling to balance career ambitions with responsibilities at home. And it is increasingly common among men, a growing number of whom say they are interested in being parents or caregivers. 

In one recent survey, over two-thirds of men said they consider caregiving to be as important as paid work and that both parents should contribute equally—yet only 40% of men who are caregivers said they shared the responsibility equally with their partners.

In the same survey, nearly two-thirds of male caregivers said work obligations interfered with their family time. A third reported having “no control” over their schedules, and only 40% said they had a hybrid or remote work arrangement. (Amazon issued a five-day RTO policy in 2024.)

Meanwhile, the study found that male caregivers are increasingly missing work or cutting back on their work hours. The percentage of men who care for adults or children with extra needs who reported missing work rose from 64% in 2019 to 76% in 2026.  

And, as Fast Company reported recently, men in some states are filing at least as many claims for parental leave as mothers.

For fathers who want to keep their career ambitions and still be equal parents, Jassy said there may be no one-size-fits-all solution.

“There are times you have to be flexible with what’s happening in your life and at work,” he said. “But it’s doable if you want to do it.”

#10826. For How Long Do You Need to Meditate?

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Picture this: 71 adults signed up for a study comparing brief to longer mindfulness practice. Many had never meditated, while others had dabbled years before.

Participants were divided at random into three groups: One group got 20 minutes of mindfulness meditation, a second group got five minutes, and the control group relaxed with audiobook excerpts from Bill Bryson’s A Short History of Nearly Everything, Bryson’s soothing recounting of the cosmos and our place in it.

In studies like these, “mindfulness meditation” typically means sitting quietly, eyes closed or softened, resting nonjudgmental attention on your breath or bodily sensations, gently noticing when your mind wanders (as it will), and just as gently bringing it back.

Now, you might expect that longer meditations would deliver greater benefits. After all, we’re conditioned to believe that more is better—especially when it comes to self-improvement. But this research and many other studies find that more minutes don’t necessarily mean more benefit: Shorter practices can work just as well—and sometimes better—while consistency may matter more than duration.

In this first study, each participant attended four 25-minute sessions over two weeks. In every session, the time not spent on audio-guided mindfulness-of-breath practice was filled by Bryson’s eloquence. To ensure the results reflected only the study activities, participants were asked to avoid any formal mindfulness practices outside these sessions.

After those two weeks, the five-minute meditators actually showed greater improvements in stress and mindful awareness (the tendency to stay present in daily life with less judgement) than the 20-minute group. Even depression and anxiety improved just as much—if not more.

Was the extra time with Bryson the magic ingredient for those five-minute meditators? The control group, who luxuriated in 25 uninterrupted minutes of his charm, showed no meaningful improvement. So while his voice may soothe, it wasn’t what moved the needle.

One five-minute meditator put it this way: “I always thought that to do mindfulness, I’d need a long time to dedicate to this, which seemed too challenging to even start. But I’ve learned that five minutes can help!” In contrast, a 20-minute participant admitted, “I think a shorter one would be better for me to learn mindfulness.”

There’s more. The facilitator observed that participants in the 20-minute meditation sessions more often fidgeted, shifted in their seats, and peeked through half-closed eyes, as if tranquility were just out of reach. Meanwhile, the five-minute meditators appeared more at ease and less prone to these signs of discomfort. Five of them even requested recordings to continue at home. The 20-minute group? Not a single request. For these beginners, less really did seem to be more.

Now, before you dash off to cancel that meditation retreat, remember that this study focused on people with little to no meditation experience. So if you’re a seasoned meditator, those longer sessions might still pack more punch for you, as we’ll explore later. And both the five-minute and 20-minute groups showed improvements in mindful awareness, depression, anxiety, and stress compared to the control group. The five-minute meditators just improved more when it came to mindful awareness and stress.

The takeaway? For beginners, a quick dip into mindfulness might be more refreshing than a long soak.

You might be wondering, Isn’t this just one study? Could it be a fluke? But multiple randomized controlled trials have explored how the length of meditation shapes its impact.

I join Christine Carter, Ph.D, in a webinar next week to talk about my book Micropractice.

Microhabits: The Science of Habits So Small They Actually Stick

In the largest of these studies, researchers recruited 372 adults online—about half of them new to meditation—to see how the “dose” of mindfulness meditation affected mindful awareness and emotional well-being. Participants were randomly sorted into one of four groups: Some meditated for 10 minutes, others for 20, and the rest listened to a 10- or 20-minute National Geographic article on sequoia trees. (Because nothing soothes stress like a deep dive into bark thickness.)

What did the researchers find? The 10-minute meditators showed greater improvements in mindful awareness than those who listened to the 10-minute sequoia lecture. Surprisingly, the 20-minute meditation group didn’t outperform any group—not even the tree enthusiasts. In fact, the 10-minute group showed slightly greater improvements in mindful awareness than the 20-minute meditators, although the difference wasn’t statistically significant, meaning it could have occurred by chance. Still, it suggests that for boosting mindful awareness right after practice, 10 minutes might be at least as effective as 20.

But that was just one session. What happens with consistent practice? In another study, 77 college students were randomly assigned to meditate daily for 10 or 20 minutes. After two weeks, both groups showed similar improvements in stress and, again, mindful awareness. But those assigned to the 20-minute group experienced a slightly greater boost in self-compassion, the skill of meeting your own struggles with the kindness you’d offer a dear friend.

So if you’ve poured hours into longer meditations, you can rest easy—it wasn’t for nothing. The evidence suggests that whether your sessions are short or sustained, they can offer meaningful benefits.

Another notable finding is that the 10-minute group practiced more consistently, logging about 82 minutes total. The 20-minute group missed more days and didn’t always finish, ending up with just 103. Double the goal, barely a quarter more practice.

If you’ve ever tried meditating, you might be nodding along. Some sessions are bliss, while others are a tug-of-war with time. Maybe you’ve battled that nagging frustration, trying to keep your mind steady for more than a fleeting moment. Or felt the magnetic pull of distraction, your mind wandering just when you need it to stay put. As the minutes crawl, doubt can creep in: Am I even doing this right? Is it worth it? These tough moments can teach you a lot, but they can also make meditation feel intimidating—and make you less likely to stick with it, especially when you’re just starting out.   Another study looked at even longer sessions, comparing 10 minutes of meditation to 30 minutes. This experiment split 161 adults into four groups: 10 or 30 minutes of daily sitting meditation, or the same amount of mindful movement. The mindful movement was a stretching practice that brings mindful awareness into motion—less stillness, same presence.

After two weeks, everyone saw benefits: Mindful awareness increased, well-being improved, and distress levels dropped. But there were no significant differences among the groups. Whether they meditated for 10 minutes or 30, sitting or moving, all the groups seemed to benefit equally.

So, here’s the bottom line: One study found that longer sessions more strongly boosted self-compassion. Another showed that shorter ones more effectively reduced stress. But across studies, most of the key benefits—mindful awareness, mental health, well-being—weren’t tied to how long people practiced.

Why? Because as we’ll keep exploring, the nervous system learns through repetition, not duration. It’s consistency that reshapes patterns. Not heroic efforts once in a while, but showing up, again and again, however you can.

The benefits of consistency over length show up in observational studies, too, where people choose how often and how long to meditate. While observational studies can’t pin down cause and effect, they offer a richer view of how shorter practices play out in the real world.

Imagine it’s early morning. You reach for your phone and open Insight Timer, a free meditation app. Before your meditation, it asks, “How are you today?” You tap an icon—maybe “OK,” maybe “great”—capturing a snapshot of your mood in seconds.

Now imagine doing this regularly, for 14 months straight. That’s exactly what 10,409 people from 103 countries did in a groundbreaking study. Many were new to meditation, using the app to track their emotional ups and downs in real time. It’s like taking your emotional temperature—not with a thermometer, but with a single tap that sums up how you feel.

The findings? Consistency (four to seven days a week)—not duration—was the strongest predictor of better mood, resilience, and equanimity, the ability to stay cool under pressure. Think of it like watering a plant: A little each week keeps it happy and growing. Forget all month and drown it after four weeks? It wilts anyway.

For beginners in the Insight Timer study, short sessions—five to 10 minutes—outshone longer ones in improving mood over time. But after 20 sessions? Longer practices—21 to 30 minutes—began to take the lead in mood improvement. It’s like your brain needs to build its meditation muscles before longer sessions feel better than short ones—like a marathon that only gives you the high once you’ve earned it.

But do shorter sessions lose their impact as you gain experience? Not at all. They might just become your secret weapon. After 80 sessions, shorter practices make a late-game comeback, boosting equanimity and showing that even seasoned meditators can benefit from briefer sessions.

The idea that consistency beats duration is backed by a meta-analysis challenging the belief that longer equals better. Think of a meta-analysis as a “study of studies,” combining data from multiple research papers to uncover clearer, big-picture insights.

When researchers analyzed 203 randomized controlled trials totaling 15,971 participants, they found no association between meditation session length and better outcomes. In fact, a trend emerged in favor of shorter practices for reducing depression symptoms, although it wasn’t statistically significant. Still, shorter sessions were at least as effective as longer ones, calling into question the assumption that more minutes always mean more benefits.   So what’s the takeaway? When you’re starting out, don’t sweat the long stuff. Quick sessions might do more to lift your mood early on. As you settle in, longer practices might bring deeper emotional gains. But even if you’re a pro, short sessions don’t lose their value—they just shift their superpower, helping you stay steady when life throws you curveballs. Different strengths, different stages. And who doesn’t love a shortcut that actually works?

Eli Susman, Ph.D., is a UC Berkeley-trained psychologist, former Harvard researcher, and author of Micropractice: A Science-Backed Approach for Calm, Clarity, and Joy in 30 Seconds or Less, forthcoming in 22 languages.

Using animal characters, educators can help children navigate big feelings with mindfulness and self-compassion.

A study of 38,000 people around the world finds that people who feel connected to nature are more hopeful, purposeful and resilient.

Whether we’ve been trying to improve our exercise, eating, sleep, or happiness, self-reflection is one of the most practical tools we have.

What does it actually take to put people over politics? Republican and Democrat students discover ways to resist the pull to demean or villainize people with whom they strongly disagree.

Psychologist and meditation teacher Tara Brach explores how practicing kindness and compassion can strengthen our relationships, ease loneliness, and help us feel more connected to others.

The Greater Good Science Center studies the psychology, sociology, and neuroscience of well-being, and teaches skills that foster a thriving, resilient, and compassionate society.

#10825. Jensen Huang says he would be ‘honored’ to return as Nvidia’s CEO after resigning

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang signaled interest Tuesday in returning to lead the computer chip company in his first public statement since he resigned last week following revelations of an extramarital affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Some tech industry figures, including SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk, have called for the reinstatement of Huang, a high-profile booster for the artificial intelligence industry who oversaw Nvidia’s development as the key provider of chips driving advanced AI models. President Donald Trump, a Huang ally who has spoken with him frequently, has also endorsed the comeback kid.

“If the board of directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO,” Huang said in a social media post.

Huang acknowledged making “serious mistakes” in his personal life but said that he’s received an “outpouring” of support from employees, investors, business partners, and customers for his return.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Huang’s statement. On Monday, a person familiar with the board’s thinking who insisted on anonymity to discuss it told The Associated Press that the board was not currently considering Huang’s return.

The Santa Clara, California-based company was scheduled to release quarterly earnings later Tuesday. Nvidia chief financial officer Colette Kress is expected to speak with Wall Street analysts on the earnings call for the first time since being named interim CEO.

A petition drive seeks Huang’s return

A “Stand with Jensen” petition set up last week has gathered momentum, gaining signatures from current and former Nvidia staff, investors, and other Silicon Valley supporters. The petition calls on the board to bring back Huang while an independent investigation proceeds or until there’s “compelling evidence” that his departure is in the company’s best interests.

“We are concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process,” the organizers said in an open letter. “We have not been shown evidence substantiating the allegation that the woman at the center of this controversy was a spy.”

Trump suggested Monday that the ex-CEO could return, saying Huang “learned a big lesson” and that he “would certainly not mind” if he was reinstated.

Huang resigned last week from Nvidia, apparently ousted by the board after revelations that he had extramarital affairs, both with a Chinese businesswoman suspected of being an intelligence asset for Beijing and with a senior executive who reported to him.

The businesswoman Huang was seeing who reportedly had links to Chinese intelligence was an executive at Singapore-based cloud computing firm Megaspeed International, a company being investigated by the U.S. for allegedly violating U.S. export controls by illegally diverting advanced Nvidia AI chips to Chinese companies.

Huang faces questioning by Congress

Also Tuesday, key House lawmakers said that Huang has voluntarily agreed to appear for transcribed interviews as the company faces intensifying national scrutiny over sales of its advanced computer chips.

Huang will appear before congressional committees on December 1, followed by the company’s general counsel, Tim Teter, on December 2.

“We look forward to hearing from Jensen Huang and Tim Teter on their roles in a potential breach of our national security,” said a statement from the chairmen of three House committees with oversight of China and intelligence issues.

“The American people need answers on Mr. Huang and Nvidia’s role in allowing dangerous PRC actors to access our most advanced chips, as well as the role of a potential Chinese intelligence asset in this scandal,” the lawmakers said, referring to the People’s Republic of China.

Meanwhile, Nvidia said it’s creating a new board committee to oversee national security and global trade compliance following the revelations about Huang.

Nvidia’s advanced chips are in high demand because they’re essential for cloud computing and data centers that underpin the AI boom, but they face restrictions on their sale to China. Trump said it was “good” that Nvidia had created the new oversight committee.

“Yes, that’s a good idea. It’s a great company,” Trump said shortly after arriving Tuesday evening in the Philippine capital of Manila, where he participated in a photo opportunity with the leaders of Australia, India, and Japan. “It’s a great idea.”

—By Kelvin Chan, AP business writer

Associated Press writers Darlene Superville and Kevin Freking in Washington and Josh Boak in Shenzhen, China, contributed.

#10823. Papa John’s wants you to take a picture of your pizza. The reason might surprise you

Papa John’s is looking to improve the quality of pizza that arrives at your door, and it’s doing so by having you snap a photo of your pie before chowing down.

The company launched a “Snap a Pizza, Get a Free Pizza” program this week in which customers are asked to photograph their orders and leave feedback in exchange for a free pizza. The images and the feedback will be analyzed using an AI-based quality-control system.

As part of Papa John’s broader “Unbox Better” strategy, this promotion aims to improve customer experience by addressing quality control in its pizza.

The program is designed to help train its in-house AI system. According to Reuters, the technology will work like in-store quality-control cameras that detect whether a pizza is burnt or unevenly cut.

Customer-submitted images will be used by the company to train a similar system that, while less accurate than in-store cameras, can be implemented more widely. Currently, only hundreds of its 6,000 stores are equipped with these cameras.

The promotion follows a 6.5% decline in quarterly same-store sales, Papa John’s reported in its third-quarter earnings. Per Reuters, the new quality-control system will aim to give every store a direct view into what needs fixing.

“We see the pizza pics, the unboxings, and the hot takes online every day, and we know our customers aren’t shy about sharing what they think,” Chris Lyn-Sue, global chief marketing officer at Papa John’s, said in a press statement.

The offer is valid from November 9 through November 20, and customers wanting to participate will need to sign in or create a Papa Rewards account. Once logged in, users must opt in to SMS messages to be eligible for the offer.

After ordering a pizza ($15 minimum), customers will receive a text message prompting them to take and upload an unedited photo of the pizza before eating it, as well as to leave an honest review of the bake, the crust, and their overall eating experience.

Once the photo and feedback are confirmed, customers will get a Papa Rewards offer, unlocking a free large one-topping pizza on their next order ($5 minimum).

Lyn-Sue added: “Every photo and every review helps us better understand what customers love and where we can improve as we continue our journey to deliver a better experience every time they choose Papa John’s.”

#10821. How to Make Better Decisions in the Age of AI

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Artificial intelligence can summarize research, generate ideas, and analyze enormous amounts of data in seconds. The power of that little “Ask anything” AI prompt box is immense, but can it help us make wiser decisions? Cheryl Strauss Einhorn, author of The Human Edge: Smarter Decisions in the Age of AI, writes:

AI has the power to enhance decision-making—or to overwhelm it. It can illuminate pathways we might not have seen or narrow our choices in ways we do not fully recognize. As AI systems become more integrated into our lives, the challenge is no longer simply about harnessing their power but about leading them with clarity, discernment, and commitment to human agency.

She argues that the rise of AI actually makes certain human qualities like reflection, empathy, values, and moral judgment even more important. We spoke with her about why human judgment is a competitive advantage, how to engage our values when we prompt AI, and how AI can help us clarify our thinking.

Kia Afcari: You argue that AI makes human judgment more, not less, important. How can we make sure that the values of kindness and care are reflected when we use AI to help us make decisions?

Cheryl Strauss Einhorn: AI has no values. It has biases and influences by those who have designed it, and from whatever information has been fed into it. So to be sure that prosocial values are reflected when we use AI, we humans need to bring them into the work that we do together.

When working with an AI tool, we want to be asking ourselves, What is the problem that I’m solving? Why am I solving it? What is the context? And so we really need to be intentional with AI, because without asking ourselves those things first, AI doesn’t know why we’re coming to it.

Before you actually sit down with a tool, you can muster those values and those important factors related to the problem that you’re solving, and then articulate them and communicate them to the machine.

If we let the machine think for us, we can lose that sense of right and wrong, for example, because we actually care about all of those details that AI can’t possibly know about the problems that we’re solving and the people that we’re solving those problems with and for.

For example, you might ask yourself or your AI tool: Before I act, help me think through the people, relationships, and responsibilities this decision affects—not just the outcome it produces.

This type of human-centric AI interaction helped Felice, a food scientist at a pet food company, who was responsible for developing new recipes that balance nutrition, taste, and market trends. She often has to delegate tasks within her research team, ensuring that product testing, ingredient sourcing, and regulatory compliance all move forward smoothly. Using AI, she was able to analyze workloads and expertise that suggested the best person for each task, but only Felice understood the deeper dynamics at play—who works best under tight deadlines, who has a passion for innovative formulations, and who needs mentorship. When AI recommended assigning a crucial stability test to her most experienced technician, this prompt supported her thinking about a different option: giving it to the junior scientist who was eager to develop his skills in quality assurance. The AI tool’s choices made sense based on efficiency, but Felice’s decision ensured both short-term success and long-term team development.

KA: In the book, your “cheetah” metaphor celebrates slowing down. Why is a strategic pause so important when working with AI?

CSE: The cheetah metaphor relates that although this is an animal that goes zero to 60 in about three seconds, its hunting prowess actually comes from being able to decelerate up to nine miles an hour in a single stride. And the reason why that’s so important in problem solving and decision making is that’s where you build agility, flexibility, and maneuverability. Do I want to stay the course, or do I want to pivot and do something else?

This pause allows you to chunk your learning and pry open that cognitive space for new information and insight. With an AI tool, it’s so important because there’s a difference between speed and quality.

When something truly matters to us, we want to make sure that we’re solving the problem in a way that solves it holistically, that takes care of the people who are involved in the problem with us. And when we’re moving really quickly, sometimes those very important factors are hard to bring forward. So there’s a real difference between what is efficient and what is effective.

KA: Why can having more information make it harder—not easier—to make good decisions?

CSE: Francis Bacon once said, “Knowledge is power,” but that’s not really true anymore, right? We now have all of the answers with AI to a lot of commonly asked questions and some uncommon questions.

We need to make sure we’re defining the problem that we’re solving, that we are understanding and communicating the motivation and providing the context, because you don’t want all the information. Otherwise, we’re going to end up with analysis paralysis.

One thing people can use is what I call the “vision of success” question. This question asks you to time travel, to jump over the decision to see that the decision has been made well. What’s happening? What are those few things that if they don’t succeed, the decision fails? It’s actually very powerful, because it allows you to clearly identify so that you can articulate those factors that you absolutely need to be able to share with AI. These become the constraints and boundaries that set the perimeter around the decision, so that it can give you research that’s targeted and focused on those things.

For example, Max deciding where to spend his next vacation. Asking AI for help directly—without turning to himself first—he got a list of destinations, airline options, and local attractions, complete with ratings, reviews, and price comparisons. On paper, that sounds ideal. But in reality, Max quickly found himself paralyzed by the sheer amount of information. What if he picked a less-than-perfect hotel? What if he overlooked a hidden gem? AI has handed him a mountain of data, but it didn’t necessarily make the decision any easier.

However, when he used the “vision of success” question, he was able to realize and to share with his AI tool: “I want to return home feeling genuinely rested, having spent quality time with my family, without overspending or feeling like I missed the experience I was hoping for.”

Only then does AI have a meaningful problem to solve. Instead of searching for the “best” vacation—a question with no objective answer—it can help Max find the destination most likely to produce the outcome he actually wants. AI can’t define success but it can optimize for the success you define.

KA: How does the AREA method help people make wiser decisions, especially when AI is involved?   CSE: AREA is more than an acronym—it’s a framework for challenging our assumptions and thinking mistakes, and actively includes the perspectives of others. It stands for Absolute, Relative, Exploration and Exploitation, and Analysis.

The first “A,” Absolute, focuses on primary, unfiltered information straight from primary sources. “R,” Relative, shifts to external viewpoints—secondary sources connected to the subject. “E,” Exploration and Exploitation, fuels creativity: Exploration broadens research through interviews and unconventional sources, while Exploitation turns inward, challenging personal biases and assumptions. The final “A,” Analysis, synthesizes everything, bringing clarity and structure to complex decisions.

When using AI, a system like AREA becomes even more important because AI doesn’t eliminate bias—it can reflect and sometimes amplify biases in its training data, design, and the way we use it. Good decisions require a process that helps us recognize and challenge both AI’s biases and our own.

For AI, so much of the information, for example, is from Reddit. Information can be incomplete or outdated, and you also know that you’re interacting with a liar. It’s a machine that just gives you answers that are wrong sometimes.

So you need to be able to have a quality due diligence process to not only be able to use the tool effectively, but to be able to evaluate its outcome. Too often, people look at an answer with AI and think that it’s a transaction, and they can just take it. AREA is going to help you make sure that it’s a conversation. So you have an evidence-based perspective that has looked at a variety of stakeholder viewpoints, and made sure that you have pried open that cognitive space to challenge the natural, well-worn mental pathways that we all lean on every day.

KA: How can we use AI in ways that strengthen—rather than undermine—our values and agency?

CSE: It’s a great question. I think the new competitive advantage is going to be self-awareness.

This is the first tool that actually asks for some of our cognitive load; it wants us to offload some of our decisions to it. And this is a machine fundamentally about decision making, because we ask it for something and it gives us an answer. But there’s a very interesting invitation here, which is to each of us to get to know ourselves better.

We each have a special sauce, which is the way that we make our decisions, and we make thousands of them every single day. But most of us have not sat down and said to themselves, What does that process actually look like? Can I actually name those pieces and put language to it?

There is this invitation to get to know our thinking so that we can translate it better, and not only for the tool. How helpful would it be in conversation with our colleagues to be able to say clearly, This is how I’m actually going through the steps, this is how I’m thinking about it, these are the variables that I’m prioritizing, and here’s what I think is the most diagnostic.

You can think about your thinking skills as muscles. If we turn over our cognitive load, we will also weaken it. But if we actually take this invitation to understand our self-awareness and our metacognition, we can make sure that we strengthen those tools.

AI is a tool that has so many implications for our environment and for our world, in addition to the fact that it has implications for our actual thinking skills.

So I would say that the invitation is to turn to yourself first. Ask yourself why you’re using the tool, and whether or not you should be turning to yourself instead. And either way, bring your own thinking forward before sitting down, so that you can feel far more comfortable that you can problem-solve both with it and beyond it.

Kia Afcari is the director of Greater Good Workplaces at GGSC. He has over 20 years of experience helping leaders, teams, and organizations with collaborative change and has served as a consultant to a wide variety of organizations, including tech, biotech, and fintech companies, health care organizations, universities, NGOs, UN agencies, and nonprofits.
