Cheryl Strauss Einhorn is the creator of the AREA Method, a decision-making system for individuals, companies, and nonprofits to solve complex problems. Cheryl is the founder of the decision-sciences company Decisive, offering leadership training, curriculum, coaching, and professional development services, and is an adjunct professor at Cornell University.

Cheryl Strauss Einhorn is the creator of the AREA Method, a decision-making system for individuals, companies, and nonprofits to solve complex problems. Cheryl is the founder of the decision-sciences company Decisive, offering leadership training, curriculum, coaching, and professional development services, and is an adjunct professor at Cornell University.

Sending reels on social media may seem frivolous or distracting, but it actually functions as a form of relationship maintenance.

Youth participatory action research engages young people as the researchers investigating how to improve their schools and communities.

When Americans face complex decisions on immigration, climate change or political candidates, they usually don't consider all of the relevant factors.

What does it actually take to put people over politics? Republican and Democrat students discover ways to resist the pull to demean or villainize people with whom they strongly disagree.

Psychologist and meditation teacher Tara Brach explores how practicing kindness and compassion can strengthen our relationships, ease loneliness, and help us feel more connected to others.

The Greater Good Science Center studies the psychology, sociology, and neuroscience of well-being, and teaches skills that foster a thriving, resilient, and compassionate society.

#10820. If AI doesn’t take your job, Robert Pattinson might

#10818. All these outages show how corporate America needs to modernize out-of-date tech

Over the past five days, a string of high-profile tech outages has left Americans struggling to send emails, catch flights, and access their bank accounts.

While the disruptions raised concerns about AI-powered cybersecurity threats, the real culprit may be something less sinister: corporate America’s tech systems are simply out of date. 

Outages like those at JPMorgan Chase, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), and Microsoft can occur when computer systems are undergoing major upgrades, Marva Bailer, CEO of tech advisory firm Qualaix, told the New York Post. Bolting on new technology can leave the systems vulnerable.

Although it is unclear whether the recent outages were directly related to artificial intelligence, many organizations are currently upgrading their networks to handle AI tools. Microsoft attributed its outage to an issue that arose during a network capacity upgrade, according to the Financial Times.

The time of year could also be a factor, as technicians rush to make sure systems are ready for the holiday shopping and travel season.

“It’s holiday time, it’s travel time, it’s shopping,” Bailer told the New York Post. “So, you have all those higher spikes in volume and more pressure to [update your systems].”

AI is also allowing companies to identify software vulnerabilities far more quickly, possibly leaving IT workers with too much work on their hands, the Post reported.

Like Bailer, U.S. Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin attributed the disruptions to companies trying to upgrade aging systems, during an appearance on CNBC earlier today.

“[They’re] pushing new technology on top of really old computer systems, and it just seems to overwhelm the system,” he said.

The disruptions serve as a reminder of how cutting-edge technology is often still dependent on centralized, aging systems.

Friday’s nationwide ground stop, which resulted in more than 18,000 flight cancellations over three days, was triggered by the installation of a single router at a remote facility in Arkansas, Reuters reported. Monday’s software glitch at JPMorgan Chase disrupted transactions for customers of other major banks, even though their own computer systems were functioning normally.

Indeed, third-party tech providers have accounted for roughly two-thirds of publicly reported IT outages over the past nine years, according to a recent report from Uptime Institute, a global digital infrastructure advisory firm based in New York City.

#10816. NowThis just bought one of Gen Z’s favorite Instagram news accounts. Here’s why

If Instagram is your preferred avenue for media consumption, you’ve probably come across posts by Impact. The popular Gen-Z-targeted account is characterized by its quick-turn analysis on progressive, left-wing topics ranging from identity politics to mental health and niche internet trends. That winning formula caught the attention of social video publisher NowThis, which recently acquired the brand.

Launched in 2020 by founders Tim Chau and Michelle Andrews, the Impact Instagram handle was born in the apex of Gen Z’s formative years, an era marked by a hyper-online generation who reckoned with Donald Trump, COVID-19, social isolation, canceled graduations, the murder of George Floyd, and climate disaster all in the same year. Impact created timely stories around narratives that Gen Z desperately craved and traditional media often failed to cover: Its content is often among the first to share pointed commentary on trending headlines. 

When comedian Shane Gillis was being criticized online for a misogynistic SNL bit, Impact published “Male comedians don’t actually have to hate women to be popular.” After the internet was in an uproar over the U.K. banning books authored by Sally Rooney due to her support for Palestine, Impact wrote about it immediately. Impact even creates content around shared woes that span multiple generations, helping it reach larger audiences—as seen in posts like “Is it rude to ask friends to stay with them when you visit their city?” The comments section of each post is the heart of the community the Impact team has built and a glimpse into real cultural capital, making it easy to see why NowThis chose to purchase the company. 

Founded in 2012 by former Huffington Post execs Kenneth Lerer and Eric Hippeau, NowThis began as a social-first go-to for millennial content before being acquired by Vox Media. As millennials aged into the next phase of adulthood, companies and news organizations from all corners of the internet competed for the coveted attention of young people, nudging the video publisher into a new chapter. In 2023, NowThis expanded into a large-scale media network targeting Gen Z, taking on a new CEO, Sharon Mussalli, and a new editor-in-chief, Michael Vito Valentino, while also reckoning with the ramifications of significant layoffs. This latest move in acquiring Impact—for an alleged seven-figure, all-cash deal—demonstrates a commitment to reaching its target audience and the community of young people behind Impact. 

“[They] care about what’s happening in the world and want to understand how it connects to their relationships, their work, their culture, and their futures. That’s closely aligned with the Gen Z female audience we’re building for at NowThis. And we saw an opportunity to invest in that relationship and give it more room to grow,” Mussalli said in a statement via the NowThis PR team. 

She adds: “Impact brings a distinct editorial voice and a strong sense of what resonates with its community. We bring the production capabilities and experience developing original video franchises to help translate that into new series and recurring formats. Our ambition is to build something this audience can grow with, across all NowThis channels. That means giving people more than an individual story they encounter in their feed—it means creating recognizable programming they seek out, share, and return to.” 

In a statement to Fast Company, Chau, who is stepping down from the Impact staff but will remain involved in some capacity, calls the move “bittersweet.” “We put everything into Impact to get it where it is today, so stepping back isn’t easy. But we couldn’t be happier about where it’s headed, because we know NowThis is the right home for it. They’re leading in a space where social now dominates—really the first of their kind—and that’s exactly the future we want Impact to be part of.”

“My hope is that Impact stays the place young people go to for stories that actually land with them, the ones they talk about with friends, share, and sit with. Legacy media has struggled to resonate with this generation, and I hope Impact can keep being the place where they feel truly understood and connected,” Chau said.

#10814. A new report says free childcare in NYC will pay for itself

Since taking office, New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani has made it his mission to expand access to affordable childcare in the city, and for good reason. On average, NYC families spend $21,000 on childcare annually per child—the equivalent of about $10 billion every year. While about 40% of children in the city receive some free or subsidized care, many families continue to pay for childcare entirely out of pocket, with 60% of children receiving no subsidies.  

A new report reveals how universal childcare would not only benefit families in New York City, but also boost the economy: Just 35 hours of free childcare per week could bring up to $6.5 billion in economic value each year, which would likely exceed the cost of providing that care. 

The report—which was produced by a think tank, the 5BORO Institute at Citizens Union, in partnership with Boston Consulting Group—surveyed over 2,700 parents of children under five, along with about a hundred families who moved away from New York City recently. 

According to the report, nearly 70% of children under the age of six are cared for by parents or adults who work. When those parents are unable to find care and forced to drop out of the workforce or switch jobs, the city loses $1.7 billion in annual income; among the parents surveyed, 15% altered their work situation due to childcare issues. For hourly workers, childcare gaps can force them to miss out on $360 million in earnings each year. The report also found that childcare was a leading concern for families who had left the city, a growing trend that is supported by plenty of other data. 

In other words, employers also feel the effects of inadequate access to care. That can mean high turnover or simply lost productivity if their workers regularly contend with disruptions to their childcare: More than half of the parents surveyed said they had missed at least one full day of work in the past three months; on average during a three month period, they missed out on three to four full days of work. 

These childcare gaps can cost New York City employers about 54 million hours of missed work, according to the report. The act of providing 35 hours of free childcare each week would also enable nearly 30,000 parents to reenter the workforce, which could yield over a billion dollars in annual earnings.

Childcare has been a key focus for Mamdani since his campaign, and as Fast Company reported in a recent cover story, the mayor has even gotten the business community on board with its investments in this area. Mamdani is already building upon the universal 3-K program established by former mayor Bill de Blasio to extend care to younger children; the recently launched 2-K program is now providing free, full-day childcare to 2,000 two-year-olds, with plans to serve an additional 10,000 children by next fall. (The city has provided free pre-K to all four-year-olds since 2014.) 

Even business leaders who are critical of Mamdani have seemingly come around to the idea of free childcare, though they may not agree on how to achieve that. This fall, the 5BORO Institute created the NYC Child Care Business Council, bringing together a number of business leaders and organizations committed to putting their muscle behind this issue and advocating for childcare investment (something the business community has not historically done).

For years, employers have invested in generous parental leave policies and backup childcare benefits for their workforce, to help them court top talent and retain some of their best employees. But it seems companies are finally realizing that their best efforts can’t make up for a lack of robust childcare options or offset the mounting cost of care.

“The market is not able to solve this problem,” Grace Rauh, the executive director of Citizens Union, recently told Fast Company. “Frankly, private businesses aren’t able to provide the kind of fully robust childcare benefits that their employees need.”

#10812. From User Feedback to a Better Prompt: 4 Tools Tested in TypeScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When an LLM app gives a wrong answer, nothing in your logs shows it. The request returns 200, the JSON is valid, and no exception fires. Usually the user is the only one who notices. A thumbs-down button records that moment, on the exact trace that went wrong, while the user still remembers why.

That button is easy to add. The hard part is what comes after: turning the thumbs-down into a reviewed test case, a judge score, and a better prompt.

We ran that loop in TypeScript in four tools: LangSmith, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. All four handled the first four steps, which are user feedback, reviewer comments, a dataset and an LLM judge. Only AcruxCore rewrote the prompt from TypeScript. On 15 support tickets that no tool had seen, the rewritten prompt routed 13 correctly, against 7 for the original.

Our test app is a support-ticket router. It reads a ticket and returns JSON with a category (account, billing, bug or feature_request) and a priority (P0, P1 or P2). The first version of the prompt describes only the output format:

The support team has a policy that this prompt never states. Sign-in problems are always account. A customer who can't use the product at all is P0. Any billing error is P1, even when the customer says there is no rush. Feature requests are P2, however angry the customer sounds.

Without that policy, the model guesses priority from the customer's tone. Here are three real replies from the first version:

Every reply is valid JSON, so no schema check catches the mistake. A person has to read the reply and say it is wrong. The loop in this article takes that judgement and turns it into a better prompt.

All four tools cover evaluation. We compared the self-hosted ones on tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent, and on prompt registries in Prompt Management in TypeScript. For a broader comparison of self-hosted options, see this LangSmith alternatives roundup. This article covers the feedback loop only.

We wrote 30 tickets. Fifteen are the "production traffic" that each tool's app processed. The other 15 are a holdout set. No tool saw them, and we used them only for the final before-and-after check.

Each tool ran the same sequence from one TypeScript script:

The reviewer comments follow one template, so the wording is identical in every tool:

The comments name one rule each. The full policy appears nowhere in the data any tool received.

The app model isn't fully deterministic at temperature 0. Each tool's run got between 6 and 8 of the 15 traffic tickets right, so the tools received 7 to 9 thumbs-down each. The holdout check below repeats every measurement three times.

All four tools accept feedback on a trace from TypeScript. They differ in how a second person, the reviewer, adds the correct answer.

LangSmith stores both as feedback on a run. The thumbs-down runs go into an annotation queue, where a reviewer adds feedback with a comment:

Langfuse stores both as scores. An annotation queue needs a score config first:

Phoenix stores both as span annotations. The reviewer's comment goes in explanation:

Phoenix stores spans asynchronously. Our first run annotated a span before Phoenix had stored it, and the API returned a 404. The script now waits until all 15 spans are visible.

AcruxCore stores both as feedback rows on the trace. The source field separates the end user from the reviewer:

This is the first step where the code differs a lot. Each tool can make a dataset, but only one builds it from feedback in one call.

In LangSmith, a run query finds the thumbs-down runs. Then createExamples copies them into a dataset. You decide where the reviewer's comment goes. We put it in the reference output:

LangSmith can also do this without code. An automation rule in the UI can add runs that match a filter to a dataset.

In Langfuse, the scores API finds the thumbs-down traces. Two details cost us a run each. A boolean score is filtered with value: "false", not 0. And the trace ID is returned only when you ask for the subject field group:

In Phoenix, getSpans can't filter by annotation. So the script lists the app's spans, reads their annotations, and keeps the ones with a thumbs-down. Then createDataset takes the examples. We kept the source span ID in the metadata.

In AcruxCore, buildFromFeedback takes the reviewer's feedback IDs. Each comment becomes that example's grading criteria, and the input comes from the variables captured on the trace:

Each tool scored the first version of the prompt on its own dataset. The judge read the reviewer's comment and the router's reply, and returned 1 for a match and 0 for a mismatch.

LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix each have an experiment runner that takes your evaluator function, and we gave all three the same judge prompt. AcruxCore grades with its built-in judge inside the optimize run. All four used the same judge model.

The first version fails almost every reviewed case. That is expected, because these are the cases users marked wrong. A score of 1 means the app model got that case right on the second call, or the judge accepted a wrong reply. We didn't check which.

The judge can also run without your code. Besides the in-code evaluator we used, Langfuse has a managed LLM-as-a-judge that runs on production traces and on experiments, with your own judge prompt. LangSmith has online evaluators, which you set up in its UI, for the same job.

An optimizer reads the failing cases and the reviewer comments, writes new versions of the prompt, and scores each one with the judge. This is the step that turns feedback into a better prompt, and it's where the four tools differ most.

LangSmith's API reference lists an optimize prompt job (POST /api/v1/repos/optimize-job). It takes a prompt name, a dataset name, evaluator IDs and an algorithm, promptim or demo. No method in the TypeScript SDK wraps it, so we called it with fetch. We gave it a code evaluator that checks the reply against the reviewer's correct answer.

The job was accepted and reported successful about ten seconds later. It ran no experiment on the dataset. With auto_commit set to true, it added no new commit to the prompt. We found no guide for this endpoint, so we may have configured it wrongly, but the job record and its logs gave no error to act on.

LangSmith's documented route to prompt optimization is promptim, which its README calls an experimental library. It is a Python package with a CLI, and it can add a human review step through LangSmith annotation queues.

Langfuse has no optimizer. Its blog shows a workflow with Claude Code agent skills that reads Langfuse data and edits the prompt. That workflow runs in a coding agent, not in your app's code.

Phoenix's Optimize Prompts Automatically tutorial uses Arize's prompt-learning package, which you clone from GitHub and install with pip. The Phoenix TypeScript client has no optimizer export, and the tutorial runs outside Phoenix. Arize's commercial platform, Arize AX, has prompt optimization built in.

For Python teams, there are more options: Opik's Agent Optimizer and DSPy both rewrite prompts from a dataset and a metric. This article covers TypeScript.

Its optimize run takes the dataset and drafts three rewrites of the production prompt. It runs each rewrite and the current production version on every example, and the judge grades all of them:

The three rewrites tied, so we took candidate A. Its rationale: "Added explicit priority rules for each category and clarified that access-blocking issues are P0, billing errors are P1, and feature requests are P2, overriding tone/urgency." The Improve a prompt from feedback guide covers the dashboard version of this step.

A rewrite that passes the cases it was built from can still fail on new tickets. So we ran both prompts on the 15 holdout tickets, three times each, outside every tool. The script calls the model directly and checks the category and the priority against the expected answer. No judge is involved.

Both come from one line in the rewrite: "P1 for bugs without workaround." That rule isn't in the team's policy, and no reviewer wrote it. The optimizer made it up to explain the examples it had. There was also no outage in the traffic set's thumbs-down cases, because the first version routed that one correctly. So no comment ever said that a full outage is P0.

The optimizer learns only from what users marked wrong. Anything they never marked stays a guess, and the rationale won't tell you which rules are guesses. Read the rewritten prompt before you promote it.

In LangSmith, Langfuse and Phoenix, shipping a new version is a tag or label move. We showed those calls in the prompt-management article. Here there was no rewrite to ship, so the step didn't apply.

In AcruxCore, promotion commits the candidate as a new prompt version and moves the production alias to it. Nothing else moves:

The app renders production on every call, so the next ticket used version 2 without a deploy.

Use LangSmith if your team already works in it and writes Python. Feedback, queues, datasets and experiments all work from TypeScript, and promptim covers optimization from Python.

Use Langfuse if you want a managed judge that runs on live traces with your own prompt. Plan to write the dataset glue code and the rewrite step yourself.

Use Phoenix if you already trace with OpenTelemetry and annotate spans. Plan to move to Python, or to Arize AX, for optimization.

Use AcruxCore if you want all six steps from TypeScript in one tool, including the rewrite. Keep a human on the promote button.

#10810. Nvidia’s first earnings call without Jensen Huang had one message: It’s business as usual

Nvidia on Tuesday held its first earnings call in its 33-year history without cofounder Jensen Huang, who resigned five days ago after a scandal involving an extramarital affair with a suspected Chinese spy. After reporting strong third-quarter numbers, interim CEO Colette Kress (who has been Nvidia’s CFO since 2013) kept the call focused on Nvidia’s immediate business concerns. 

Analysts wanted to know what the company would say about the succession plan after Huang’s departure, and about the future of Nvidia’s pivotal role as the company powering the AI race. 

Most of the salient information about Huang was given at the front of the call by Harvey Jones, Nvidia’s chair of the board. Jones said Nvidia doesn’t intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO, providing a clapback to the growing contingent on social media lobbying for the ousted CEO’s return. Jones said the executive search firm Heidrick & Struggles is handling the search for a new CEO, but that Nvidia is looking at both internal and external candidates for the job. 

“The board is acting deliberately and in the best interest of the company’s employees and investors,” Jones said. “With that we will take no further questions about the circumstances of Jensen’s resignation.”

The numbers

Kress then took over the call and kept things focused on Nvidia’s numbers. Kress has run Nvidia’s earnings calls for years, but always alongside Huang, who set the company’s vision and did much of its dealmaking with customers and partners. Today her main job was to report the quarterly numbers, and convince investors that the world’s most important AI chip company can run without its founder.

The numbers, at least, are good. Nvidia reported $115.8 billion in revenue for its fiscal third quarter, well above Wall Street’s roughly $109 billion forecast, and projected $130 billion for the current quarter. Investors expected a beat. 

Kress said Nvidia has seen “explosive” adoption of its latest Vera Rubin computing platform, which includes a Rubin GPU and a Vera CPU. “Our momentum is real,” she said. “Our ambition will not change, our passion for innovation will not change.” 

Revenue for the quarter came in at $115.8 billion, about $6.5 billion higher than the roughly $109 billion analysts expected. Adjusted earnings were $2.68 per share, 21 cents above the $2.47 consensus. 

But all that happened during a third quarter that ended October 25—that is, before the Huang scandal broke. That’s why analysts were more concerned about the guidance numbers for a future without Huang. Some analysts had even questioned whether Nvidia would venture to give a full year 2028 guidance. 

But it did. For the short term, Nvidia forecast fourth-quarter revenue of $130 billion. The company had previously said it expects to see 70% growth in 2028, but Kress said its moving that guidance up to 75% growth, citing continued strong demand for AI infrastructure, including Vera Rubin, and despite the chance that chip exports to China will constrict somewhat in the wake of the Huang scandal.

That may have settled some analysts’ nerves about the AI sell-off that started in October, which was driven by the fear that Big Tech companies would slow their spending on AI data centers. 

Why this call mattered

Huang, who cofounded Nvidia in 1993, resigned on November 12 amid two scandals. The first involved an alleged affair with a woman U.S. intelligence believe may have been spying for China. Beijing has strongly denied the allegations. Nvidia’s board has opened an internal investigation into whether company or national security secrets were compromised. Huang stepped down shortly after reports of a second alleged affair, this one with Kimberly Powell, the company’s vice president of healthcare (and the CEO’s direct report).

The board made Kress interim CEO. She also stays on as CFO until a permanent CEO is named. Longtime director Harvey Jones became Nvidia’s first board chair. 

The stock was already falling before the scandal. Nvidia closed at a record $239.24 on October 6. Shares then fell on fears that AI spending would slow, and fell further as the Huang story unfolded. By Tuesday the stock was below $154, its lowest level since mid-2025. That’s a loss of about $2 trillion in market value. That’s why Wall Street was listening to Kress so closely. 

Before the call, analyst Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives predicted Kress would talk very little about Huang, that her main job would be to “restore order and give the Street confidence in the Nvidia story going forward,” he told Fox Business. 

And that’s exactly what happened. After the brief section at the front, the call had a business-as-usual tone, complete with a blizzard of numbers showing that Nvidia—as of the third quarter, at least—continues to sit at the center of the AI world.

#10805. Cockpit-Simulator 737X CDU (v2) Review: An Essential Component for Flight Sim Enthusiasts

The CS 737X CDU (v2) brings an authentic, premium-build Boeing 737 Control Display Unit to the home cockpit, but its high price makes it a niche product for serious flight sim enthusiasts.

Accurate 1:1 replica of the real Boeing 737 Control Display Unit

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Thanks to the popularity of Microsoft Flight Simulator 2020, followed by Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024, a growing number of companies have entered the flight simulation peripheral market — offering everything from budget-friendly products to high-end equipment designed for professional and home cockpits.

Cockpit-Master and Cockpit-Simulator were jointly founded in 2018 to develop and manufacture flight simulation equipment. In 2024, the company was split into two divisions, with Cockpit-Simulator focusing on more premium, professional-grade equipment.

Today, we are looking at the CS 737X CDU v2, a 1:1 replica of the Boeing 737 Control Display Unit (CDU). We will examine its build quality, how closely it replicates the real 737 CDU, and how well it integrates with Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024.

Before taking a look at the product itself, let’s first look at the real 737 Control Display Unit (CDU) and explain its functionality.

The CDU is the primary interface pilots use to interact with the aircraft’s Flight Management System (FMS). The FMS can be thought of as the aircraft’s “brain,” handling a wide range of navigation and performance-related tasks. Pilots use the CDU to enter and modify the flight plan, while the FMS calculates the lateral route and vertical profile of the flight. It also manages performance calculations, monitors fuel and flight progress, and provides guidance to the autopilot.

The CDU features a display screen with six line select keys on either side. These keys allow pilots to select and enter information into specific fields on the display, including data entered into the scratchpad. The CDU also includes dedicated function keys for tasks such as accessing performance information and entering instrument departures and approaches. Below the display is an alphanumeric keypad, which pilots use to enter text and other data before transferring it to the selected field using the line select keys.

Unlike other consumer grade CDUs on the market, the CS 737X CDU v2 uses a full-metal, CNC-machined aluminum shell.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The buttons are made of silicone rubber, which also closely replicates what we felt in our time on a 737 simulator.

The display is where we noticed the biggest departure from the real 737 CDU. The real unit uses a thick, chemically strengthened, anti-glare optical glass cover, whereas the CS 737X CDU v2 uses a standard commercial display. As a result, there is some glare, although the display itself is excellent and offers very wide viewing angles. Despite this difference, the visual presentation is remarkably close to that of the real 737 CDU. The font styling, image sharpness, and color reproduction all closely match what you would see on the real flight deck.

The CS 737X CDU v2 also features a handle, which on the real aircraft is used for the maintenance crew to remove the CDU. This isn’t entirely necessary in a home setup, but it is a nice touch.

The unit can be easily placed on a desk using the included desktop stand, or integrated directly into a larger cockpit setup.

The weight, rigidity, and metallic construction closely replicate the feel of the CDU we experienced in a 737 simulator. The silicone buttons also require a firm, deliberate press to actuate, helping prevent accidental double-inputs during turbulence. This tactile resistance closely matches the experience of using the real unit. Manually entering a long route, rather than importing it from SimBrief, will give your fingers a workout – and will give you a newfound appreciation for the import button.

For our testing inside of Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024, we used the PMDG 737-800. We flew the following routes:

This allowed us to examine how the CS 737X CDU v2 handles routes of various duration.

For hardware, the device only requires a USB connection. It also requires the CockpitSimulator Bridge, which needs to be left open throughout the flight. This can be downloaded from the official Cockpit-Simulator site.

The overall feel of the unit, combined with the firm, deliberate key presses, adds another layer of immersion. More importantly, having a physical CDU means we can operate it directly — rather than panning the camera toward the virtual CDU, zooming in, and navigating it with a mouse. This makes interacting with the FMS considerably faster and more intuitive.

This is particularly useful when ATC gives you a direct-to instruction shortly after takeoff, while you are still hand-flying the aircraft. Performing the direct-to operation on a physical CDU is much easier than interacting with the virtual CDU, as it allows you to make the necessary inputs without taking your eyes away from the Primary Flight Display.

One of the first things that stood out was the extremely low input latency. Our key presses were reflected almost instantly on both the unit’s physical display and the virtual CDU inside Microsoft Flight Simulator 2024, making the two feel effectively synchronized.

We were also able to switch seamlessly between the various CDU pages, even with longer flight plans loaded into the unit. Everything shown on the virtual CDU was accurately reproduced on the hardware, including aircraft-specific options for the PMDG 737-800 we were flying. The information was presented with accurate fonts, colors, and formatting, making the physical CDU feel like a natural extension of the one inside the simulator.

The CS 737X CDU v2 is the most premium and immersive Boeing 737 CDU we have tested. From the weight and feel of its metallic construction to the tactile resistance of the silicone keys and the accurate rendering of fonts and colors on the display, nearly every aspect of the unit contributes to an authentic experience. It's a fitting addition to any high-end home cockpit setup.

This level of construction and attention to detail comes at a price: the CS 737X CDU v2 retails for $799, which means it will likely be out of reach for many flight simmers. But if you're building a dedicated, high-end cockpit and looking for a CDU that closely replicates the experience of the real aircraft, it's an excellent addition.

#10802. Friendly Fraud Prevention for Developers: What to Log, Block and Automate

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Friendly fraud prevention comes down to three jobs you can do in code: log the right checkout data, block risky payments before they settle, and automate your response when a dispute still lands. Get the first one wrong and you can't win the dispute later, no matter how good your case is.

In this tutorial, we'll build each piece in Node.js in a way that works with any payment service provider (PSP). Your code owns the data and the logic, and a thin adapter talks to Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com or whoever processes your payments.

Friendly fraud happens when a customer makes a real purchase, then disputes it with their card issuer. They might claim they didn't authorize it, that it never arrived, or that they canceled. Sometimes it's honest confusion. A spouse used the saved card, or the charge showed up under a name they didn't recognize. Sometimes it's a free refund with extra steps.

Either way, the money leaves your account. A dispute (also called a chargeback) reverses the payment, and your processor charges a dispute fee on top. Lose too many, and the card networks put your account into a monitoring program with higher fees.

It's also common. Visa puts friendly fraud at around 20% of fraudulent disputes globally, and up to 30% for high-volume online merchants. Mastercard goes further: it says first-party fraud makes up 75% of the fraud online businesses experience.

So why is this your problem and not finance's? Because the evidence that wins these disputes is created at checkout, by your code. If your app never stored the customer's IP address, device ID or login history, nobody can produce it three months later when the dispute arrives.

Friendly fraud can be stopped at four points, and each one is a different piece of code:

Most guides only cover stages 1 and 4. The rest of this tutorial walks through all four.

Log four things on every order: the customer's IP address, a device ID, their account ID and the shipping address. Those are the data elements Visa's Compelling Evidence 3.0 (CE3.0) rules check when you fight an "unauthorized" fraud dispute.

Here's how CE3.0 works. You win the dispute if you can show two earlier, undisputed purchases on the same card, made 120 to 365 days before the disputed one. All three purchases must share at least two data elements, and one of them must be the IP address or device ID. In plain terms: same card, same person, same device, and they never complained before.

Most PSPs support CE3.0, including Stripe, Adyen, Checkout.com and Airwallex. But none of them can submit data you never captured. And because the window reaches back a full year, you need to keep these logs for at least 13 months (Mastercard's rule, below).

Mastercard has its own version, called First-Party Trust, for disputes filed under reason code 4837 (no cardholder authorization). It asks for one data point from each of three categories: a device factor (IP address, device ID or fingerprint), a delivery factor (shipping address, email or phone) and a different identity factor (such as account login ID or billing address). On the dispute route, Mastercard calls a lookup service that you host, with named fields such as deviceIpAddress, orderDeviceId, orderEmail and userIdName. That service has to answer in about 2.5 seconds on average and search 13 months of sales data.

First-Party Trust is live in the US. Per Mastercard's October 2026 announcement, it reaches Eastern Europe, the Middle East and Africa from October 2026 and the rest of Europe from July 2027. If you sell into Europe, the logging work is due now.

The good news: one evidence table serves both networks. Capture the data once, then keep the eligibility checks for Visa and Mastercard separate.

Start with a table that stores this data per order, separate from your PSP:

Then capture it in your checkout route. If your app sits behind a proxy or CDN, set Express's trust proxy so req.ip returns the customer's address, not your load balancer's:

The psp object is your adapter: one small module per provider that maps its API to the same shape. Swap providers, and this route doesn't change.

For digital products and SaaS, log usage too. Login timestamps, downloads and feature activity prove the customer used what they paid for, which beats "I never received it" claims.

The cheapest dispute is the one that never happens. Three checkout changes do most of the work: 3D Secure on risky payments, a billing descriptor customers recognize, and a few rules that stop obvious abuse.

Use 3D Secure where risk is higher. 3D Secure (3DS) asks the cardholder to confirm the payment with their bank, often through an app prompt. For authenticated payments, liability for fraud disputes generally shifts from you to the card issuer. Every major PSP supports it, and most let you request it per payment instead of forcing it on everyone. Forcing it everywhere adds friction and costs conversions, so trigger it on risk signals.

Make your billing descriptor obvious. The descriptor is the text on the customer's card statement. If it reads PMT*SRV 8812 instead of your product name, expect "I don't recognize this charge" disputes. Use the brand name customers know, and add a support URL or phone number where your PSP allows it. If you sell several products, use a dynamic descriptor that names each one.

Add a risk check before you charge. Most PSPs include a rules engine, but a small check in your own code keeps the logic portable:

Store the signals array with the order. If a dispute arrives later, you'll know whether the payment looked risky at the time, which helps you decide whether to fight it or accept it.

Keep the rules few and specific. A rule that blocks 5% of good customers to stop 1% of bad ones costs more than the disputes it prevents.

Yes, if you ship physical goods. The window between a successful payment and the moment an order ships is the cheapest place to stop friendly fraud: nothing has left the warehouse, so canceling costs you nothing but the sale. Most fraud tools decide at checkout and never look again.

The pattern is a fulfillment gate. Instead of sending every paid order straight to your warehouse or 3PL, route it through a decision step first:

The verify path does two jobs. Legitimate buyers confirm and their order ships. Buyers planning to dispute see that the store is watching, and a confirmed verification becomes evidence if they dispute anyway.

The hard part is screenOrder. Your own data only shows how a customer behaved with you. A buyer who has filed "item not received" disputes at 10 other stores looks brand new on their first order with yours. That's why post-purchase screening works best on shared data across many merchants.

Chargeflow Prevent is one example of this kind of friendly fraud prevention. It scores each order after checkout and before fulfillment, links device, IP, email and payment behavior across a network of 20,000+ merchants, and returns approve, verify or cancel. Today it runs as a Shopify app, with no checkout changes. A public Prevent API isn't available yet, so on other stacks you'd build the gate above with your own rules and data.

Build an order lookup endpoint. When a cardholder taps "I don't recognize this" in their banking app, the issuer can now ask the merchant for order details before a dispute is filed. Visa does this through Verifi Order Insight, and Mastercard through Ethoca Consumer Clarity. Show the customer a receipt with your logo, the items and the delivery date, and many disputes are never filed.

One endpoint can serve both networks, plus Mastercard's First-Party Trust dispute route. Mastercard's published service levels for this kind of lookup are strict:

The tricky part is matching. Issuers often send partial data, so match on whatever arrives: acquirer reference number (ARN), authorization code, date, amount, currency, and card BIN plus last four digits.

On top of lookups, Visa and many PSPs offer rules-based auto-refunds. Verifi's Rapid Dispute Resolution (RDR) and Visa Resolve refund pre-disputes that match rules you set, such as "refund anything under $25". You don't write code for these, but you do pick the thresholds, so treat them like the REFUND_THRESHOLD below.

If you sell subscriptions, many friendly fraud disputes aren't about stolen cards at all. The customer forgot about a renewal, couldn't find the cancel button, or thinks they canceled. Each of these has a code fix.

Here's a daily job that sends renewal reminders and records that each one went out:

That evidenceLog row matters. When a customer later claims they never knew about the renewal, you can show the exact date you told them.

Automate two things: refunding payments that get flagged before they become chargebacks, and building evidence packets for the ones you fight. Both run off webhooks, and both have hard deadlines that humans miss.

Card networks can warn you before a dispute is filed. Visa runs this through Verifi and Mastercard through Ethoca, and many PSPs pass these alerts or their own early fraud warnings to you by webhook. Refunding at this stage costs you the sale, but you skip the dispute fee and the dispute doesn't count against your ratio.

The threshold is a business call. Refunding a $20 order is cheaper than fighting it; a $900 order with strong evidence may be worth contesting.

When a fraud dispute arrives, check whether it qualifies for CE3.0 by looking for two earlier undisputed orders on the same card that share data with the disputed one:

If you get two matches, pass them to your PSP's CE3.0 evidence fields. On Stripe that's evidence.enhanced_evidence.visa_compelling_evidence_3; other PSPs have their own equivalents. Your adapter handles the mapping. If not, fall back to standard evidence: the IP, receipt, usage logs, renewal reminders and terms acceptance you've been logging all along.

Always submit before the deadline your PSP returns with the dispute. A late response is an automatic loss.

The code above covers the basics. A production system also needs evidence templates per reason code, network deadlines per card brand, retries, and win-rate tracking. That maintenance multiplies with every PSP you add.

PSP-native tools only see that PSP's data, so they miss the usage logs, renewal reminders and support threads in your own app. A dedicated platform connects to your PSPs and lets you send that data in.

Here's what the buy path looks like with the Chargeflow API. Chargeflow ingests disputes from connected PSPs (Stripe, PayPal, Adyen, Checkout.com, Braintree and 20+ others), fires a dispute.created webhook, and you enrich the dispute with the evidence you logged:

Chargeflow then builds the response and submits it to the PSP before the deadline. Pre-dispute alerts work the same way: Chargeflow can refund flagged transactions for you, or send alerts.created and alerts.transaction.linked webhooks so your code issues the refund and reports the result to POST /alerts/{id}/outcome.

Using Claude, ChatGPT or Cursor to build this? Chargeflow publishes its whole developer documentation as one machine-readable file, docs.chargeflow.io/llms-full.txt. Point your assistant at it and it can write the webhook handler and field mapping against the real API, not a guess.

Then your merchants get the disputes, and you need to give them a dispute product without building a dispute team. Chargeflow Connect is built for this. It exposes the same prevention, alerts and recovery engine to your platform in a few delivery models:

EaaS is the most developer-friendly entry point. You POST /public/2025-04-01/evidence with a merchant account_id and a dispute, get back an in_progress record, and receive an evidence.ready webhook with the PDF link, typically in about 30 seconds. Store the evidence id, not the URL, because the download link expires after 7 days. You can run it on auto-pilot (Chargeflow generates and submits) or co-pilot (your merchant approves first).

Don't wait for a customer to dispute a charge to find out your evidence pipeline is broken. Most PSPs give you test cards that create disputes on demand.

One more check before launch: confirm your webhook endpoints verify signatures and return 200 fast. PSPs and Chargeflow retry deliveries that fail, and a slow handler can process the same dispute twice.

If you only do one thing this week, start logging IP address and device ID on every order. Everything else builds on that data.

True fraud is when a criminal uses a stolen card. Friendly fraud is when the real cardholder makes a purchase, then disputes it. Prevention differs: true fraud is stopped at checkout with risk checks and 3DS, while friendly fraud is won with evidence after the fact.

Mostly, yes. Both describe a real cardholder disputing a legitimate charge. "Chargeback fraud" usually implies the customer did it on purpose, while friendly fraud also covers honest mistakes, like a forgotten subscription or a family member's purchase. Visa calls it first-party misuse, and Mastercard calls it first-party fraud.

Check the reason code first. For "unauthorized" fraud claims, submit Visa CE3.0 or Mastercard First-Party Trust evidence if the customer qualifies. For "not received" or "canceled" claims, submit delivery proof, usage logs and your cancellation records. For low-value orders, accepting the dispute or refunding on an alert is often cheaper than fighting it.

Partly. 3DS generally shifts liability for fraud disputes to the issuer, so "I didn't authorize this" claims on authenticated payments rarely cost you. It doesn't help with "item not received" or "I canceled" disputes, which need delivery proof and cancellation records.

No. CE3.0 needs two undisputed purchases on the same card made 120 to 365 days earlier, so it only protects returning customers. For first-time buyers, rely on 3DS and standard evidence.

Yes. Mastercard's First-Party Trust program covers reason code 4837 disputes. It needs one device, one delivery and one extra identity data point, and Mastercard checks them against the cardholder's past purchases. The same evidence table you build for Visa feeds both.

It depends on the stage. At checkout, your PSP's 3D Secure and rules engine. After payment, post-purchase screening such as Chargeflow Prevent holds risky orders before they ship. Before a chargeback, Verifi Order Insight and Ethoca Consumer Clarity show cardholders the order details, while Verifi and Ethoca alerts (offered by many PSPs and by Chargeflow Alerts) and RDR let you refund early. After a chargeback, Visa CE3.0, Mastercard First-Party Trust and dispute automation tools submit the evidence. For developers, the Chargeflow API covers the alert and dispute stages across PSPs, and Chargeflow Connect does the same for platforms.

Darrel Dai wants to know the exact reasons why Jensen resigned.

Just days after Jensen Huang suddenly resigned from his position at Nvidia, an Nvidia Robot Research Engineer said that he would leave the company as well. But more than just showing support for the former CEO with a generic “I stand with Jensen” message, Darrel Dai laid out several reasons for the decision on X.

I’ve decided to leave NVIDIA. There are a few things I want to say. As a robotics engineer at NVIDIA, I have seen Jensen as much more than the company’s CEO. I admire his willingness to invest in a future that was not yet obvious. He committed to CUDA long before the deep learning boom and positioned NVIDIA for AI before it became everyone’s priority. Those decisions helped build the infrastructure that powers today’s AI. I have seen that same vision in physical AI: investing in robotics, sharing open-source tools and projects, and building computing platforms that bring AI into the physical world. This is the work I do every day—and a future I believe in. But what matters most to me is the culture he helped build. Engineers are encouraged to explore, take risks, and learn from mistakes. Jensen reads employees’ Top 5 emails, follows their work, and engages directly. In a company this large, that connection matters. It makes an individual engineer feel that their ideas can still be heard. That is why this has been so disappointing. I do not have all the facts surrounding the allegations, and I cannot prejudge the investigation. My admiration for Jensen does not put him above scrutiny. What I find difficult to accept is having to come to terms with his departure before receiving an explanation that helps me understand it. What this means for our direction, our culture, and the people doing the work remains unclear to me. I have felt that engineers’ perspectives matter here. On this decision, I have not felt heard. That gap has been more painful than I expected. I understand that some information cannot be shared immediately. But that does not erase the feeling of being left outside a decision that affects the company we help build every day. A company’s greatness comes from its leadership, its governance, and the people who turn its ambitions into reality. Employees’ trust and sense of belonging are part of that, too. Jensen’s vision and the engineering culture he shaped were important reasons I wanted to stay. His departure led me to decide that my own chapter here should also come to a close. I leave with sadness and gratitude. The colleagues I have worked with, and the work we believed in together, will always matter to me. These are my personal views.November 15, 2026

“What I find difficult to accept is having to come to terms with his departure before receiving an explanation that helps me understand it. What this means for our direction, our culture, and the people doing the work remains unclear to me,” the engineer posted. “I have felt that engineers’ perspectives matter here. On this decision, I have not felt heard. That gap has been more painful than I expected.”

It seems that Jensen Huang has gained the respect of the people working under him, with many calling for his reinstatement despite the many controversies he is currently facing. There’s also an online petition demanding that the Nvidia board “Bring Jensen Back,” with over 150,000 signatures at the time of writing.

Nvidia is centered around Jensen Huang, with the former chief executive reportedly having a small army of 60 people directly reporting to him. But despite his busy schedule and legendary persona, Dai said that “Jensen reads employees’ Top 5 emails, follows their work, and engages directly,” and that “It makes an individual engineer feel that their ideas can still be heard.” Dai also pointed out that “employees’ trust and sense of belonging” are part of what makes a company great. More importantly, Nvidia is known as one of the tech companies with the most generous pay packages. Jensen was even quoted saying, “I pay my employees as much as I can.”

It remains unclear what the former Nvidia CEO will do after all these issues. Elon Musk has already said on X that “it would be an honor” to hire Jensen Huang, especially as he’s embarking on his own ambitious chipmaking journey. As for the employees left behind at Nvidia, some are staying and have shown their full support to interim CEO Colette Kress, while many more want him back. However, there has also been talk that various Silicon Valley tech and semiconductor firms are aggressively attempting to poach Nvidians, although these are just rumors so far.

You won't worry about having a flashlight until you need it. Looking deep inside a PC case, trying to repair under a cabinet, lost power during a power cut, or stuck outside in the dark: you'll want a light source you can rely on that isn't going to be a drain on your phone's battery.

Cleaning is one of those PC maintenance tasks that we've all probably left until our PC cases are fill with dust and grime, not to mention our keyboards and mice. A good set of cleaning tools, especially an air duster, can make all the difference. Blast it all out, and give it a nice clean brush down, and you'll help to keep your machine running in top working order.

These tools are versatile, so you can use them whenever you need to defeat some dust and grime, whether it's your car dashboard or your project room.

This compact air duster can move air at up to 45 m/s with its 150,000 RPM turbofan. A rechargeable battery gives up to 100 minutes of use time and supports USB Type-C charging. The MF100 includes five nozzles and two brush tools to help clean your PC equipment.

Designed for electronics, these two 10-oz bottles of compressed air will blow the dust, dead skin, and last week's sandwich from your keyboard and help prevent the inside of your PC case from becoming a biohazard. Designed for electronics, these four, 10oz bottle of compressed air will blow the dust, dead skin and last week's sandwich from your keyboard and help prevent the inside of your PC case becoming a biohazard.

A brush is an underrated part of any toolkit. You've got every type of brush you'll need included in this kit to deep clean your PC, keyboard and whatever else needs cleaning in your rig.

You'll want Isopropyl Alcohol on hand as it's safe to use with electronics. Buying in bulk and decanting into dollar store spray bottles is the smart way to use it, from a cost point of view.

This rechargeable 110,000 RPM electric air duster comes with three adjustable speed settings, and about 20-40 minutes of continuous cleaning power (displayed on the LED screen) from the 7500 MAh battery. It comes with five nozzles and four soft brushes for any task.

This isn't an exhaustive list, but a good selection of the tools and accessories that I reckon every PC builder, maker, and hobbyist needs at their disposal.

You won't always need them. Some of them aren't glamorous or exciting, but they'll all indispensable parts of your toolkit that, once you've got them close to hand, you'll come to rely on them.

#10790. More game developers are starting to use generative AI

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

The GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey shows an increase in AI usage among game developers.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

More video game developers are using generative AI tools in their jobs compared to last year, according to the GDC Festival of Gaming’s latest annual industry survey. Forty percent of developers surveyed say they use it “as part of their job,” an increase from 36 percent in 2025. Usage was higher with game developers that work on free-to-play games at 58 percent and mobile-only studio workers at 70 percent.

The survey is being published as the game industry continues to reckon with generative AI tools. While many game developers, particularly indie developers, have spoken out strongly against the technology, AI is at the center of indie awards conversations in part because Big Walk developer House House “sparingly” used LLMs to assist with technical challenges in the “final stretch” of development. AI is also increasingly being used to reverse-engineer retro games. And in a post on Thursday, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma seemed to indicate that AI will be central to Xbox’s future, saying that “A small team will be able to build worlds that today demand hundreds of developers and hundreds of millions of dollars.”

Seventy three percent of the survey’s respondents said they used AI for research and brainstorming, while 62 percent used it for data analysis and summarization, and 56 percent for code generation and debugging. Twenty two percent of respondents used generative AI for concept art or visual ideation, and 8 percent used it for production art or final game assets.

The survey also included grim numbers about layoffs, with 34 percent of respondents saying they have been laid off sometime in the past two years.

#10788. What AI Gets Wrong About the Front End, and Why You Still Need to Know

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The product carousel looks finished. The image fills the card, the price sits under the product name, and the arrow on the right takes you to the next item. The demo goes smoothly.

Then you put the mouse down. You press Tab, and focus skips the arrows entirely. If you rely on a keyboard, the first product is the only one you can reach.

That’s a front-end bug hiding in plain sight. It’s also a good illustration of where AI fits into front-end development. A prompt can describe what a component should look like and what should happen on click. The implementation still has to account for how people reach that control, activate it, and understand what changed.

Imagine asking an AI coding assistant for this:

Build a React product carousel with previous and next arrows. Show one product at a time and wrap around at the ends.

Here’s an illustrative excerpt from the sort of implementation that can satisfy a quick mouse-driven demo:

With some CSS, this can look exactly like the arrow in the design. The click handler advances the slide. A screenshot shows the right layout.

But a plain div sits outside the normal Tab sequence and has no native button semantics or keyboard activation. Adding tabIndex={0} would make it reachable while leaving Enter and Space behavior to be implemented separately. The browser already provides those behaviors through a native button.

The real omission was in the requirement. “Show arrows” described the visual design. “Let someone reach and operate both controls with a keyboard” describes behavior you can test.

Here’s a small version of the same carousel. The product data is fixed for the example, and the slides change only when the user requests it.

The buttons have visible labels and work with both Enter and Space. When a slide changes, focus stays on the button you pressed, so you can press it again to keep browsing. The status message gives assistive technology a way to announce the new product and its position. Only the current product link is rendered, so Tab never lands on a link from a hidden slide.

Those are deliberate interaction choices. W3C’s carousel pattern provides guidance on using native buttons, keeping focus on the slide controls after activation, and handling automatic rotation when a design requires it. W3C Carousel Pattern

I’d revise the original prompt like this:

Build a manual React product carousel. Use native Previous and Next buttons. Both must work with Tab, Enter, and Space. Keep focus on the activated button after a slide change. Announce the new product name and position through a status message. Keep only the current product link in the Tab sequence. Show what happens when the user advances past the last product.

The extra detail also gives you a review plan. For this carousel, I’d use four checks:

AI can help draft automated tests for the active link and wraparound behavior. I’d still run the keyboard and screen reader checks on the integrated page. A global stylesheet can erase a visible focus outline. A surrounding component can introduce an extra tab stop. The experience depends on the page the customer actually uses.

A checkout form can look complete with sample data and lose the customer’s entries when a request fails. A responsive header can match the design at two widths and overlap when a translated label is longer. A component can render correctly in isolation and shift the page when a slow image loads.

Each example gives AI a concrete job. Ask it to preserve form values after a failed request, check the header with longer labels, or reserve space for the image. Then reproduce the condition yourself. Knowing which conditions matter is part of knowing the front end.

The next time AI gives you a carousel that looks ready, start the review with your hands off the mouse. Tab to Next, press Space twice, and see where focus lands. That short test gives you evidence about how the component works for someone who relies on a keyboard.

#10787. Developer creates a 100% valid ZIP file that doubles as a BIOS-bootable operating system

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The original definition of "hacking" was making hardware and software do something it wasn't supposed to.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hacking stories these days mostly involve someone using an army of AI agents to find vulnerabilities in networks and software that were carefully hidden in plain sight. Today, for a change, we have a story about hacking in its original meaning — twisting computing artifacts into purposes not in their design spec. David Oberhollenzer, aka Goliath, a greybeard in spirit if not in shade, crafted a 100% valid ZIP file that's simultaneously an archive, BIOS-bootable, and a Multiboot-compatible OS image.

You can unpack the ZIP file just like any other, boot it using Linux's GRUB, QEMU, or any other Multiboot-compatible loader (even if imaged to different media), or alternatively, perform the finest party trick: image-write it to a floppy and boot from it directly from BIOS on any compatible PC, without modification.

Much like me, that sequence of words may be triggering a head-tilt reminiscent of a confused puppy, so we'll explain. The first thing you need to know is that unlike most file types, the metadata (in this case, file directory) of a ZIP is actually at the end of the archive. Up to that point, the data is comprised of every compressed file in sequence.

The file packs all have a predictable structure: "PK" followed by bytes 03 04, metadata (including name), some extra/comment fields, and then the file's contents. Notably, any file can be stored uncompressed, and Goliath made use of that for the very first file, called ZIPHEAD.COM — remember, this is the start of the ZIP archive.

Goliath said that it was "easy" to make the ZIP file a valid Multiboot system image. A valid image only needs to contain bytes 0x1BADB002 in the first 8 KiB, followed by some flags and a checksum. It was trivial to place that datum in ZIPHEAD's data, along with the location of where the bootloader lies.

Grub or QEMU don't care about anything else in the image, as once they have the initialization information, from that point it's the bootloader's problem. The actual bootloader, predictably, is in ZIPHEAD's uncompressed data, and the image will happily boot directly, or, say, from a floppy.

Making the same exact archive bootable via a standard PC BIOS took a little more work and some clever shenanigans. Making a disk bootable is simple enough: the BIOS reads the first exact 512 bytes of the disk's contents, and if they end with bytes 0x55AA, then it's bootable, and the BIOS starts executing whatever code is the first byte.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Placing 0x55AA precisely at position 510 of the ZIP, already in ZIPHEAD's contents, is once again simple, and won't corrupt the Multiboot image, as if you'll recall, the Multiboot spec is flexible with data locations. But you may wonder: once the BIOS runs code in sequence starting from the ZIP's first byte, then ZIPHEAD's header ("PK" + 0x0304, plus all metadata) surely is garbage for practical purposes, and not proper code? Well, yes, but actually no.

The first four bytes represent CPU instructions that don't write anything to memory, so they're harmless, other than a push-to-stack. The next few belong to ZIPHEAD's metadata, and Goliath massaged those values to represent instructions that would either be innocuous or perform operations he could use, namely using the modification time bytes as instructions to jump further down the data.

Sounds simple, but the actual process of choosing bytes that represent (1) non-crashy CPU instructions, (2) useful code for setting up the boot process, and (3) still represent a valid file header in a ZIP archive is far trickier than the above. Do go and check out the full blog post if you want all the details.

Download the Tom's Hardware app for up-to-the-minute updates on iOS and Android. Also follow Tom's Hardware on Google News, or add us as a preferred source, to get our latest news, analysis, & reviews in your feeds.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10784. 25 years later, Xbox is trying to reinvent itself again

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Xbox has always been about trying something new, so what does Microsoft do next?﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft has spent the past 25 years trying to shake up the game console market. The original Xbox, which launched on November 15th in 2001, took on an industry dominated by Sony and Nintendo. Twenty-five years later, Xbox finds itself in a similar position. It’s not starting from scratch this time around, but to reach another 25-year milestone, it needs to do something radically different.

The original Xbox was radically different. Born out of a fear of Sony putting a PC in the living room, Microsoft built its first game console around familiar PC technology. A custom Intel Pentium III processor powered the original Xbox, alongside an Nvidia GPU and 64MB of unified RAM. It was different from the fully custom architectures that Sony and Nintendo used at the time, allowing Microsoft to entice developers to its platform with tools that were more familiar to PC game makers.

Microsoft also included two hardware features that were unusual for a console at the time: a built-in hard drive and Ethernet port. The hard drive let game developers cache content without having to rely fully on optical discs, and Xbox players could also use it for game saves without having to purchase a separate memory card. You could even rip CDs to an Xbox and listen to custom soundtracks while playing games.

Xbox wasn’t the first to bring online gaming to consoles, but Microsoft took a different approach. Instead of relying on a built-in modem like Sega’s Dreamcast, Microsoft included an Ethernet port, anticipating a shift toward broadband internet connections. Xbox Live, which launched in November 2002, really nailed the entire online experience. There were friends lists, unique gamertags, voice chat through a bundled headset, and integrated multiplayer services. Microsoft created an entire platform for online gaming, making it easier for developers to support and fundamentally changing how people played games together.

The Xbox 360 took these ideas a step further, with swappable hard drives, digital game downloads, wireless controllers, and backward compatibility. It also turned Microsoft’s game console into an entertainment hub for the living room, complete with streaming video apps. Kinect was perhaps its most radical addition. Launched in 2010, the motion-sensing device used cameras and microphones to let players control games with their bodies and voices. It quickly became the fastest-selling consumer electronics device in early 2011.

But the success of Kinect and Microsoft’s ambition to turn Xbox into a home entertainment device helped lead to its first major strategic misstep with the Xbox One. Microsoft positioned the successor to the Xbox 360 around TV, entertainment, and an always-online future, instead of focusing on the games that had made Xbox successful. Its plans for online DRM and restrictions on used games sparked a huge backlash, while bundling Kinect pushed the console’s price $100 above Sony’s PlayStation 4. Six months after launch, Microsoft was forced to drop Kinect from the Xbox One console bundle to close that price gap.

The Xbox One showed what happens when Microsoft loses sight of what gamers want. The company spent most of that generation playing catch-up to Sony’s impressive PlayStation 4 sales. But its weakened position also forced Microsoft to once again try something radically different with Xbox Game Pass.

Introduced in 2017, Game Pass shook up the very economics of how people play games. The subscription service lets you access hundreds of games instead of buying them individually. Microsoft went on an acquisition spree to power its Game Pass ambitions, buying up smaller studios, Bethesda, and eventually Activision Blizzard.

These days, Microsoft is spinning many Xbox plates. The Xbox Series X and S consoles that launched in 2020 are way behind Sony’s PS5 sales numbers, and Xbox Cloud Gaming hasn’t yet been able to reach the millions more players that Microsoft has always wanted for its ecosystem.

Xbox Cloud Gaming promised to make games accessible on phones and tablets without needing a dedicated console, and combined with Microsoft’s messy project to bring Xbox games to PS5 and Nintendo consoles, the efforts have raised questions about what the Xbox brand actually represents these days. Microsoft hasn’t quite decided whether it wants to be a full third-party publisher, an ecosystem owner, or an unusual combination of both.

Some of Microsoft’s recent radically different Xbox bets haven’t paid off, so it’s now turning its attention to a much smaller Xbox division, with a restructuring that will see overall Xbox headcount reduced by 20 percent by the end of Microsoft’s fiscal year in June 2027. “I believe the future of gaming will look more like its beginning, when a small team with a big idea could change how the world plays,” Xbox CEO Asha Sharma wrote in a message to Xbox employees today.

Microsoft is celebrating 25 years of Xbox today with an event in Seattle for fans, where it’s launching Xbox Playback, a personalized look back at players’ 25-year Xbox history. There will be more fan-focused announcements throughout the day, but I’m not expecting any news on the next Xbox console. The celebration is awkwardly timed, because rumors have suggested that Microsoft is also preparing for more Xbox layoffs in November, during the same month it’s celebrating a milestone.

The reset also comes as Microsoft is preparing for a next-generation Xbox console, codenamed Project Helix, which will play both Xbox games and PC titles. It sounds like an effort to combine Windows and Xbox like never before. The next Xbox could also play every Xbox game ever made, thanks to Microsoft’s impressive bets on backward compatibility over the years.

It’s an interesting full-circle moment. The original Xbox was essentially a PC built for the living room. Project Helix appears to be taking a similar approach, but this time around bringing the flexibility of PC gaming into a console experience. If Microsoft gets it right, it could open Xbox up to a much bigger library of games, and entice people trying to decide between a console or a gaming PC.

The problem for Xbox is getting consumers to care about this next console, especially when its games are available on PC and sometimes on Sony’s and Nintendo’s platforms. “Xbox has the best console ecosystem around, packed with consumer-friendly features like free cloud saves, Game Pass, and my preferred controller,” says Jon Clarke, cofounder and editor-in-chief of DayOne. “None of these things are particularly sexy or flashy. They’re simply features that work, and that can make them difficult to get customers excited about.”

Rebuilding the Xbox brand and launching a new console gives Microsoft an unusual opportunity to make another bold bet on the future, just like it did 25 years ago. But the risks this time around are greater, after more than a decade of trying and failing to catch up to the competition.

“Up against competitors that have firmly established their place in the market, Xbox finds itself in a difficult position,” says Clarke. “Rebuilding the brand will be an expensive endeavor, and the bold ambition of combining the best of console gaming with the openness and flexibility of PC is easier said than done.”

Xbox also needs good games, and after years of studio acquisitions it now has plenty of IP to play with. “Many of those franchises have lost their luster owing to years of mismanagement and potentially a generalized lack of vision,” says Jez Corden, executive editor at Windows Central. “Microsoft from the top will need to be willing to invest here to ensure Xbox’s franchises can not only find new audiences, but simply maintain what they already have.”

Xbox also faces the challenge of RAMageddon’s impact on the hardware industry right now, pushing the prices of components beyond levels that consumers can comfortably afford. “If Xbox is to reclaim any of its former glory, innovating on the costs of the traditional video game ‘console’ will be absolutely paramount, otherwise it’s hard to imagine that it will continue to exist in its current form,” says Corden.

A console projected to cost between $1,200 and $1,800 won’t grow the Xbox ecosystem. It could make more sense to let PC makers build their own Xbox hardware, opening up the platform to a wider range of prices and devices rather than relying on expensive flagship consoles.

Twenty-five years ago, Microsoft built a PC and called it an Xbox. Now it’s preparing to build something that looks increasingly like a PC again, only this time it needs to convince people that it should still be called an Xbox. Microsoft has another opportunity to shake up the console industry, but its biggest challenge might be convincing players that Xbox needs to exist as a console at all.

I’m always keen to hear from readers, so please drop a comment here, or you can reach me at notepad@theverge.com if you want to discuss anything else. If you’ve heard about any of Microsoft’s secret projects, you can reach me via email at notepad@theverge.com or speak to me confidentially on the Signal messaging app, where I’m tomwarren.01. I’m also tomwarren on Telegram, if you’d prefer to chat there.

#10783. Hacker gets Doom running on a USB flash drive's built-in LCD, played by copying files
